MySQL Foundations · Answer Key

Part 1: SQL Foundations

Sessions 1 to 7. Modules 1 to 4: install MySQL, build and change tables, query, summarize and join. Runs on its own as a short course and ends with the Part 1 assessment.

Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 1: What Databases Are and Installing MySQL

Answers and reasoning for every Session 1 item: the diagnostic, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 1 12 self-check items Confidential

Start Here · Diagnostic

Not graded. Each reason also says where the skill comes back in this course. The sheet is the one on the workbook's Start Here page; MySQL's own answers to D1 to D5 are in the diagnostic lab's output below.

D1
You filter the city column to show only Chicago. How many rows are left?
A) 1
B) 2
C) 3
D) 6
Why: Orders 1002 and 1003. A filter keeps the rows that match a condition; in SQL that is WHERE city = 'Chicago'. Skill: filtering rows. Where it comes back: WHERE in Lesson 3.1 (and the lesson's first SELECT).
D2
You sort by price, largest first. Which item is on the top row?
A) Pride and Prejudice
B) The Hobbit
C) Dune
D) 1984
Why: 14.99 is the highest price (Dune appears twice with it). Sorting in SQL is ORDER BY price DESC. Skill: sorting. Where it comes back: ORDER BY in Lesson 3.2.
D3
How many rows have the city New York AND a qty of 2 or more?
A) 1
B) 2
C) 4
D) 5
Why: Only 1005 (Frank Miller) meets both conditions. With OR the answer would be 4 rows (1001, 1002, 1004, 1005): the most common slip, and the reason for the lab's TODO 3. Skill: AND versus OR. Where it comes back: WHERE with AND/OR in Lesson 3.1.
D4
You add a column total = qty × price. What is the total for order 1004?
A) 14.99
B) 17.99
C) 29.98
D) 44.97
Why: 3 × 14.99 = 44.97. In SQL a formula column is an expression in the SELECT list: qty * price AS total. Skill: a formula column. Where it comes back: expressions in SELECT (Lesson 1.1's taste, then 3.3).
D5
=COUNTA(city) counts the filled-in cells of the city column. What does it give?
A) 4
B) 5
C) 6
D) 0
Why: Six rows, but Eve's city is empty, so 5. SQL has the same idea: COUNT(city) skips empty (NULL) values, COUNT(*) counts rows. Skill: empty cells. Where it comes back: NULL in Lessons 2.2 and 3.3.
D6
You saved bookstore.sql in your Downloads folder. Your terminal is open in your home folder. Which path names the file?
A) bookstore.sql
B) Downloads/bookstore.sql
C) /bookstore.sql
D) home/Downloads.sql
Why: A relative path starts from the folder the terminal is in: from home, the file is inside Downloads. (Windows writes Downloads\bookstore.sql.) From Lesson 1.3 on you run .sql files this way: SOURCE Downloads/bookstore.sql. Skill: files and folders. Where it comes back: the Setup Guide and Lesson 1.3 (running .sql files).

1.1 · What Is a Database?: self-check

Q1
Which sentence describes MySQL correctly?
A) MySQL is a database: the file that holds your tables
B) MySQL is a database management system: server software that stores databases, enforces their rules and answers SQL
C) MySQL is a query language, a dialect of SQL
D) MySQL is a spreadsheet program that understands SQL
Why: A database is the organized data (for example the course's bookstore); the DBMS is the program that guards it and answers queries. MySQL, MariaDB and PostgreSQL are DBMSs.
A is wrong: The database is your data; MySQL is the software around it, and one MySQL server holds many databases.
C is wrong: SQL is the language; MySQL is a product that understands it (with its own small dialect).
D is wrong: A DBMS enforces types, keys and permissions that a spreadsheet does not.
Q2
A books table has the columns id, title, author_id, price. Which column is most likely a foreign key?
A) id
B) title
C) author_id
D) price
Why: author_id holds another table's primary key (authors.id) to say which author wrote the book: that is what a foreign key is.
A is wrong: id is this table's own primary key.
B is wrong: A title is ordinary data; two books can even share one.
D is wrong: A price is ordinary data, not a pointer to another row.
Q3
What does the last statement print?
CREATE TABLE pets (id INT PRIMARY KEY, name VARCHAR(20), kind VARCHAR(10));
INSERT INTO pets VALUES (1, 'Rex', 'dog'), (2, 'Tom', 'cat'), (3, 'Fido', 'dog');
UPDATE pets SET kind = 'cat' WHERE id = 3;
SELECT name FROM pets WHERE kind = 'cat' ORDER BY id;
Two rows: Tom and Fido
Why: The UPDATE changes only the row whose id is 3 (Fido) from dog to cat, so two cats come back, in id order. A WHERE clause picks the rows an UPDATE touches, exactly as it picks the rows a SELECT shows.
Q4
The pets table from Q3 is still there. What happens when this runs?
-- pets now holds ids 1, 2 and 3
INSERT INTO pets VALUES (2, 'Kit', 'cat');
A) ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry '2' for key 'pets.PRIMARY'
B) A new row is added: Kit gets id 4 automatically
C) ERROR 1366 (HY000): Incorrect integer value
D) Tom's row is replaced by Kit's
Why: id is the primary key and 2 already belongs to Tom. A primary key must be unique, so MySQL refuses the whole row (the lesson's Common Mistake 1). Leaving the id to AUTO_INCREMENT avoids it.
B is wrong: The id was given explicitly (and this table has no AUTO_INCREMENT): MySQL does not change your values.
C is wrong: 2 is a perfectly good integer; 1366 is for text in a number column.
D is wrong: An INSERT never overwrites an existing row.
Q5
A music app must remember, for millions of logged-in users, which song each is playing right now, and look it up in under a millisecond. Which kind of database fits best?
A) A relational database with a table per song
B) A graph database
C) A document store
D) A key-value store such as Redis
Why: One value (the current song) looked up by one key (the session or user id), very fast, usually in memory: the job key-value stores are built for. The app's accounts and payments would still live in a relational database.
A is wrong: It would work, but the data is a single key and value, and speed is the whole requirement.
B is wrong: A graph database answers questions about chains of connections.
C is wrong: Documents suit records whose fields vary; here every record is one key and one value.
Q6
Carol closes her account. Which statement removes her row but keeps the table and every other customer?
A) DROP TABLE customers;
B) DELETE FROM customers WHERE id = 3;
C) UPDATE customers SET email = NULL WHERE id = 3;
D) DELETE FROM customers;
Why: DELETE removes rows (the D in CRUD); its WHERE clause picks which ones.
A is wrong: DROP TABLE removes the table itself, structure and all rows.
C is wrong: UPDATE changes values in a row that stays (and here it would break the NOT NULL rule on email).
D is wrong: Without WHERE, DELETE removes every customer.

1.1 · What Is a Database?: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE TABLE notes (id INT PRIMARY KEY, title VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL);
INSERT INTO notes VALUES (1, 'Buy milk'), (2, 'Call Bob'), (3, 'Pay rent');
DELETE FROM notes WHERE id = 2;
SELECT * FROM notes;
+----+----------+
| id | title    |
+----+----------+
|  1 | Buy milk |
|  3 | Pay rent |
+----+----------+
Why: DELETE removes only the row whose id is 2; the other two stay with their ids. Ids are never renumbered after a delete.
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE TABLE members (id INT PRIMARY KEY, email VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL UNIQUE);
INSERT INTO members VALUES (1, 'ana@example.com');
INSERT INTO members VALUES (2, 'ana@example.com');
ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry 'ana@example.com' for key 'members.email'
Why: The first INSERT works. The second repeats an email in a UNIQUE column, so MySQL refuses it with 1062 and names the key (members.email). The table keeps one row.

1.2 · Installing MySQL: self-check

Q7
mysql --version in a terminal prints 9.7.2. What does that prove?
A) The MySQL server is running
B) Your password is correct
C) The mysql client program is installed and on your PATH
D) Your server is MySQL 9.7, not MariaDB
Why: mysql --version describes the client on your computer and works with no server running at all. Only SELECT VERSION();, sent after logging in, describes the server (the lesson's Exercise 1).
A is wrong: No connection is made; a stopped server gives the same answer.
B is wrong: No password is asked for.
D is wrong: A 9.7 client can talk to an 8.4 or a MariaDB server; ask the server.
Q8
mysql -u root -p answers ERROR 1045 (28000): Access denied for user .... What do you fix first?
A) The user name or the password
B) Start the server
C) Add mysql to your PATH
D) Reinstall MySQL with the native password plugin
Why: 1045 means the server is running and answered, but refused the credentials. Retype the password (it is case-sensitive); remember -p; on Ubuntu's own package root may only work through sudo mysql.
B is wrong: A stopped server cannot refuse you: it gives 2002 or 2003.
C is wrong: Without the client on the PATH you would get "command not found", not a MySQL error.
D is wrong: That plugin is removed in 9.x (error 1524), and the problem is the credentials.
Q9
On the course's MySQL 9.7 server, what does this print?
SELECT VERSION() LIKE '9.7.%' AS on_97;
1 (in a column named on_97)
Why: LIKE compares the version text with a pattern (% means anything), and a comparison gives 1 for true and 0 for false. VERSION() is 9.7.2 here, so the answer is 1; on 8.4 or MariaDB it would be 0.
Q10
You use Homebrew on a Mac. Which command installs the version this course uses?
A) brew install mysql
B) brew install mariadb
C) brew install mysql-client
D) brew install mysql@9.7
Why: The versioned formula mysql@9.7 stays on the 9.7 LTS series (it is keg-only: add its bin folder to the PATH).
A is wrong: The plain formula follows the newest innovation release (calendar numbers such as 26.x).
B is wrong: That is MariaDB, a different product.
C is wrong: That installs only the client, with no server.
Q11
Your MySQL container was started with -p 3307:3306. Which command connects to it from a mysql client installed on your laptop?
A) mysql -u root -p
B) mysql -h localhost -P 3306 -u root -p
C) mysql -h 127.0.0.1 -P 3307 -u root -p
D) mysql -h 127.0.0.1 -P 3306 -u root -p
Why: The container's port 3306 is published on the laptop's port 3307, and 127.0.0.1 forces a network connection (localhost would make the client look for a local socket file and ignore the port).
A is wrong: That looks for a local server on the default socket: error 2002.
B is wrong: localhost ignores -P on macOS and Linux, and 3306 is the wrong laptop port anyway.
D is wrong: Right host, wrong port: 3306 on the laptop is not where the container listens.
Q12
On a fresh MySQL 9.7 install (not Docker), what does this print?
SELECT plugin
FROM mysql.user
WHERE user = 'root' AND host = 'localhost';
caching_sha2_password
Why: caching_sha2_password is the default login method for every new account in 8.4 and 9.x. mysql_native_password no longer exists in 9.x, so you should never see it there.

1.2 · Installing MySQL: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT CURRENT_USER();
+----------------+
| CURRENT_USER() |
+----------------+
| root@localhost |
+----------------+
Why: CURRENT_USER() is the account MySQL matched: root, connected from this computer. Through Docker's published port it would be root@%.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT VERSION() LIKE '%MariaDB%' AS on_mariadb,
       @@character_set_server   AS charset;
+------------+---------+
| on_mariadb | charset |
+------------+---------+
|          0 | utf8mb4 |
+------------+---------+
Why: 0 because the server is MySQL, not MariaDB; utf8mb4 is the default character set since 8.0.

Lab Reference Solutions

Environment check: labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/check_environment.sql (+ .sh / .ps1)

A tool, not an exercise. On a ready MySQL 9.7 server in Session 1 it prints (the .sh and .ps1 helpers first print a client line with mysql --version and an OK or CHECK line for the client):

+----------------+------------------------------+
| server_version | edition                      |
+----------------+------------------------------+
| 9.7.2          | MySQL Community Server - GPL |
+----------------+------------------------------+
+---------------------------------------------------+
| server_check                                      |
+---------------------------------------------------+
| OK     MySQL 9.7 LTS, the version the course uses |
+---------------------------------------------------+
+-------------------------------+--------------------------------+
| charset_check                 | sql_mode_check                 |
+-------------------------------+--------------------------------+
| OK     utf8mb4 (full Unicode) | OK     default strict SQL mode |
+-------------------------------+--------------------------------+
+----------------+-----------------------+
| logged_in_as   | login_plugin          |
+----------------+-----------------------+
| root@localhost | caching_sha2_password |
+----------------+-----------------------+
+----------------------------------------------------------------+
| write_check                                                    |
+----------------------------------------------------------------+
| OK     created a database and a table, and read back: it works |
+----------------------------------------------------------------+
+---------------------------------------------------------+
| bookstore_check                                         |
+---------------------------------------------------------+
| INFO   no bookstore database yet: Lesson 2.1 creates it |
+---------------------------------------------------------+
+-----------------------------+
| verdict                     |
+-----------------------------+
| READY for MySQL Foundations |
+-----------------------------+

On MySQL 8.4 the server line says CHECK and the verdict READY WITH NOTES. On MariaDB (measured on 12.3): the server line, the SQL mode line (no ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY) and the login plugin (mysql_native_password, normal there) differ, and the verdict is READY WITH NOTES. A client that cannot connect never reaches the tables: the helpers print the ERROR line and the triage hint (1045 credentials, 2002/2003 server or address).

Diagnostic practice: labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/diagnostic_starter.sql → diagnostic_solution.sql

The six Start Here questions, D1 to D5 done in SQL (D6 is about paths). The solution prints:

Database changed
+----------+---------------+----------+---------------------+-----+-------+
| order_no | customer      | city     | item                | qty | price |
+----------+---------------+----------+---------------------+-----+-------+
|     1001 | Alice Johnson | New York | Dune                |   1 | 14.99 |
|     1002 | Bob Smith     | Chicago  | 1984                |   2 |  9.99 |
|     1003 | Grace Wilson  | Chicago  | The Hobbit          |   1 | 10.99 |
|     1004 | Dave Brown    | Houston  | Dune                |   3 | 14.99 |
|     1005 | Frank Miller  | New York | Fahrenheit 451      |   2 | 10.49 |
|     1006 | Eve Davis     | NULL     | Pride and Prejudice |   1 |  7.99 |
+----------+---------------+----------+---------------------+-----+-------+
+----------+--------------+---------+
| order_no | customer     | city    |
+----------+--------------+---------+
|     1002 | Bob Smith    | Chicago |
|     1003 | Grace Wilson | Chicago |
+----------+--------------+---------+
+------+-------+
| item | price |
+------+-------+
| Dune | 14.99 |
+------+-------+
+----------+--------------+----------+-----+
| order_no | customer     | city     | qty |
+----------+--------------+----------+-----+
|     1005 | Frank Miller | New York |   2 |
+----------+--------------+----------+-----+
+----------+-----+-------+-------+
| order_no | qty | price | total |
+----------+-----+-------+-------+
|     1004 |   3 | 14.99 | 44.97 |
+----------+-----+-------+-------+
+----------+------------------+
| all_rows | cities_filled_in |
+----------+------------------+
|        6 |                5 |
+----------+------------------+

Common sticking points: deleting the quotes with the placeholder in D1 (ERROR 1064); putting DESC before price in D2; TODO 3 with OR left in place (four rows: exactly the diagnostic's most common wrong answer).

Lab 1.1-A: labs/module-01/l1_1-what-is-a-database/order_sheet_starter.sql → order_sheet_solution.sql

The solution prints:

Database changed
+----------+---------------+-------------------+
| order_no | customer_name | customer_email    |
+----------+---------------+-------------------+
|        1 | Alice Johnson | alice@example.com |
|        2 | Alice Jonson  | alice@example.com |
|        6 | alice johnson | alice@example.com |
+----------+---------------+-------------------+
+----------+---------------+---------------+
| order_no | customer_name | customer_city |
+----------+---------------+---------------+
|        3 | Bob Smith     | Chicago       |
|        4 | Bob Smith     | Boston        |
+----------+---------------+---------------+
+----------+------------------+
| order_no | books            |
+----------+------------------+
|        1 | Dune, 1984, 1984 |
|        3 | Dune             |
|        4 | Dune Messiah     |
|        5 | The Hobbit, Dune |
+----------+------------------+
+----------+----------+
| order_no | total    |
+----------+----------+
|        5 | about 26 |
+----------+----------+
+----------+-------------+-------+
| order_no | customer_id | total |
+----------+-------------+-------+
|        1 |           1 | 34.97 |
|        2 |           1 |  7.99 |
|        6 |           1 |  9.49 |
+----------+-------------+-------+
+----+---------------+---------------------+
| id | name          | email               |
+----+---------------+---------------------+
|  1 | Alice Johnson | alice.j@example.com |
|  2 | Bob Smith     | bob@example.com     |
|  3 | Grace Wilson  | grace@example.com   |
+----+---------------+---------------------+

What each TODO teaches: (1) the same customer typed three ways (two by a typo, one by capitals), because the name is copied into every order; (2) two cities for one customer: one copy was updated, the other was not; (3) a list in a cell forces a text search, and LIKE '%Dune%' also matches Dune Messiah: a silent wrong answer, the most important of the four; (4) a text column accepts about 26, so the total cannot be trusted or added up. Step 5 rebuilds it as customers + orders: Alice exists once, her three orders point at id 1, and one UPDATE changes her email everywhere. The relational design still lacks an order_items table for the books: that is the 1.1 project's Step 4.

Lab 1.1-B: labs/module-01/l1_1-what-is-a-database/crud_tour_starter.sql → crud_tour_solution.sql

The solution prints:

Database changed
+----+------------------+-------+
| id | title            | price |
+----+------------------+-------+
|  2 | The Great Gatsby |  8.99 |
+----+------------------+-------+
+----+------------------+-------+----------+
| id | title            | price | in_stock |
+----+------------------+-------+----------+
|  2 | The Great Gatsby |  8.99 |        0 |
|  3 | The Hobbit       |  9.99 |        1 |
|  4 | Fahrenheit 451   | 10.49 |        1 |
+----+------------------+-------+----------+

Reading it: under $10 only The Great Gatsby qualifies before the sale; Fahrenheit 451 gets id 4 because ids keep counting; The Hobbit drops to 9.99; in_stock prints 0 and 1 (a BOOLEAN is a tiny integer in MySQL); Dune (id 1) is gone and id 1 is not reused. Ask what a DELETE without its WHERE line would have done: every book gone, no undo.

Handout: labs/module-01/l1_1-what-is-a-database/debug_rules.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints (the four bugs, then the table with the three rows that got in):

Database changed
ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry '2' for key 'books.PRIMARY'
ERROR 1364 (HY000): Field 'title' doesn't have a default value
ERROR 1366 (HY000): Incorrect decimal value: 'cheap' for column 'price' at row 1
ERROR 1406 (22001): Data too long for column 'isbn' at row 1
+----+---------------------+---------------+-------+
| id | title               | isbn          | price |
+----+---------------------+---------------+-------+
|  1 | Dune                | 9780441013593 | 14.99 |
|  2 | 1984                | 9780451524935 |  9.99 |
|  6 | Pride and Prejudice | 9780141439518 |  7.99 |
+----+---------------------+---------------+-------+
  1. Bug A, 1062: id 2 already belongs to 1984. Fix: another id (3) or a table with AUTO_INCREMENT and no id in the INSERT.
  2. Bug B, 1364: title is NOT NULL and the INSERT leaves it out. Fix: add the title ('The Hobbit').
  3. Bug C, 1366: 'cheap' is not a decimal number. Fix: 9.49.
  4. Bug D, 1406: the ISBN with hyphens has 17 characters; the column holds 13. Fix: '9781451673319' (or a wider column).

Fixed, the final SELECT shows six books and no ERROR lines. On MariaDB 1062 names the key 'PRIMARY' and 1366 the full column kit_s1_rules.books.price.

Lab 1.2-A: labs/module-01/l1_2-installing-mysql/server_facts_starter.sql → server_facts_solution.sql

The solution prints (on the course's MySQL 9.7 test server, Linux):

+---------+------------------------------+
| version | edition                      |
+---------+------------------------------+
| 9.7.2   | MySQL Community Server - GPL |
+---------+------------------------------+
+-------------+------------+
| is_mysql_97 | is_mariadb |
+-------------+------------+
|           1 |          0 |
+-------------+------------+
+---------+--------------------+
| charset | collation          |
+---------+--------------------+
| utf8mb4 | utf8mb4_0900_ai_ci |
+---------+--------------------+
+--------+------------------+
| engine | lower_case_names |
+--------+------------------+
| InnoDB |                0 |
+--------+------------------+
+-----------------+
| max_connections |
+-----------------+
|             151 |
+-----------------+
+--------------------+
| SCHEMA_NAME        |
+--------------------+
| information_schema |
| mysql              |
| performance_schema |
| sys                |
+--------------------+

What may differ on a learner's server: the patch version (any 9.7.x); lower_case_names 1 on Windows and 2 on macOS (names are compared without case there, with case on Linux: a reason to always type table names exactly); max_connections if someone changed it. On 8.4 is_mysql_97 is 0; on MariaDB is_mariadb is 1, the edition is mariadb.org binary distribution and the collation utf8mb4_uca1400_ai_ci. The column header SCHEMA_NAME comes back in capitals: information_schema's own names, not a mistake.

Lab 1.2-B: labs/module-01/l1_2-installing-mysql/accounts_check_starter.sql → accounts_check_solution.sql

The solution prints:

+----------------+-----------------+
| you_typed      | matched_account |
+----------------+-----------------+
| root@localhost | root@localhost  |
+----------------+-----------------+
+------------------+-----------+-----------------------+----------------+
| user             | host      | plugin                | account_locked |
+------------------+-----------+-----------------------+----------------+
| mysql.infoschema | localhost | caching_sha2_password | Y              |
| mysql.session    | localhost | caching_sha2_password | Y              |
| mysql.sys        | localhost | caching_sha2_password | Y              |
| root             | localhost | caching_sha2_password | N              |
+------------------+-----------+-----------------------+----------------+
+------------------+----------------+
| user             | account_locked |
+------------------+----------------+
| mysql.infoschema | Y              |
| mysql.session    | Y              |
| mysql.sys        | Y              |
+------------------+----------------+
Empty set

Reading it: USER() is the name the client sent, CURRENT_USER() the account that matched (they differ with wildcard hosts, Lesson 7.4). The three mysql.* accounts are the server's own and locked (Y): nobody can log in as them. The empty last result is the good news: no account uses the removed login method. Docker adds root with host %. On MariaDB mysql.user has no account_locked column (ERROR 1054): drop that column there.

Handout: labs/module-01/l1_2-installing-mysql/debug_old_advice.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints:

ERROR 1524 (HY000): Plugin 'mysql_native_password' is not loaded
ERROR 1193 (HY000): Unknown system variable 'default_authentication_plugin'
Empty set
  1. 1524: the mysql_native_password plugin is not loaded: removed in 9.x, disabled by default in 8.4. No account is created (the check query is empty). Modern replacement: leave out IDENTIFIED WITH ... (the default caching_sha2_password) and update the client or library.
  2. 1193: default_authentication_plugin no longer exists. Modern replacement: SELECT @@authentication_policy;.

On MariaDB the first statement fails with 1064 (MariaDB writes IDENTIFIED VIA); with corrected MariaDB syntax it would CREATE the account, so never "fix" it in class.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 1.1 · What Is a Database?
  1. B The database is the data; the DBMS is the software that manages it: The database is the organized data itself. The DBMS, such as MySQL, is the software that stores it, enforces its rules and answers queries about it.
  2. C A foreign key: A foreign key holds another table's primary key to record which row this one belongs to. Many orders can point at the same customer.
  3. A SELECT: R is Read, and reading rows is what SELECT does. INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE cover Create, Update and Delete.
  4. C id 1 is already used, and a primary key must be unique: id is the primary key and 1 already belongs to Alice. Primary key values must be unique, so MySQL refuses the duplicate entry.
  5. B An order_items table with one row per book per order, holding order_id, book_id and quantity: An order_items table holds one row per book in an order, with order_id and book_id foreign keys and a quantity. Each fact sits in its own row and can be counted and joined.
Lesson 1.2 · Installing MySQL
  1. C docker run ... mysql:9.7: The 9.7 tag pins the LTS series. latest, plain brew install mysql and distribution packages can each give you a different version.
  2. A The server is not running: 2002 and 2003 mean nothing answered. A stopped server is the usual reason; start it and try again.
  3. B It fails with error 1524, because the plugin is not loaded: mysql_native_password is removed in 9.x, so the statement fails with error 1524. Current phpMyAdmin and PHP support the default caching_sha2_password.
  4. A mysql -h 127.0.0.1 -P 3306 -u root -p: 127.0.0.1 forces a TCP connection to the published port. The name localhost makes the client look for a local socket file, which the container does not provide.
  5. C It is MariaDB: most SQL works, but some results and features differ from MySQL 9.7: XAMPP bundles MariaDB, a MySQL fork with its own version numbers. Basic SQL runs, but some features, messages and result orders differ from MySQL 9.7.

Opening: Reading the Room

There is no retrieval warm-up in Session 1. The opening's two checks have their answers above: the environment check output (what READY looks like) and the diagnostic D1 to D6. Learners who end the clinic still NOT READY leave with a numbered plan in their workbook's Start Here box.

Exit Ticket

  1. Server, client, database: the server (mysqld) stores the data, enforces the rules and answers SQL; a client (mysql, phpMyAdmin, Workbench) is what you type into and it sends your SQL to the server; a database is a named set of tables inside the server (preview_shop today, bookstore from Session 2).
  2. 2002 vs 1045: 2002 means nothing answered: check that the server is running (start command for your platform) and that you are asking the right place (Docker: -h 127.0.0.1 -P <port>). 1045 means the server answered and refused: check the user name and password (and -p).
  3. Keys: id is the primary key, customer_id the foreign key (it holds a customers.id). Storing the name in every order copies one fact many times; the copies drift apart (the order-sheet lab's three Alices).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A ("MySQL is a database"). The everyday usage. Draw the three boxes again: one server, many databases inside.
  • Q4 B ("Kit gets id 4"). Expects MySQL to repair the input. A database refuses rather than guesses; that is the point of rules.
  • Q6 A or D (DROP TABLE / DELETE without WHERE). Mixes up removing rows with removing the table, or forgets that WHERE picks the rows. Both are destructive with no undo: worth a minute in the Session 2 warm-up.
  • Q7 D ("the client version proves the server version"). The most common misconception of the day. Exercise 1 of Lesson 1.2 is built on it.
  • Q8 B ("start the server" for 1045). Has not separated "no answer" from "answered and refused". Repeat the triage line.
  • Q11 B or D. Either localhost (socket, ignores the port) or the container's internal port. Draw the two ports: the laptop's 3307 leads to the container's 3306.
  • Exit ticket 1 with "client = phpMyAdmin only". Fine, but ask what they type into in the terminal: the mysql command is a client too.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 2: The MySQL Client and Your First Tables

Answers and reasoning for every Session 2 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 2 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-Up: Session 1

Not graded. Each reason says where the idea came from in Session 1.

W1
Is MySQL a database?
A) Yes: MySQL is the file that holds your tables
B) No: MySQL is the server (a DBMS); a database such as bookstore lives inside it
C) No: MySQL is a query language
D) Yes: every MySQL install is one database
Why: MySQL is the server software; one server holds many databases. Session 1's exit ticket question 1.
W2
Which error tells you the server is running and answered?
A) ERROR 2002
B) ERROR 2003
C) ERROR 1045 (28000): Access denied
D) None of them
Why: 1045 means the server answered and refused the credentials; 2002 and 2003 mean nothing answered.
W3
What does the SELECT print?
CREATE TABLE pets (id INT PRIMARY KEY, name VARCHAR(20));
INSERT INTO pets VALUES (1, 'Rex'), (2, 'Tom'), (3, 'Fido');
DELETE FROM pets WHERE id = 2;
SELECT * FROM pets;
A) Rows 1 Rex and 3 Fido
B) Rows 1 Rex and 2 Fido: the ids are renumbered
C) All three rows
D) Empty set
Why: DELETE removes only the row whose id is 2; ids are never renumbered. MySQL prints:
+----+------+
| id | name |
+----+------+
|  1 | Rex  |
|  3 | Fido |
+----+------+
W4
Which statement removes Carol's row (id 3) and keeps every other customer?
A) DROP TABLE customers;
B) DELETE FROM customers;
C) DELETE FROM customers WHERE id = 3;
D) UPDATE customers SET id = NULL WHERE id = 3;
Why: DELETE with a WHERE removes the rows the WHERE picks. Without WHERE every row goes; DROP TABLE removes the table.
W5
mysql --version prints 9.7.2. What does that prove about your server?
A) That it is 9.7.2
B) That it is running
C) That it is MySQL, not MariaDB
D) Nothing: it describes the client; ask the server with SELECT VERSION();
Why: The client and the server are different programs. Only a query sent after logging in describes the server.

1.3 · MySQL CLI & phpMyAdmin: self-check

Q1
Which of these does nothing in phpMyAdmin's SQL tab, because only the mysql command-line client understands it?
A) SHOW DATABASES;
B) DESCRIBE user;
C) SOURCE ~/mysql-course/hello.sql
D) SELECT DATABASE();
Why: SOURCE (like \G, \c, help and exit) is a client command: the mysql program handles it itself and never sends it to the server. phpMyAdmin has its own Import tab for files.
A is wrong: SHOW DATABASES is SQL (a MySQL addition to the standard): every client can send it.
B is wrong: DESCRIBE is SQL too; phpMyAdmin also shows it as the Structure tab.
D is wrong: A SELECT is plain SQL and works in any client.
Q2
You press Enter and the prompt changes from mysql> to '>. What does it mean?
A) The statement only needs a semicolon
B) A single quote is still open: close it, then type ;
C) The server is still running the query
D) You were logged out
Why: The client shows '> while a text value in single quotes is unfinished. Close the quote, then end the statement with a semicolon (or \c after closing the quote to throw it away).
A is wrong: That prompt is ->; inside an open quote a semicolon is just part of the text.
C is wrong: Nothing has been sent yet: the statement is not finished.
D is wrong: Logging out prints Bye and returns you to the shell.
Q3
On MySQL 9.7, how many rows does this print?
SHOW TABLES FROM mysql LIKE 'time_zone%';
5 (time_zone, time_zone_leap_second, time_zone_name, time_zone_transition, time_zone_transition_type)
Why: SHOW TABLES FROM lists another database's tables without switching to it, and LIKE filters the names: % means any characters. The mysql database keeps five time-zone tables.
Q4
You type mysql -u root -p secret123 (with a space after -p). What happens?
A) It logs in with the password secret123
B) ERROR 1045 (28000): Access denied
C) It logs in without asking for a password
D) It asks for the password, then fails with ERROR 1049 (42000): Unknown database 'secret123'
Why: -p on its own means "ask me"; the word after the space is read as the database to start in. Type the password at the prompt instead (the lesson's warning box).
A is wrong: Only -psecret123 (no space) would pass it, and that leaves it in your shell history.
B is wrong: The password typed at the prompt can be right; the failure is the unknown database.
C is wrong: -p always makes the client ask.
Q5
report.sql ends its query with \G. Which way of running it stops with Unknown command on MySQL 9.7?
A) mysql -u root -p < report.sql
B) SOURCE report.sql at the mysql> prompt
C) mysql --commands -u root -p < report.sql
D) Typing the query at the prompt
Why: Read from a redirected file, the 9.x client is in batch mode and ignores its own commands unless started with --commands. At the prompt, typed or SOURCEd, client commands always work.
B is wrong: SOURCE runs the file as if you typed it: client commands work.
C is wrong: --commands switches client commands back on in batch mode.
D is wrong: Interactive use is not affected.
Q6
Which statement lists only the columns of mysql.user whose names contain lock?
A) SHOW TABLES FROM mysql LIKE '%lock%';
B) SHOW COLUMNS FROM mysql.user LIKE '%lock%';
C) DESCRIBE mysql.user WHERE lock;
D) SELECT lock FROM mysql.user;
Why: SHOW COLUMNS FROM table LIKE 'pattern' is DESCRIBE with a filter. On 9.7 it prints two columns: Lock_tables_priv and account_locked (LIKE ignores case).
A is wrong: That lists tables, not columns.
C is wrong: DESCRIBE takes no WHERE: a syntax error.
D is wrong: There is no column named lock: ERROR 1054.

1.3 · MySQL CLI & phpMyAdmin: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT DATABASE() AS before_use;
USE mysql;
SELECT DATABASE() AS after_use;
+------------+
| before_use |
+------------+
| NULL       |
+------------+
Database changed
+-----------+
| after_use |
+-----------+
| mysql     |
+-----------+
Why: Before the first USE a session has no current database, so DATABASE() is NULL. USE mysql; prints Database changed, and from then on it is mysql.
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
SHOW COLUMNS FROM mysql.user LIKE 'account_locked';
+----------------+---------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| Field          | Type          | Null | Key | Default | Extra |
+----------------+---------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| account_locked | enum('N','Y') | NO   |     | N       |       |
+----------------+---------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
Why: account_locked is an ENUM of two letters, required, N by default: new accounts are unlocked unless you lock them.

2.1 · Creating Databases & Tables: self-check

Q7
What happens when this runs?
CREATE DATABASE kit_q7 CHARACTER SET utf8;
SHOW WARNINGS;
A) ERROR 1064: utf8 is not a character set
B) The database stores full Unicode, emoji included
C) Nothing: CHARACTER SET is ignored
D) The database is created with utf8mb3 (no emoji), and warning 3719 says to use utf8mb4
Why: MySQL's utf8 is an alias for the three-byte utf8mb3, which cannot store emoji and some rarer characters. The statement works and warns; always write utf8mb4.
A is wrong: utf8 is accepted, with a warning.
B is wrong: Only utf8mb4 stores every character.
C is wrong: It is used: the database gets utf8mb3.
Q8
Which column does the last statement list first?
CREATE TABLE members (
    id   INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    name VARCHAR(40) NOT NULL
);
ALTER TABLE members ADD COLUMN email VARCHAR(100) FIRST;
SHOW COLUMNS FROM members;
email (then id and name)
Why: ADD COLUMN puts a new column at the end unless you say AFTER column or FIRST. FIRST makes email the first column; existing rows would get NULL in it.
Q9
A script stops with ERROR 1046 (3D000): No database selected. What does it mean?
A) There is no current database: run USE first, or write database.table
B) The database name is misspelled
C) The table already exists
D) Your account may not create tables
Why: A fresh session, or one whose current database was just dropped, has no current database, so a bare table name has nowhere to go.
B is wrong: A misspelled database gives 1049 Unknown database.
C is wrong: That is 1050.
D is wrong: Permission errors are 1044 or 1142 (Lesson 7.4).
Q10
Which name follows the course's naming conventions for a table of book reviews?
A) BookReviews
B) tbl_review
C) book_reviews
D) bookReview
Why: Lowercase, snake_case, a plural noun. Lowercase also keeps the name working when the database moves to a Linux server, where table names are case-sensitive.
A is wrong: Capitals can break on Linux servers.
B is wrong: No tbl_ prefix, and tables are plural.
D is wrong: camelCase and singular.
Q11
In shelf, title was VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL. After the ALTER below, what is true?
CREATE TABLE shelf (
    id    INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    title VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL
);
ALTER TABLE shelf CHANGE COLUMN title book_title VARCHAR(250);
SHOW COLUMNS FROM shelf;
A) The column is now book_title and is still required
B) The column is now book_title, VARCHAR(250), and allows NULL
C) ERROR: CHANGE cannot rename a column
D) title is kept and a new column book_title is added
Why: CHANGE takes the old name, the new name and the FULL new definition. Whatever is left out, here NOT NULL, is gone: SHOW COLUMNS prints YES under Null. Repeat every constraint, then check.
A is wrong: Only a definition that says NOT NULL keeps it.
C is wrong: Renaming plus redefining is exactly what CHANGE is for.
D is wrong: CHANGE replaces the column; ADD COLUMN would add one.
Q12
Write the one statement that removes every row of the customers table, keeps the table and starts the ids at 1 again.
TRUNCATE TABLE customers;
Why: TRUNCATE empties the table and resets AUTO_INCREMENT. DELETE FROM customers; also removes every row but keeps counting ids; DROP TABLE removes the table itself.

2.1 · Creating Databases & Tables: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE TABLE tickets (
    id    INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    label VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO tickets (label) VALUES ('first'), ('second');
TRUNCATE TABLE tickets;
INSERT INTO tickets (label) VALUES ('third');
SELECT * FROM tickets;
+----+-------+
| id | label |
+----+-------+
|  1 | third |
+----+-------+
Why: TRUNCATE removed both rows AND reset the AUTO_INCREMENT counter, so the next row gets id 1. A DELETE FROM tickets would have given id 3.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE TABLE notes (
    id    INT PRIMARY KEY,
    body  VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL
);
ALTER TABLE notes MODIFY COLUMN body TEXT;
SHOW COLUMNS FROM notes LIKE 'body';
+-------+------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| Field | Type | Null | Key | Default | Extra |
+-------+------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| body  | text | YES  |     | NULL    |       |
+-------+------+------+-----+---------+-------+
Why: MODIFY replaced the whole definition with TEXT alone, so NOT NULL is gone: Null says YES. Write MODIFY COLUMN body TEXT NOT NULL to keep the rule.

Lab 1.3-A: labs/module-02/l1_3-mysql-cli-phpmyadmin/find_your_way_starter.sql → find_your_way_solution.sql

The solution prints:

Database changed
+-------------+
| current_db  |
+-------------+
| kit_s2_cafe |
+-------------+
+-----------------------+
| Tables_in_kit_s2_cafe |
+-----------------------+
| cafe_orders           |
| menu_items            |
| staff_notes           |
+-----------------------+
+----------+--------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
| Field    | Type         | Null | Key | Default | Extra          |
+----------+--------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
| id       | int          | NO   | PRI | NULL    | auto_increment |
| name     | varchar(60)  | NO   | UNI | NULL    |                |
| price    | decimal(5,2) | NO   |     | NULL    |                |
| vegan    | tinyint(1)   | YES  |     | 0       |                |
| added_on | date         | YES  |     | NULL    |                |
+----------+--------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
+------------+----------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| Field      | Type     | Null | Key | Default | Extra |
+------------+----------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| ordered_at | datetime | NO   |     | NULL    |       |
| paid_at    | datetime | YES  |     | NULL    |       |
+------------+----------+------+-----+---------+-------+
+------------------------------+
| Tables_in_mysql (time_zone%) |
+------------------------------+
| time_zone                    |
| time_zone_leap_second        |
| time_zone_name               |
| time_zone_transition         |
| time_zone_transition_type    |
+------------------------------+
*************************** 1. row ***************************
      id: 3
    name: Oat Latte
   price: 4.10
   vegan: 1
added_on: 2026-02-14
*************************** 1. row ***************************
       Table: staff_notes
Create Table: CREATE TABLE `staff_notes` (
  `id` int NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
  `note` varchar(200) COLLATE utf8mb4_unicode_ci NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (`id`)
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_ci

Reading it: menu_items has id as the primary key (PRI) and name as a unique column (UNI); name and price say NO under Null, so they are required; vegan is a BOOLEAN shown as tinyint(1) with default 0. Of the _at columns only paid_at may be empty. TODO 4 reaches the mysql database with FROM, without leaving the café. TODO 5's vertical row and TODO 6's definition use \G. Learners who wrote LIKE '%_at' get the same two rows here (the _ matches any one character, and the only candidates end in _at); '%\_at' is the exact form.

Reading it: 51 columns, 31 of them privilege flags (one per kind of permission, Lesson 7.4). Host and User are fixed-length CHAR; password_last_changed is the only one of the four that may be NULL. TABLE_ROWS is an estimate for InnoDB tables. On 8.4 the help-table estimates differ; on MariaDB mysql.user is a view with 47 columns (see the README).

Handout: labs/module-02/l1_3-mysql-cli-phpmyadmin/debug_lost.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints (the four errors, then the accounts):

ERROR 1049 (42000): Unknown database 'mysq'
Database changed
ERROR 1146 (42S02): Table 'mysql.users' doesn't exist
ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near 'current_date' at line 1
ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near 'SELECT CURRENT_USER() AS account' at line 2
+------------------+-----------+
| User             | Host      |
+------------------+-----------+
| mysql.infoschema | localhost |
| mysql.session    | localhost |
| mysql.sys        | localhost |
| root             | localhost |
+------------------+-----------+
  1. Bug A, 1049: mysq is a typo. Fix: USE mysql; (SHOW DATABASES; and copy the exact name).
  2. Bug B, 1146: the accounts table is user, singular; the message names the database it looked in (mysql.users). Fix: FROM user.
  3. Bug C, 1064: current_date is a built-in function (like current_time in the lesson). Fix: AS today or backticks.
  4. Bug D, 1064: no semicolon after the first SELECT, so MySQL reads both lines as one statement and gets lost at the second SELECT (near 'SELECT CURRENT_USER() ...' at line 2). Fix: end the first line with ;.

Fixed, the file prints version, account, a date and the four accounts with no ERROR lines. Docker adds root with host %.

Reading it: IF NOT EXISTS turned the second CREATE TABLE rooms into a Note (1050), and rooms kept its three columns. Both tables inherited utf8mb4_unicode_ci from the database.
