Handout: labs/module-02/l2_1-creating-databases-tables/debug_build_script.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints:

ERROR 1007 (HY000): Can't create database 'kit_s2_shelf'; database exists
Database changed
ERROR 1046 (3D000): No database selected
Database changed
ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near ')' at line 6
ERROR 1050 (42S01): Table 'shelves' already exists
ERROR 1146 (42S02): Table 'kit_s2_shelf.shelf' doesn't exist
  1. Bug A, 1007: the database already exists. Fix: CREATE DATABASE IF NOT EXISTS kit_s2_shelf; (or delete the line: the database was just created).
  2. Bug B, 1046: the session's current database (kit_s2_scratch) was dropped one line earlier, so there is no database selected. Fix: delete the stray CREATE TABLE (it belonged to the scratch database), or USE kit_s2_shelf; before it and drop the later duplicate.
  3. Bug C, 1064: a comma after the last column; MySQL gets lost at the closing ). Fix: remove the comma after capacity INT NOT NULL.
  4. Bug D, 1050: the table already exists, as it will whenever a script is rerun. Fix: CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS shelves, or a DROP TABLE IF EXISTS shelves; before it.
  5. Bug E, 1146: the table is shelves, plural (the message names kit_s2_shelf.shelf). Fix: DESCRIBE shelves;.

Fixed, the file prints DESCRIBE shelves with four columns (id, label UNI, genre, capacity) and no ERROR lines.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 1.3 · MySQL CLI & phpMyAdmin
  1. A The statement has no semicolon yet, so the client is waiting for more: The client sends a statement only when it sees the terminator. The arrow prompt means it is waiting for the rest; type a semicolon.
  2. B ERROR 1146: Table 'mysql.customers' doesn't exist: MySQL looks for the table in the current database, mysql, and reports the full name it tried: Table 'mysql.customers' doesn't exist.
  3. C \G at the end of a query: Backslash commands such as \G are handled by the mysql client itself and never reach the server, so other tools do not understand them.
  4. B ERROR 1064, because current_time is a reserved word: CURRENT_TIME is a reserved word (a built-in function), so it cannot be a plain alias. Rename it (now_time) or wrap it in backticks.
  5. A End the statements with ; instead of \G (or run the client with --commands): In batch mode the 9.x client ignores its own commands. Ending statements with semicolons makes the file work everywhere; --commands is the alternative.
Lesson 2.1 · Creating Databases & Tables
  1. C Nothing changes; MySQL reports a note and keeps the existing table: IF NOT EXISTS turns the error into a note (1050) and leaves the existing table and its rows exactly as they were. The new definition is ignored.
  2. A SHOW CREATE TABLE books;: SHOW CREATE TABLE prints the exact CREATE TABLE statement that would rebuild the table, with every constraint, the engine and the character set and collation.
  3. B title is VARCHAR(250) and now allows NULL: MODIFY replaces the whole column definition with exactly what you typed. NOT NULL was not repeated, so title now accepts NULL, without any warning.
  4. A A table with one row, id 1: TRUNCATE removes every row and resets the AUTO_INCREMENT counter, so the next row gets id 1. The table itself, with its columns and keys, stays.
  5. C MySQL's utf8 is a three-byte subset that cannot store every character: In MySQL, utf8 means utf8mb3, a three-byte subset that cannot store emoji and some rarer characters, and MySQL warns about it (warning 3719). utf8mb4 is full Unicode.

Exit Ticket

  1. Name two statements that work in phpMyAdmin's SQL tab and one command that only the mysql client understands. Answer: SQL (any client): SHOW TABLES;, DESCRIBE books;, USE bookstore;, SELECT DATABASE();, CREATE TABLE .... Client only: \G, SOURCE file.sql, \c, help, exit, status.
  2. A script stops with ERROR 1046 (3D000): No database selected. Give two ways to fix it. Answer: Put USE bookstore; (the right database) before the statement, or write the full name bookstore.books. It happens in a fresh session and after dropping the database you were in.
  3. title is VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL. You run ALTER TABLE books MODIFY COLUMN title VARCHAR(250);. What did you lose, and which statement shows it? Answer: The NOT NULL rule: MODIFY replaces the whole definition. DESCRIBE books; (or SHOW COLUMNS FROM books LIKE 'title';) shows YES under Null. Fix: repeat it, VARCHAR(250) NOT NULL.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 B ("DESCRIBE does nothing in phpMyAdmin"). Thinks only SELECT is SQL. Show the Structure tab and the SQL tab running DESCRIBE.
  • Q2 A ("it only needs a semicolon"). Has not seen the quote prompt. Type SELECT 'abc; live: the semicolon becomes part of the text.
  • Q4 B (1045). Reads every login failure as a password problem. The message says Unknown database: read the message.
  • Q7 B ("utf8 stores emoji"). The most expensive misconception of the day: a database created with utf8 loses emoji years later. Repeat: always utf8mb4.
  • Q9 B ("misspelled database"). Mixes 1046 with 1049. Write the pair on the board: 1049 = no database by that name; 1046 = no database chosen.
  • Q11 A ("still required"). Expects ALTER to keep what it is not told. The MODIFY trap from the demo; the exit ticket's question 3.
  • Exit ticket 2 answered with "create the database". Needs the cabinet picture: the database exists, the session is just not inside it.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 3: Data Types, Constraints and CRUD

Answers and reasoning for every Session 3 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 3 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-Up: Session 2

Not graded.

W1
The prompt shows ->. What do you type?
A) exit
B) ; and Enter
C) Ctrl+C
D) USE bookstore;
Why: -> means the statement is not finished: the client waits for the semicolon (Lesson 1.3).
W2
MySQL says ERROR 1146 (42S02): Table 'mysql.books' doesn't exist. What do you check first?
A) Reinstall MySQL
B) Whether books was dropped
C) Which database you are in: the message says mysql, not bookstore
D) The password
Why: The message names the database it looked in. USE bookstore; (or bookstore.books) fixes it.
W3
Which of these works in phpMyAdmin's SQL tab?
A) DESCRIBE books;
B) SOURCE create_bookstore.sql
C) \G
D) exit
Why: DESCRIBE is SQL; the other three are mysql client commands.
W4
What does SHOW COLUMNS print under Null?
CREATE TABLE w4 (id INT PRIMARY KEY, title VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL);
ALTER TABLE w4 MODIFY COLUMN title VARCHAR(250);
SHOW COLUMNS FROM w4 LIKE 'title';
A) NO: NOT NULL is kept
B) YES: MODIFY replaced the whole definition
C) Nothing: ERROR 1064
D) NO, with a warning
Why: MODIFY uses exactly the definition you typed: repeat NOT NULL. Session 2's exit ticket question 3. MySQL prints:
+-------+--------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| Field | Type         | Null | Key | Default | Extra |
+-------+--------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| title | varchar(250) | YES  |     | NULL    |       |
+-------+--------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
W5
Right after DROP DATABASE scratch; (you were inside scratch), CREATE TABLE t (id INT); fails. Why?
A) The table exists (1050)
B) A syntax error (1064)
C) Permission denied
D) No database selected (1046): USE one, or write database.table
Why: Dropping the current database leaves the session with none.

2.2 · Data Types & Constraints: self-check

Q1
A column holds a discount as a whole percentage from 0 to 100. Which type is the smallest sensible choice?
A) TINYINT UNSIGNED
B) INT
C) DECIMAL(5, 2)
D) VARCHAR(3)
Why: TINYINT UNSIGNED holds 0 to 255 in one byte: enough for 0 to 100, and it refuses negatives. Add a CHECK (percent BETWEEN 0 AND 100) to enforce the exact range.
B is wrong: It works, but takes four bytes for a value that fits in one.
C is wrong: There are no decimals to store.
D is wrong: A number stored as text sorts and adds up wrongly.
Q2
What does the SELECT print?
CREATE TABLE q2 (amount DECIMAL(5, 2));
INSERT INTO q2 VALUES (7.005);
SELECT amount FROM q2;
7.01
Why: A DECIMAL(5, 2) keeps two decimals, so 7.005 is rounded (half up) to 7.01, with a Note 1265. Rounding to the column's precision is not an error; a value too big for the column is (1264).
Q3
What happens when this runs?
CREATE TABLE q3 (due DATE);
INSERT INTO q3 VALUES ('2026-04-31');
A) 2026-05-01 is stored
B) 2026-04-30 is stored
C) ERROR 1292 (22007): Incorrect date value: '2026-04-31' for column 'due' at row 1
D) NULL is stored with a warning
Why: April has 30 days. In strict mode (the default) MySQL checks that a date really exists and refuses the row.
A is wrong: MySQL does not roll dates over.
B is wrong: MySQL does not correct your value.
D is wrong: That is what happens with strict mode switched off: a reason to keep it on.
Q4
A library stores the moment each long-term loan ends, some of them in 2045. Which type fits best?
A) TIMESTAMP
B) DATE
C) YEAR
D) DATETIME
Why: A moment needs a date AND a time, and DATETIME runs to the year 9999. This course uses DATETIME everywhere.
A is wrong: TIMESTAMP stops in January 2038 on MySQL (error 1292 for 2040 in the lesson).
B is wrong: A DATE has no time of day.
C is wrong: YEAR holds only a year (1901 to 2155).
Q5
email is NOT NULL. An INSERT gives it the value NULL explicitly. Which error?
A) ERROR 1364: Field 'email' doesn't have a default value
B) ERROR 1048: Column 'email' cannot be null
C) ERROR 1062: Duplicate entry
D) ERROR 3819: Check constraint is violated
Why: Two flavors of NOT NULL: an explicit NULL gives 1048; leaving the column out of the INSERT when it has no default gives 1364.
A is wrong: 1364 is the column left out entirely.
C is wrong: 1062 is a duplicate in a UNIQUE column or primary key.
D is wrong: 3819 is a CHECK constraint.
Q6
In which order does the SELECT list the three sizes?
CREATE TABLE q6 (size ENUM('small', 'medium', 'large'));
INSERT INTO q6 VALUES ('large'), ('small'), ('medium');
SELECT size FROM q6 ORDER BY size;
small, medium, large
Why: ORDER BY on an ENUM sorts by the position in the list the column was declared with, not alphabetically (that would be large, medium, small). Handy here; surprising elsewhere.

2.2 · Data Types & Constraints: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE TABLE codes (code CHAR(6), label VARCHAR(6));
INSERT INTO codes VALUES ('ab  ', 'ab  ');
SELECT CHAR_LENGTH(code)  AS code_len,
       CHAR_LENGTH(label) AS label_len
FROM codes;
+----------+-----------+
| code_len | label_len |
+----------+-----------+
|        2 |         4 |
+----------+-----------+
Why: Both received 'ab' plus two spaces. CHAR pads when storing and strips trailing spaces when reading (2); VARCHAR keeps them (4).
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE TABLE counts (n TINYINT UNSIGNED);
INSERT INTO counts VALUES (255);
INSERT INTO counts VALUES (256);
SELECT n FROM counts;
ERROR 1264 (22003): Out of range value for column 'n' at row 1
+------+
| n    |
+------+
|  255 |
+------+
Why: TINYINT UNSIGNED stops at 255: the first INSERT works, the second is refused with 1264, and the table holds one row.

2.3 · CRUD Operations: self-check

Q7
Why should you avoid INSERT INTO books VALUES (NULL, 'Emma', ...); without a column list?
A) It is slower than listing the columns
B) NULL is not allowed in an id
C) It inserts the row twice
D) It depends on the table's exact column order: an added column breaks it (1136) or shifts values silently
Why: With a column list, the INSERT keeps working whatever columns are added later.
A is wrong: Speed is the same.
B is wrong: NULL in an AUTO_INCREMENT column means "give it the next number".
C is wrong: One VALUES list is one row.
Q8
What does the last SELECT print?
CREATE TABLE q8 (id INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY, t VARCHAR(10));
INSERT INTO q8 (t) VALUES ('a');
INSERT INTO q8 (t) VALUES ('b'), ('c'), ('d');
SELECT LAST_INSERT_ID();
2
Why: After a multi-row INSERT, LAST_INSERT_ID() returns the id of the FIRST row of that statement (b got 2, c 3, d 4).
Q9
An UPDATE reports Rows matched: 0 Changed: 0 Warnings: 0. What is most likely?
A) The WHERE found no rows: often a typo in the value or the wrong column
B) It worked: every row was changed
C) The rows already had those values
D) Safe-updates mode refused it
Why: Rows matched counts what the WHERE found; Changed counts the rows that got a new value. Matched 0 means nothing was found.
B is wrong: Changed 0 says nothing was changed.
C is wrong: That is Rows matched 1 (or more), Changed 0.
D is wrong: A refused statement prints ERROR 1175, not a row count.
Q10
After SET SQL_SAFE_UPDATES = 1;, which statement still runs? (id is the primary key.)
A) UPDATE books SET price = 0;
B) DELETE FROM books WHERE price > 10;
C) UPDATE books SET price = 9.99 WHERE id = 2;
D) DELETE FROM books;
Why: Safe-updates refuses UPDATE and DELETE unless the WHERE uses a key column (or a LIMIT is given). The other three end with ERROR 1175.
A is wrong: No WHERE at all: 1175.
B is wrong: price is not a key column: 1175, even with a WHERE.
D is wrong: No WHERE: 1175.
Q11
A category name already exists. Which statement updates its description and keeps its id and every other column?
A) REPLACE INTO categories (name, description) VALUES (...);
B) INSERT ... AS new ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE description = new.description;
C) INSERT IGNORE INTO categories (name, description) VALUES (...);
D) DELETE the old row, then INSERT the new one
Why: The upsert changes only the columns you name in the UPDATE part and keeps the row (and its id).
A is wrong: REPLACE deletes the old row and inserts a new one: a new id, and unlisted columns get their defaults.
C is wrong: INSERT IGNORE skips the row: nothing is updated.
D is wrong: That is what REPLACE does, with the same problems.
Q12
What does ROW_COUNT() print?
CREATE TABLE q12 (name VARCHAR(20) PRIMARY KEY, note VARCHAR(30));
INSERT INTO q12 VALUES ('a', 'x'), ('b', 'y');
INSERT INTO q12 VALUES ('a', 'x'), ('b', 'z'), ('c', 'w') AS new
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE note = new.note;
SELECT ROW_COUNT() AS affected;
3
Why: An upsert counts 1 per inserted row, 2 per updated row, 0 per row that already had those values: a unchanged (0) + b updated (2) + c inserted (1) = 3.

2.3 · CRUD Operations: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE TABLE notes (id INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY, t VARCHAR(10));
INSERT INTO notes (t) VALUES ('a'), ('b'), ('c');
DELETE FROM notes WHERE id = 3;
INSERT INTO notes (t) VALUES ('d');
SELECT * FROM notes;
+----+------+
| id | t    |
+----+------+
|  1 | a    |
|  2 | b    |
|  4 | d    |
+----+------+
Why: Id 3 is gone and is never handed out again, so the new row gets 4.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE TABLE tags (name VARCHAR(20) PRIMARY KEY, uses INT NOT NULL);
INSERT INTO tags VALUES ('sale', 1);
INSERT INTO tags VALUES ('sale', 1) AS new
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE uses = tags.uses + new.uses;
SELECT * FROM tags;
+------+------+
| name | uses |
+------+------+
| sale |    2 |
+------+------+
Why: The second INSERT finds 'sale' and adds the new uses to the old: 1 + 1 = 2. The column is written tags.uses on purpose: an unqualified uses = uses + new.uses is ERROR 1052 (Column 'uses' in field list is ambiguous) on MySQL 9.7, because the row alias new has a column named uses too.

Lab Reference Solutions

Lab 2.2-A: labs/module-03/l2_2-data-types-constraints/types_matter_starter.sql → types_matter_solution.sql

As shipped, the starter prints (no error anywhere):

+-------+--------+
| title | copies |
+-------+--------+
| 1984  | 9      |
| Emma  | 15     |
| Dune  | 120    |
+-------+--------+
+-------------------+
| total_cost        |
+-------------------+
| 556.9499866962433 |
+-------------------+
+-------+------------+
| title | arrives    |
+-------+------------+
| 1984  | 01/15/2027 |
| Dune  | 03/10/2026 |
| Emma  | 12/01/2026 |
+-------+------------+
+---------------+
| last_delivery |
+---------------+
| 12/01/2026    |
+---------------+

The solution prints:

+-------+--------+
| title | copies |
+-------+--------+
| Dune  |    120 |
| Emma  |     15 |
| 1984  |      9 |
+-------+--------+
+------------+
| total_cost |
+------------+
|     556.95 |
+------------+
+-------+------------+
| title | arrives    |
+-------+------------+
| Dune  | 2026-03-10 |
| Emma  | 2026-12-01 |
| 1984  | 2027-01-15 |
+-------+------------+
+---------------+
| last_delivery |
+---------------+
| 2027-01-15    |
+---------------+

What each TODO teaches: (1) text sorts character by character, so '9' comes after '15' and '120'; (2) FLOAT stores binary fractions: 556.9499866962433 instead of 556.95, a crumb that grows with every order; (3) dates as text sort by the month digits, so 12/01/2026 looks like the latest; a DATE column refuses the US format (1292), and STR_TO_DATE converts it. Accept SMALLINT UNSIGNED for copies and any DECIMAL with 2 decimals for the cost.

Lab 2.2-B: labs/module-03/l2_2-data-types-constraints/membership_rules_starter.sql → membership_rules_solution.sql

The solution prints:

+----+------------------+------------+--------+--------+------------+
| id | email            | first_name | tier   | points | birthday   |
+----+------------------+------------+--------+--------+------------+
|  1 | ana@example.com  | Ana        | reader |      0 | NULL       |
|  2 | ben@example.com  | Ben        | reader |      0 | NULL       |
|  3 | cleo@example.com | Cleo       | patron |    250 | 1990-07-14 |
+----+------------------+------------+--------+--------+------------+
+-----------------+-----------------+
| CONSTRAINT_NAME | CONSTRAINT_TYPE |
+-----------------+-----------------+
| chk_points      | CHECK           |
| PRIMARY         | PRIMARY KEY     |
| email           | UNIQUE          |
+-----------------+-----------------+
+------------+-------------------------------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
| Field      | Type                                | Null | Key | Default | Extra          |
+------------+-------------------------------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
| id         | int                                 | NO   | PRI | NULL    | auto_increment |
| email      | varchar(100)                        | NO   | UNI | NULL    |                |
| first_name | varchar(30)                         | NO   |     | NULL    |                |
| tier       | enum('reader','collector','patron') | NO   |     | reader  |                |
| points     | int                                 | NO   |     | 0       |                |
| birthday   | date                                | YES  |     | NULL    |                |
+------------+-------------------------------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+

As shipped, Ana's and Ben's tier and points are NULL and the only constraint is PRIMARY. With the rules, the defaults fill them (reader, 0), SHOW COLUMNS says NO under Null for the four required columns, and information_schema lists the CHECK by the name chk_points. points INT UNSIGNED NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 also keeps negatives out (with 1264 instead of 3819): accept it, and ask which error message is easier to read.

Handout: labs/module-03/l2_2-data-types-constraints/debug_bad_rows.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints:

ERROR 1048 (23000): Column 'email' cannot be null
ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry 'ana@example.com' for key 'kit_members.email'
ERROR 1265 (01000): Data truncated for column 'tier' at row 1
ERROR 1292 (22007): Incorrect date value: '1995-02-29' for column 'birthday' at row 1
ERROR 1364 (HY000): Field 'first_name' doesn't have a default value
ERROR 3819 (HY000): Check constraint 'chk_points' is violated.
+----+-----------------+------------+-----------+--------+----------+
| id | email           | first_name | tier      | points | birthday |
+----+-----------------+------------+-----------+--------+----------+
|  1 | ana@example.com | Ana        | reader    |      0 | NULL     |
|  2 | ben@example.com | Ben        | collector |      0 | NULL     |
|  4 | ivy@example.com | Ivy        | reader    |     75 | NULL     |
+----+-----------------+------------+-----------+--------+----------+
  1. Bug A, 1048 (R1): an explicit NULL email. Fix: a real email, 'dev@example.com'.
  2. Bug B, 1062 (R1): ana@example.com is taken. Fix: Anna's own email.
  3. Bug C, 1265 (R3): gold is not one of the tiers. Fix: 'patron' (or add gold to the ENUM, repeating the whole definition).
  4. Bug D, 1292 (R5's type): 1995 was not a leap year. Fix: '1995-02-28' (or '1996-02-29').
  5. Bug E, 1364 (R2): first_name is required and has no default. Fix: add it.
  6. Bug F, 3819 (R4): points may not be negative. Fix: 0 or a positive number.

Notice Ivy's id: 4, not 3. The refused duplicate (Bug B) had already reserved id 3. Fixed, the file lists nine members.

Lab 2.3-A: labs/module-03/l2_3-crud-operations/new_arrivals_starter.sql → new_arrivals_solution.sql

The solution prints:

+--------------+
| first_new_id |
+--------------+
|            1 |
+--------------+
+----+--------------+-------+
| id | title        | price |
+----+--------------+-------+
|  4 | Moby Dick    | 12.40 |
|  5 | Little Women | 10.20 |
+----+--------------+-------+
+--------------+
| rows_changed |
+--------------+
|            2 |
+--------------+
+--------------+-------+----------+
| title        | price | in_stock |
+--------------+-------+----------+
| Frankenstein |  6.99 |        0 |
+--------------+-------+----------+
+----+--------------+------------+
| id | title        | published  |
+----+--------------+------------+
|  1 | Emma         | 1815-12-23 |
|  2 | Frankenstein | 1818-01-01 |
+----+--------------+------------+
+--------------+
| rows_deleted |
+--------------+
|            2 |
+--------------+
+----+------------------+-------+----------+
| id | title            | price | in_stock |
+----+------------------+-------+----------+
|  3 | Dracula          |  9.75 |        1 |
|  4 | Moby Dick        | 13.02 |        1 |
|  5 | Little Women     | 10.71 |        1 |
|  6 | The Time Machine |  6.95 |        1 |
|  7 | Middlemarch      | 11.10 |        1 |
+----+------------------+-------+----------+

Reading it: LAST_INSERT_ID() is the FIRST id of a multi-row INSERT (1); the preview and the UPDATE use the same WHERE, and both find 2 rows; 12.40 × 1.05 = 13.02 and 10.20 × 1.05 = 10.71; Frankenstein is changed once more (6.99, out of stock) and then deleted with Emma; Middlemarch gets 7 because ids 1 and 2 are never reused.

Lab 2.3-B: labs/module-03/l2_3-crud-operations/category_feed_starter.sql → category_feed_solution.sql

The solution prints:

+----------+
| affected |
+----------+
|        3 |
+----------+
+----+---------+-----------------------------+
| id | name    | description                 |
+----+---------+-----------------------------+
|  1 | Fiction | Novels and stories          |
|  2 | Poetry  | Poems and verse collections |
|  3 | Travel  | Guides and journeys         |
|  4 | History | The past, told well         |
|  5 | Cooking | Recipes and kitchen craft   |
+----+---------+-----------------------------+
+---------+------+-----------------------------------------------------+
| Level   | Code | Message                                             |
+---------+------+-----------------------------------------------------+
| Warning | 1062 | Duplicate entry 'History' for key 'categories.name' |
+---------+------+-----------------------------------------------------+
+----+---------+-----------------------------+
| id | name    | description                 |
+----+---------+-----------------------------+
|  2 | Poetry  | Poems and verse collections |
|  3 | Travel  | Guides and journeys         |
|  4 | History | The past, told well         |
|  5 | Cooking | Recipes and kitchen craft   |
|  9 | Fiction | NULL                        |
+----+---------+-----------------------------+

Reading it: one statement handled three rows: Poetry updated (2), Travel unchanged (0), Cooking inserted (1) = 3. INSERT IGNORE stored nothing and turned the 1062 into a warning. REPLACE deleted Fiction (id 1) and inserted a new row: id 9 and a NULL description. Ids 6 to 8 were reserved by the upsert's two non-inserting rows and by INSERT IGNORE, and never used. On MariaDB, AS new is a syntax error: VALUES(description) there.

Handout: labs/module-03/l2_3-crud-operations/debug_crud_slips.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints:

ERROR 1136 (21S01): Column count doesn't match value count at row 1
ERROR 1054 (42S22): Unknown column 'Rosa' in 'field list'
ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry 'nina@example.com' for key 'customers.email'
ERROR 1175 (HY000): You are using safe update mode and you tried to update a table without a WHERE that uses a KEY column.
ERROR 1292 (22007): Truncated incorrect DOUBLE value: 'Chicago'
+----+------------+-----------+--------+-------------------+
| id | first_name | last_name | city   | email             |
+----+------------+-----------+--------+-------------------+
|  1 | Nina       | Park      | Denver | nina@example.com  |
|  2 | Omar       | Haddad    | Boston | omar@example.com  |
|  3 | Paula      | Reyes     | Denver | paula@example.com |
+----+------------+-----------+--------+-------------------+
  1. Bug A, 1136: three columns, two values. Fix: add Quinn's email.
  2. Bug B, 1054: Rosa without quotes is read as a column name. Fix: 'Rosa'.
  3. Bug C, 1062: nina@example.com is taken (email is UNIQUE). Fix: Nora's own email.
  4. Bug D, 1175: with safe-updates on, WHERE city = 'Denver' is refused because city is not a key column. Fix: turn safe-updates off for a reviewed change, or name the rows by key: WHERE id IN (1, 3) (after a preview).
  5. Bug E, 1292: AND instead of a comma in SET. With text values strict mode refuses it ('Chicago' cannot be read as a number); with numbers the same slip is silent (Lesson 2.3, Common Mistake 5). Fix: SET city = 'Chicago', email = 'omar.h@example.com'.

Fixed, the final SELECT shows five customers: Nina and Paula in Aurora, Omar in Chicago with omar.h@example.com, Quinn and Rosa.

Note on Lesson 2.3's Bonus Challenge

The challenge's hint qualifies the old value: copies = stock_counts.copies + new.copies. The unqualified copies = copies + new.copies is ERROR 1052 on MySQL 9.7 (and 8.4), Column 'copies' in field list is ambiguous, because the row alias new has a copies column too. Verified: 5 copies of Dune twice gives 10. Learners who type it without the table name will hit 1052; that is the teaching moment.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 2.2 · Data Types & Constraints
  1. B DECIMAL(8, 2): DECIMAL stores exact decimal values, so prices and totals are correct to the cent. DECIMAL(8, 2) holds up to 999999.99.
  2. C Fails with error 1264, Out of range value: A signed TINYINT holds -128 to 127. In strict mode an out-of-range value is an error (1264) and the row is not stored.
  3. A One with an email that another row already has: UNIQUE refuses a second row with the same value, with error 1062 Duplicate entry. A NULL would fail too, but with 1048, because of NOT NULL.
  4. D A two-letter country code: CHAR suits values that always have the same length, such as a two-letter country code or a 13-digit ISBN.
  5. B WHERE in_stock: BOOLEAN is a TINYINT, and MySQL treats any non-zero number as true. WHERE in_stock keeps 1, 5 and every other non-zero value.
Lesson 2.3 · CRUD Operations
  1. A Every book's price becomes 0.00: Without a WHERE clause, UPDATE applies to every row. Only safe-updates mode (error 1175) would stop it, and it is off by default.
  2. C The row was found but already had those values: The WHERE found one row, but it already had the new values, so there was nothing to change. Matched 0 would point at a WHERE that found nothing.
  3. B ... AS new ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE description = new.description: The row alias form (AS new, then new.column) is the current MySQL syntax; VALUES() is deprecated. On MariaDB, which lacks the alias, VALUES() is still the way.
  4. C 11: AUTO_INCREMENT only moves forward. The gap at 10 stays, and the new row gets the next number, 11. Only TRUNCATE resets the counter.
  5. A Sets its price to 0.00 and leaves it in stock: SET reads price = (15.99 AND (in_stock = FALSE)). The book is in stock, so the comparison is false (0), and the price becomes 0.00. Separate assignments with commas.

Exit Ticket

  1. Why is money stored as DECIMAL and never as FLOAT? One sentence and one example. Answer: DECIMAL is exact; FLOAT stores binary fractions, so most decimal values are slightly off and the crumbs add up: 0.1 stored as FLOAT times 3 printed 0.30000000447034836 in the demo, and the lab's FLOAT total was 556.9499866962433 instead of 556.95.
  2. An UPDATE says Rows matched: 3 Changed: 2. What happened to the third row? Answer: The WHERE found it, but it already had the new value, so nothing changed in it. (Matched 0 would mean the WHERE found nothing.)
  3. What do you run before every UPDATE or DELETE that touches more than one row, and why? Answer: A SELECT with exactly the same WHERE: it shows which rows will be touched, and its row count must match the UPDATE's Rows matched. Safe-updates mode (SET SQL_SAFE_UPDATES = 1; or mysql --safe-updates) is the second net.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 B (INT). Correct but not thoughtful: ask for the smallest type that comfortably fits.
  • Q3 A or B. Expects MySQL to repair a date. Strict mode refuses; that is the point.
  • Q4 A (TIMESTAMP). Has not met the 2038 limit: show the lesson's 2040 error.
  • Q5 A (1364). Mixes the two NOT NULL errors: explicit NULL 1048, left out 1364.
  • Q9 C. Mixes Matched 0 with Changed 0. Write both lines on the board.
  • Q10 B. Thinks any WHERE satisfies safe-updates; it wants a key column.
  • Q11 A (REPLACE). The word sounds right. REPLACE is delete plus insert: a new id.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 4: Filtering, Sorting and Limiting

Answers and reasoning for every Session 4 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, Checkpoint 1 (rubric, model answer, Part D), the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 4 12 self-check items Checkpoint 1 Confidential

Warm-Up: Session 3

Not graded.

W1
Which type stores a book's price?
A) FLOAT
B) DECIMAL(8, 2)
C) VARCHAR(10)
D) INT
Why: Money is exact: DECIMAL. FLOAT's binary fractions add up to crumbs (the types lab: 556.9499866962433).
W2
An INSERT gives the NOT NULL column email the value NULL. Which error?
A) 1364
B) 1062
C) 1048
D) 3819
Why: 1048 for an explicit NULL; 1364 when the column is left out.
W3
What does the second line say after this UPDATE?
CREATE TABLE w3 (id INT PRIMARY KEY, price DECIMAL(6, 2));
INSERT INTO w3 VALUES (1, 9.99);
UPDATE w3 SET price = 9.99 WHERE id = 1;
A) Rows matched: 0 Changed: 0
B) Rows matched: 1 Changed: 1
C) ERROR 1062
D) Rows matched: 1 Changed: 0
Why: The row was found, but already had that value. MySQL prints:
Query OK, 0 rows affected
Rows matched: 1  Changed: 0  Warnings: 0
W4
What do you run right before an UPDATE that touches several rows?
A) A SELECT with exactly the same WHERE
B) SHOW TABLES;
C) SET SQL_SAFE_UPDATES = 0;
D) Nothing: UPDATE can be undone
Why: The preview: its row count must match Rows matched.
W5
Which statement gives an existing category a NEW id?
A) INSERT ... AS new ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE ...
B) INSERT IGNORE ...
C) REPLACE INTO ...
D) UPDATE ... WHERE id = 3
Why: REPLACE deletes and re-inserts; the upsert keeps the row.

3.1 · WHERE Clauses, Operators & Filtering: self-check

Q1
Two of the 8 customers live in Chicago and two have no city. How many rows does this return?
SELECT * FROM customers WHERE city != 'Chicago';
A) 6
B) 4
C) 8
D) 0
Why: != drops the two Chicago rows AND the two NULL cities: "is an unknown city different from Chicago?" is unknown, not true. Add `OR city IS NULL` to keep them (6).
A is wrong: 6 is what you get once you add OR city IS NULL.
C is wrong: Chicago is excluded on purpose.
D is wrong: Only city = NULL (or a NULL inside NOT IN) finds nothing.
Q2
On the Module 3 data, what does this print?
SELECT COUNT(*) AS n
FROM books
WHERE price BETWEEN 8.99 AND 9.99;
4
Why: BETWEEN includes both ends: The Great Gatsby and The Catcher in the Rye (8.99), Brave New World (9.49) and 1984 (9.99).
Q3
Which WHERE keeps every book NOT written by Tolkien or Orwell?
A) WHERE author NOT IN ('J.R.R. Tolkien', 'George Orwell')
B) WHERE author != 'J.R.R. Tolkien' OR author != 'George Orwell'
C) WHERE NOT author = 'J.R.R. Tolkien' OR 'George Orwell'
D) WHERE author NOT LIKE 'Tolkien, Orwell'
Why: NOT IN removes both names: 7 of the 10 books remain (Tolkien and Orwell have three between them).
B is wrong: Every author differs from at least one of the two names, so the condition is always true: all 10 books.
C is wrong: OR 'George Orwell' is a separate test on a piece of text, not on author.
D is wrong: That compares with one literal string, which no author matches.
Q4
created_at is a DATETIME. Which WHERE finds the customers who signed up on January 27, 2026 (Frank, at 13:30)?
A) WHERE created_at = '2026-01-27'
B) WHERE created_at BETWEEN '2026-01-27' AND '2026-01-27'
C) WHERE created_at >= '2026-01-27' AND created_at < '2026-01-28'
D) WHERE created_at LIKE 2026-01-27
Why: A half-open range: on or after midnight of the day, and before midnight of the next. It never misses an evening row.
A is wrong: '2026-01-27' means midnight exactly: 0 rows (measured).
B is wrong: The same single instant, midnight: 0 rows.
D is wrong: Without quotes 2026-01-27 is arithmetic (1998), and LIKE on a date is the wrong tool.
Q5
Which first names does this return, in order?
SELECT first_name
FROM customers
WHERE city = 'New York' OR city = 'Chicago' AND first_name LIKE 'G%'
ORDER BY first_name;
Alice, Frank, Grace
Why: AND runs before OR: city = 'New York' OR (city = 'Chicago' AND first_name LIKE 'G%'). Both New Yorkers pass, and of the Chicago customers only Grace. Written with parentheses, (New York OR Chicago) AND LIKE 'G%', it would return only Grace.
Q6
Which of these returns no rows at all?
A) WHERE city IS NULL
B) WHERE city <=> NULL
C) WHERE city LIKE '%o%'
D) WHERE city NOT IN ('Reno', NULL)
Why: NOT IN ('Reno', NULL) means city != 'Reno' AND city != NULL; the second part is unknown for every row, so nothing passes (COUNT 0 on the course data).
A is wrong: IS NULL finds Eve and Hank.
B is wrong: <=> is NULL-safe: it works like IS NULL here.
C is wrong: New York, Chicago, Los Angeles and Houston all contain an o.

3.1 · WHERE Clauses, Operators & Filtering: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT COUNT(*) AS n
FROM customers
WHERE city NOT IN ('Chicago', 'Houston');
+---+
| n |
+---+
| 3 |
+---+
Why: Chicago (2) and Houston (1) are left out, and so are Eve and Hank: NULL NOT IN (...) is unknown. Alice, Carol and Frank remain: 3.
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT title
FROM books
WHERE title LIKE '%o%o%'
ORDER BY title;
+-----------------------+
| title                 |
+-----------------------+
| Lord of the Rings     |
| To Kill a Mockingbird |
+-----------------------+
Why: %o%o% needs two o's anywhere: Lord of the Rings (Lord, of) and To Kill a Mockingbird (To, Mockingbird).

3.2 · Sorting & Limiting Results: self-check

Q7
What does ORDER BY in_stock, price DESC do?
A) in_stock ascending (0 first), then price descending within each group
B) Both columns descending
C) ERROR 1064: DESC must come after every column
D) price first, then in_stock
Why: ASC and DESC belong to the one column right before them; ASC is the default. For both descending write `in_stock DESC, price DESC`.
B is wrong: DESC applies only to price.
C is wrong: It is valid SQL.
D is wrong: The first column listed is the first sort key.
Q8
What does this print?
SELECT title
FROM books
ORDER BY pages DESC
LIMIT 1 OFFSET 2;
Dune (412 pages: the third longest)
Why: Longest first: Lord of the Rings 1178, Pride and Prejudice 432, Dune 412. OFFSET 2 skips two rows, LIMIT 1 keeps the next.
Q9
What happens with 'price' in quotes: ORDER BY 'price' DESC LIMIT 3?
A) The three most expensive books, as intended
B) ERROR 1054: Unknown column
C) No error, but the rows come in no particular order: 'price' is a fixed piece of text
D) The rows are sorted alphabetically by title
Why: In single quotes, price is text, the same for every row, so there is nothing to sort by. Column names are written bare (or in backticks).
A is wrong: On the test server it returned Dune, 1984, To Kill a Mockingbird: not the top three.
B is wrong: A text value is valid, so there is no error: the danger.
D is wrong: Nothing sorts by title here.
Q10
Where do Eve and Hank (no city) appear with ORDER BY city DESC, first_name?
A) First
B) They are left out
C) ERROR 1064
D) Last
Why: MySQL treats NULL as smaller than any value: first in an ascending sort, last in a descending one. To force them last in an ascending sort: `ORDER BY city IS NULL, city`.
A is wrong: That is the ascending sort.
B is wrong: ORDER BY never removes rows.
C is wrong: Valid SQL.
Q11
A list sorted by id shows 20 rows per page, and page 1 ended at id 57. Which query gets the next page fast, even on a huge table?
A) ... ORDER BY id LIMIT 20 OFFSET 57
B) ... WHERE id > 57 ORDER BY id LIMIT 20
C) ... WHERE id >= 57 LIMIT 20
D) ... ORDER BY id LIMIT 57, 20
Why: Keyset pagination: remember the last row seen and ask for the rows after it. With the primary key's index MySQL jumps straight to id 58.
A is wrong: OFFSET counts rows, not ids, and reads and discards them all: slower with every page.
C is wrong: It repeats id 57 and has no ORDER BY.
D is wrong: The comma form is OFFSET 57: the same row-counting problem.
Q12
With 3 books per page, how many pages? What does this print?
SELECT CEILING(COUNT(*) / 3) AS pages FROM books;
4
Why: 10 books / 3 = 3.33, and CEILING rounds up: the last page holds one book.

3.2 · Sorting & Limiting Results: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT first_name, city
FROM customers
ORDER BY city DESC, first_name
LIMIT 3;
+------------+-------------+
| first_name | city        |
+------------+-------------+
| Alice      | New York    |
| Frank      | New York    |
| Carol      | Los Angeles |
+------------+-------------+
Why: Descending puts New York first and NULLs last; first_name breaks the New York tie: Alice, Frank, then Carol (Los Angeles).
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT title, pages
FROM books
ORDER BY pages
LIMIT 2 OFFSET 8;
+---------------------+-------+
| title               | pages |
+---------------------+-------+
| Pride and Prejudice |   432 |
| Lord of the Rings   |  1178 |
+---------------------+-------+
Why: Shortest first, skip 8 of the 10: the two longest are left, Pride and Prejudice (432) and Lord of the Rings (1178).

Checkpoint 1: Rubric and Model Answer

Five rows, 0 to 2 points each (10 in all). Score from the learner's file (run it once) and the Part D page. 8 to 10: on track. 5 to 7: review the weakest row before Session 6. Under 5: a one-to-one before Lesson 4.1.

CriterionStrong (2)Partial (1)Missing (0)
Script hygiene (Modules 1 and 2)Runs twice in a row: DROP DATABASE IF EXISTS, utf8mb4 + a named collation, USE; a comment per table; every INSERT has a column listRuns once but not twice, or no collation, or one INSERT without a column listDoes not run, or tables created in the wrong database (1046 or bookstore)
Types (2.1, 2.2)DECIMAL for the offer, DATE for received, ENUM for the condition, BOOLEAN for paid, sensible VARCHAR sizes; book_condition (not the reserved word condition)One wrong type (FLOAT money, a date as text, INT for the condition)Several wrong types, or everything VARCHAR
Constraints (2.2)AUTO_INCREMENT primary keys; NOT NULL where the spec says required; UNIQUE email; CHECK chk_offer for 0.50 to 20.00; DEFAULT 'good' and DEFAULT FALSETwo or three rules missing (often the CHECK or a DEFAULT), or the CHECK unnamedPrimary keys only, or no rules
Changing data safely (2.3)B3 and B5 by id; B4 preview with the same WHERE and rows_paid 2; B6 one upsert (AS new ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE) that leaves Ivy as id 2 in HendersonResults right but no preview, or B6 done with UPDATE (works, but not the asked statement) or REPLACE (new id)A WHERE that changes the wrong rows, or an UPDATE/DELETE without WHERE
Reading MySQL's answers (Part C, Part D)C1 to C3 match the model answer; Part D names 3819, 1062 and 1265 with the rule each one protects and a fixPart C right, two of the three error numbersPart C wrong, or Part D blank
Model answer: labs/module-04/checkpoint-1/checkpoint_solution.sql
-- ---------- Part A · build (rerunnable) ----------
DROP DATABASE IF EXISTS kit_cp1_tradein;
CREATE DATABASE kit_cp1_tradein
    CHARACTER SET utf8mb4
    COLLATE utf8mb4_unicode_ci;
USE kit_cp1_tradein;

-- People who bring books in. email is how the desk finds them again.
CREATE TABLE sellers (
    id     INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    name   VARCHAR(80)  NOT NULL,
    email  VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL UNIQUE,
    city   VARCHAR(50)
);

-- One row per book brought in. seller_id says whose it is (Module 4 makes it a foreign key).
CREATE TABLE trade_ins (
    id              INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    seller_id       INT NOT NULL,
    title           VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL,
    book_condition  ENUM('like new', 'good', 'worn') NOT NULL DEFAULT 'good',
    offer           DECIMAL(5, 2) NOT NULL,
    received        DATE NOT NULL,
    paid            BOOLEAN NOT NULL DEFAULT FALSE,
    CONSTRAINT chk_offer CHECK (offer BETWEEN 0.50 AND 20.00)
);

-- ---------- Part B · fill and change ----------
-- B1 · three sellers
INSERT INTO sellers (name, email, city) VALUES
    ('Mo Rahman', 'mo@example.com', 'Reno'),
    ('Ivy Chen', 'ivy@example.com', NULL),
    ('Sam Okoro', 'sam@example.com', 'Sparks');

-- B2 · five trade-ins (paid is left to its default)
INSERT INTO trade_ins (seller_id, title, book_condition, offer, received) VALUES
    (1, 'Dune', 'good', 4.00, '2026-05-01'),
    (1, 'Emma', 'worn', 1.50, '2026-05-01'),
    (2, 'The Hobbit', 'like new', 6.00, '2026-05-03'),
    (3, '1984', 'good', 3.50, '2026-05-04'),
    (3, 'Dracula', 'worn', 1.00, '2026-05-04');

-- B3 · Dune's offer goes up by one dollar (it is id 1)
UPDATE trade_ins SET offer = offer + 1.00 WHERE id = 1;

-- B4 · Mo is paid for all his books: preview, then the same WHERE
SELECT id, title, paid FROM trade_ins WHERE seller_id = 1 ORDER BY id;
UPDATE trade_ins SET paid = TRUE WHERE seller_id = 1;
SELECT ROW_COUNT() AS rows_paid;

-- B5 · Dracula (id 5) was turned down
DELETE FROM trade_ins WHERE id = 5;

-- B6 · Ivy sends her details again, now with a city: insert her, or update her
INSERT INTO sellers (name, email, city)
VALUES ('Ivy Chen', 'ivy@example.com', 'Henderson') AS new
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE city = new.city;

-- ---------- Part C · read ----------
-- C1 · every trade-in, with 10% extra if taken as store credit
SELECT id, title, book_condition, offer,
       ROUND(offer * 1.10, 2) AS store_credit, paid
FROM trade_ins
ORDER BY id;

-- C2 · the conditions that occur, each once
SELECT DISTINCT book_condition FROM trade_ins ORDER BY book_condition;

-- C3 · the sellers now
SELECT id, name, city FROM sellers ORDER BY id;

DROP DATABASE kit_cp1_tradein;

It prints:

Database changed
+----+-------+------+
| id | title | paid |
+----+-------+------+
|  1 | Dune  |    0 |
|  2 | Emma  |    0 |
+----+-------+------+
+-----------+
| rows_paid |
+-----------+
|         2 |
+-----------+
+----+------------+----------------+-------+--------------+------+
| id | title      | book_condition | offer | store_credit | paid |
+----+------------+----------------+-------+--------------+------+
|  1 | Dune       | good           |  5.00 |         5.50 |    1 |
|  2 | Emma       | worn           |  1.50 |         1.65 |    1 |
|  3 | The Hobbit | like new       |  6.00 |         6.60 |    0 |
|  4 | 1984       | good           |  3.50 |         3.85 |    0 |
+----+------------+----------------+-------+--------------+------+
+----------------+
| book_condition |
+----------------+
| like new       |
| good           |
| worn           |
+----------------+
+----+-----------+-----------+
| id | name      | city      |
+----+-----------+-----------+
|  1 | Mo Rahman | Reno      |
|  2 | Ivy Chen  | Henderson |
|  3 | Sam Okoro | Sparks    |
+----+-----------+-----------+
Part D answers (each run after the model answer)
D1
The desk offers 25.00 for a rare copy of Emma.
INSERT INTO trade_ins (seller_id, title, offer, received)
VALUES (1, 'Emma', 25.00, '2026-05-02');
ERROR 3819 (HY000): Check constraint 'chk_offer' is violated.
Why: The CHECK chk_offer keeps offers from 0.50 to 20.00 (the desk's rule). Fix: an offer within the range, or a manager changes the rule with ALTER TABLE.
D2
Mo comes back with a new name on his card, same email.
INSERT INTO sellers (name, email)
VALUES ('Mo R.', 'mo@example.com');
ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry 'mo@example.com' for key 'sellers.email'
Why: email is UNIQUE: one seller per email, so the desk finds people again. Fix: UPDATE Mo's name by id, or the B6 upsert.
D3
Ivy's Emma is in "mint" condition.
INSERT INTO trade_ins (seller_id, title, book_condition, offer, received)
VALUES (2, 'Emma', 'mint', 3.00, '2026-05-02');
ERROR 1265 (01000): Data truncated for column 'book_condition' at row 1
Why: mint is not on the ENUM list; 1265 "Data truncated" is how MySQL says so. Fix: 'like new', or add the value by redefining the whole column.
Scoring notes
  • Accept DECIMAL(4, 2) or wider for the offer, TINYINT(1) for paid, VARCHAR sizes close to the spec, and B4 without the ROW_COUNT line if the preview is there.
  • B6 by UPDATE gives the right table but not the asked statement: 1 on row 4. B6 by REPLACE gives Ivy a new id (4) and is a 1 too, with a note on why.
  • A file that only runs once (no DROP DATABASE IF EXISTS, or CREATE without IF NOT EXISTS after an edit) loses the hygiene point it most needs.
  • Column named condition: a 1064 they should have fixed with the hint; if the rest runs, score the rest.

Lab Reference Solutions

Lab 3.1-A: labs/module-04/l3_1-where-clauses-operators-filtering/staff_requests_starter.sql → staff_requests_solution.sql

The solution prints:

+-----------------------+---------------+
| title                 | author        |
+-----------------------+---------------+
| 1984                  | George Orwell |
| Fahrenheit 451        | Ray Bradbury  |
| To Kill a Mockingbird | Harper Lee    |
+-----------------------+---------------+
+-----------------+-------+------------+
| title           | price | published  |
+-----------------+-------+------------+
| Brave New World |  9.49 | 1932-01-01 |
| The Hobbit      | 10.99 | 1937-09-21 |
| 1984            |  9.99 | 1949-06-08 |
+-----------------+-------+------------+
+------------+-----------+
| first_name | last_name |
+------------+-----------+
| Alice      | Johnson   |
| Grace      | Wilson    |
+------------+-----------+
+-------+
| title |
+-------+
| 1984  |
| Dune  |
+-------+
+------------+-------------+
| first_name | city        |
+------------+-------------+
| Bob        | Chicago     |
| Carol      | Los Angeles |
| Dave       | Houston     |
| Eve        | NULL        |
| Grace      | Chicago     |
| Hank       | NULL        |
+------------+-------------+
+-----------------+---------------+-------+
| title           | author        | price |
+-----------------+---------------+-------+
| Brave New World | Aldous Huxley |  9.49 |
| 1984            | George Orwell |  9.99 |
| Fahrenheit 451  | Ray Bradbury  | 10.49 |
+-----------------+---------------+-------+
+------------+---------------------+
| first_name | created_at          |
+------------+---------------------+
| Grace      | 2026-02-02 08:55:00 |
| Hank       | 2026-02-10 19:12:00 |
+------------+---------------------+

Request 5 is the one most learners get wrong first: city != 'New York' alone returns 4 rows; Eve and Hank need OR city IS NULL. Request 7 uses a half-open range; BETWEEN '2026-02-01' AND '2026-02-14' would miss anyone who signed up on the 14th after midnight.

Lab 3.1-B: labs/module-04/l3_1-where-clauses-operators-filtering/null_detective_starter.sql → null_detective_solution.sql

The solution prints:

+------------+-------------+------------+-------------+---------+
| first_name | city        | is_chicago | not_chicago | no_city |
+------------+-------------+------------+-------------+---------+
| Alice      | New York    |          0 |           1 |       0 |
| Bob        | Chicago     |          1 |           0 |       0 |
| Carol      | Los Angeles |          0 |           1 |       0 |
| Dave       | Houston     |          0 |           1 |       0 |
| Eve        | NULL        |       NULL |        NULL |       1 |
| Frank      | New York    |          0 |           1 |       0 |
| Grace      | Chicago     |          1 |           0 |       0 |
| Hank       | NULL        |       NULL |        NULL |       1 |
+------------+-------------+------------+-------------+---------+
+------------+
| in_chicago |
+------------+
|          2 |
+------------+
+-----------+
| elsewhere |
+-----------+
|         4 |
+-----------+
+---------+
| no_city |
+---------+
|       2 |
+---------+
+------------+-------------+
| first_name | city        |
+------------+-------------+
| Alice      | New York    |
| Bob        | Chicago     |
| Carol      | Los Angeles |
| Eve        | NULL        |
| Frank      | New York    |
| Grace      | Chicago     |
| Hank       | NULL        |
+------------+-------------+
+------------+------+
| first_name | city |
+------------+------+
| Eve        | NULL |
| Hank       | NULL |
+------------+------+

As shipped, no_city is 0 and queries 3 and 4 return nothing: the NULL inside NOT IN makes every row unknown, and = NULL is never true. With IS NULL the counts add to 8.

Handout: labs/module-04/l3_1-where-clauses-operators-filtering/debug_filters.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints:

ERROR 1054 (42S22): Unknown column 'Chicago' in 'where clause'
ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near '< 500 ORDER BY pages' at line 1
Empty set
Empty set
+------------------------+---------------+----------+
| title                  | author        | in_stock |
+------------------------+---------------+----------+
| Pride and Prejudice    | Jane Austen   |        1 |
| The Catcher in the Rye | J.D. Salinger |        0 |
+------------------------+---------------+----------+
  1. Bug A, 1054: Chicago without quotes is read as a column. Fix: 'Chicago'.
  2. Bug B, 1064: each comparison needs its column: pages > 300 AND pages < 500 (or BETWEEN 301 AND 499).
  3. Bug C, silent: = NULL finds nothing. Fix: IS NULL (Eve, Hank).
  4. Bug D, silent: = takes the % literally. Fix: LIKE 'The %' (three titles).
  5. Bug E, silent: AND before OR lets the out-of-stock Catcher in. Fix: in_stock = TRUE AND (author = 'Jane Austen' OR author = 'J.D. Salinger') (only Pride and Prejudice).

Empty set
+------------------------+
| note                   |
+------------------------+
| page 4 printed nothing |
+------------------------+
+----+-----------------------+-------+
| id | title                 | price |
+----+-----------------------+-------+
|  2 | 1984                  |  9.99 |
| 10 | Fahrenheit 451        | 10.49 |
|  6 | The Hobbit            | 10.99 |
|  3 | To Kill a Mockingbird | 11.99 |
+----+-----------------------+-------+

Two books cost 8.99, so id breaks the tie and the pages never repeat or skip a book. Page 4 (OFFSET 12) is not an error: it prints nothing (in the interactive client, Empty set). The keyset query returns exactly page 2.

Handout: labs/module-04/l3_2-sorting-limiting-results/debug_sorting.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints:

ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near 'WHERE in_stock = TRUE' at line 1
ERROR 1054 (42S22): Unknown column '3' in 'order clause'
ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near '* 4' at line 1
+-----------------------+-------+
| title                 | pages |
+-----------------------+-------+
| Dune                  |   412 |
| 1984                  |   328 |
| To Kill a Mockingbird |   281 |
+-----------------------+-------+
+------------------------+----------+-------+
| title                  | in_stock | pages |
+------------------------+----------+-------+
| The Catcher in the Rye |        0 |   234 |
| The Great Gatsby       |        0 |   180 |
| Lord of the Rings      |        1 |  1178 |
| Pride and Prejudice    |        1 |   432 |
| Dune                   |        1 |   412 |
| 1984                   |        1 |   328 |
| Brave New World        |        1 |   311 |
| The Hobbit             |        1 |   310 |
| To Kill a Mockingbird  |        1 |   281 |
| Fahrenheit 451         |        1 |   158 |
+------------------------+----------+-------+
  1. Bug A, 1064: WHERE must come before ORDER BY.
  2. Bug B, 1054: there is no third column; sort by name: ORDER BY price (a column you did not select is allowed).
  3. Bug C, 1064: no formulas in LIMIT/OFFSET: OFFSET 8.
  4. Bug D, silent: 'pages' in quotes is a constant; the three rows are arbitrary (they can differ). Fix: ORDER BY pages DESC, title.
  5. Bug E, silent: in_stock is ascending. Fix: ORDER BY in_stock DESC, pages DESC, title.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 3.1 · WHERE Clauses, Operators & Filtering
  1. B 0: city = NULL is unknown for every row, even for Eve and Hank, and WHERE only keeps rows where the test is true. The query returns an empty set without any error. Use IS NULL instead.
  2. C a = 1 OR (b = 2 AND c = 3): AND is evaluated before OR, just as multiplication comes before addition. Add parentheses whenever you mix the two so the meaning is clear.
  3. A 9.49, 10.99 and everything in between: BETWEEN includes both ends. It is shorthand for: price is at least 9.49 AND price is at most 10.99.
  4. C LIKE '___': Each underscore matches exactly one character, so three underscores match exactly three: Bob and Eve.
  5. A The text value Austen is missing its quotes: Without quotes, Austen is read as a column name. Text values belong in single quotes: WHERE author = 'Jane Austen'.
Lesson 3.2 · Sorting & Limiting Results
  1. A At the top: MySQL sorts NULL as smaller than every value, so NULLs come first in ascending order and last in descending order.
  2. C Rows 7 to 9: OFFSET 6 skips rows 1 to 6, then LIMIT 3 keeps the next three: rows 7, 8 and 9.
  3. B The first row is the 7.99 book; the second can be either 8.99 book: The 7.99 book is certain to come first, but the 8.99 books tie, so which one fills the second slot is up to the server. Add a unique tie-breaker such as title.
  4. A LIMIT 4 OFFSET (3 - 1) * 4: LIMIT and OFFSET accept plain numbers or placeholders, not expressions. Compute 8 first and write OFFSET 8.
  5. C LIMIT 20 OFFSET 80: Page N starts after (N - 1) x page size rows: (5 - 1) x 20 = 80 rows skipped, then 20 returned.

Exit Ticket

  1. Why does WHERE city != 'Chicago' skip Eve and Hank, and how do you include them? Answer: Their city is NULL, so the comparison is unknown, not true, and only true rows are kept. Add OR city IS NULL.
  2. Write the WHERE clause for: books from $9 to $11, both ends included, that are not by Tolkien. Answer: WHERE price BETWEEN 9 AND 11 AND author != 'J.R.R. Tolkien' (on the course data: Brave New World, 1984, Fahrenheit 451).
  3. Why should every ORDER BY that feeds a LIMIT end with a unique column? Answer: If rows tie at the cut-off, the server may return either one, and the choice can change between runs: page 1 and page 2 can repeat or skip a row. A unique column (id, title here) makes the order repeatable.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A (6). Counts the NULL cities as "not Chicago". The day's main idea: unknown is dropped.
  • Q3 B. The classic != OR != that is always true. Translate it into English with them.
  • Q4 A. Treats a DATETIME like a DATE: show the 0-row result.
  • Q7 B. Thinks DESC applies to the whole ORDER BY.
  • Q9 A. Does not see the quotes: the most dangerous silent sort mistake.
  • Q11 A or D. Confuses rows skipped with ids: an OFFSET counts rows.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 5: Aggregates and Subqueries

Answers and reasoning for every Session 5 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs and the fixed debug handouts, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 5 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up

W1
Who comes back, and why?
SELECT first_name, city, id
FROM customers
WHERE city = 'Chicago' OR city = 'Houston' AND id > 5
ORDER BY id;
Bob and Grace. AND binds before OR: city = 'Chicago' OR (city = 'Houston' AND id > 5), and Dave (Houston) has id 4.
+------------+---------+----+
| first_name | city    | id |
+------------+---------+----+
| Bob        | Chicago |  2 |
| Grace      | Chicago |  7 |
+------------+---------+----+
W2
Two customers have no city. Why does this find nobody? What would you write instead?
SELECT first_name FROM customers WHERE city = NULL;
Nobody: nothing equals NULL, not even NULL. Write WHERE city IS NULL (Eve and Hank).
Empty set
W3
In which order must you write WHERE, ORDER BY and LIMIT?
WHERE, then ORDER BY, then LIMIT. Lesson 3.3 adds GROUP BY and HAVING between WHERE and ORDER BY.
W4
Which three books does this show?
SELECT title, price
FROM books
ORDER BY price DESC, title
LIMIT 3 OFFSET 2;
The 3rd, 4th and 5th most expensive: OFFSET 2 skips Lord of the Rings and Dune.
+-----------------------+-------+
| title                 | price |
+-----------------------+-------+
| To Kill a Mockingbird | 11.99 |
| The Hobbit            | 10.99 |
| Fahrenheit 451        | 10.49 |
+-----------------------+-------+
W5
Predict: how many customers live outside Chicago, according to this query?
SELECT first_name
FROM customers
WHERE city NOT IN ('Chicago', NULL);
None: city != 'Chicago' AND city != NULL is unknown for every row. A NULL inside a NOT IN list empties the result (Lesson 3.1), and it comes back inside subqueries in Lesson 3.4.
Empty set

3.3 · Aggregate Functions & GROUP BY: self-check

Q1
Grace (Chicago) and Hank (no city) are the only customers who signed up in February. What does this print?
SELECT COUNT(*), COUNT(city)
FROM customers
WHERE created_at >= '2026-02-01';
A) 2 and 2
B) 2 and 1
C) 1 and 1
D) 8 and 6
Why: WHERE keeps the two February rows first. COUNT(*) counts both rows; COUNT(city) counts only the filled-in cities, and Hank's is NULL.
A is wrong: COUNT(column) skips NULL; only COUNT(*) counts every row.
C is wrong: Hank's row is still a row: COUNT(*) counts rows, not values.
D is wrong: Those are the counts for the whole table; the WHERE line runs before the counting.
Q2
You want only the authors whose books average more than $10. Where does that condition go?
A) In WHERE, before GROUP BY
B) In ORDER BY
C) In HAVING, after GROUP BY
D) In the SELECT list, as AVG(price) > 10
Why: The average belongs to a whole group (an author's pile), so it can only be tested after the piles exist: HAVING. A condition on single rows (price > 10) goes in WHERE.
A is wrong: WHERE runs before the piles exist; an aggregate there is error 1111.
B is wrong: ORDER BY sorts; it never removes rows.
D is wrong: That adds a 0/1 column; every author still appears.
Q3
The flat bookstore has no books by Isaac Asimov. What does this print?
SELECT COUNT(*) AS n, MAX(price) AS top
FROM books
WHERE author = 'Isaac Asimov';
One row: n = 0 and top = NULL
Why: An aggregate over no rows still returns one row. COUNT answers 0; every other aggregate has no values to work with and returns NULL (use COALESCE(MAX(price), 0) when a report needs 0).
Q4
What happens when this runs on MySQL 9.7?
SELECT city, first_name
FROM customers
GROUP BY city;
A) ERROR 1055 (42000): first_name is not in GROUP BY and not aggregated
B) One row per city, each with one customer's first name
C) ERROR 1140: an aggregated query without GROUP BY
D) One row per customer, sorted by city
Why: Chicago's pile holds Bob and Grace: which first name belongs in Chicago's one row? MySQL's default ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY mode refuses to guess (error 1055). Fix: aggregate it (MIN(first_name)), group by it, or drop it.
B is wrong: That is what MariaDB (no ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY) does silently: an arbitrary name per city.
C is wrong: 1140 is for an aggregate with no GROUP BY at all; this query has one.
D is wrong: GROUP BY makes one row per group, never one per input row.
Q5
A colleague says: "I don't need ORDER BY after GROUP BY city: the cities always come out sorted." On MySQL 9.7, is that right?
A) Yes: GROUP BY sorts the groups by the grouped column
B) Yes, but only when the column has an index
C) Only on Windows servers
D) No: since MySQL 8.0 the order of groups is not guaranteed; add ORDER BY
Why: MySQL 8.0 and later return groups in whatever order is convenient (the lesson's Common Mistake 5 shows them in first-seen order). MariaDB still sorts, so the same query can come back in a different order on another server. A report always says ORDER BY.
A is wrong: That was true of MySQL 5.7 and is still true of MariaDB, not of MySQL 9.7.
B is wrong: An index may make a sorted order likely, never guaranteed.
C is wrong: The operating system has nothing to do with it.
Q6
What does this print? (Under $10: three books in stock and two out of stock.)
SELECT in_stock, COUNT(*) AS books
FROM books
WHERE price < 10
GROUP BY in_stock
ORDER BY in_stock;
Two rows: 0 | 2 and 1 | 3
Why: WHERE keeps the five books under $10; GROUP BY makes one pile per in_stock value (0 = out of stock, 1 = in stock); ORDER BY in_stock puts 0 first.

3.3 · Aggregate Functions & GROUP BY: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT COUNT(*), COUNT(city), COUNT(DISTINCT city)
FROM customers
WHERE id > 4;
+----------+-------------+----------------------+
| COUNT(*) | COUNT(city) | COUNT(DISTINCT city) |
+----------+-------------+----------------------+
|        4 |           2 |                    2 |
+----------+-------------+----------------------+
Why: Customers 5 to 8 are Eve (NULL), Frank (New York), Grace (Chicago) and Hank (NULL): four rows, two filled-in cities, two different cities.
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT in_stock, MAX(pages) AS longest
FROM books
GROUP BY in_stock
HAVING MAX(pages) > 300
ORDER BY in_stock;
+----------+---------+
| in_stock | longest |
+----------+---------+
|        1 |    1178 |
+----------+---------+
Why: Two piles: in stock (longest 1178, Lord of the Rings) and out of stock (longest 234). HAVING drops the out-of-stock pile; one row remains.

3.4 · Subqueries & Nested Queries: self-check

Q7
The average price is 10.99. What does this print?
SELECT COUNT(*)
FROM books
WHERE price < (SELECT AVG(price) FROM books);
A) 4
B) 6
C) 7
D) 10
Why: The subquery returns 10.99; six books cost less (7.99 to 10.49). The Hobbit costs exactly 10.99 and is not less than itself.
A is wrong: Four books cost MORE than or equal to the average (three above, The Hobbit equal).
C is wrong: Seven would include The Hobbit, which costs exactly 10.99.
D is wrong: Ten is every book: the subquery is a real filter, not a decoration.
Q8
What happens when this runs?
SELECT title
FROM books
WHERE price = (SELECT price FROM books
               WHERE author = 'J.R.R. Tolkien');
A) ERROR 1242 (21000): Subquery returns more than 1 row
B) It returns Lord of the Rings
C) ERROR 1241 (21000): Operand should contain 1 column(s)
D) ERROR 1248 (42000): Every derived table must have its own alias
Why: Tolkien has two books, so the subquery returns two prices, and = needs one value. Fix: IN (any of his prices) or an aggregate such as MAX(price). The query would have worked while he had one book: the lesson's warning about queries that break when the data grows.
B is wrong: MySQL does not pick one of the values for you.
C is wrong: 1241 is too many COLUMNS; here there is one column and too many rows.
D is wrong: 1248 is for a subquery in FROM without a name; this one is in WHERE.
Q9
Customer 5 (Eve) has no city; customer 6 (Frank) lives in New York. What does this print?
SELECT COUNT(*)
FROM customers
WHERE city NOT IN (SELECT city FROM customers WHERE id IN (5, 6));
0 (no customer passes the test)
Why: The list is (NULL, 'New York'). city NOT IN (...) means city != NULL AND city != 'New York', and anything compared with NULL is unknown, so no row is ever kept. Add AND city IS NOT NULL inside the subquery, or use NOT EXISTS.
Q10
You need the customers whom no other customer shares a city with, and city can be NULL. Which approach gives a predictable answer with no NULL trap?
A) city NOT IN (SELECT city FROM customers ...)
B) city != ALL (SELECT city ...)
C) NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM customers AS other WHERE other.city = c.city AND other.id != c.id)
D) city NOT LIKE (SELECT city ...)
Why: NOT EXISTS asks "is there a matching row?": a NULL city never matches, so it cannot empty the result, and the customers with no city are kept (nobody is known to share their city). That is why many developers write NOT EXISTS by default.
A is wrong: One NULL in the list empties a NOT IN.
B is wrong: != ALL is the same as NOT IN, with the same trap.
D is wrong: LIKE compares with one pattern; with several rows it fails with error 1242.
Q11
What does this print? (Two customers have no city.)
SELECT MAX(n) AS biggest_group
FROM (
    SELECT city, COUNT(*) AS n
    FROM customers
    GROUP BY city
) AS per_city;
2
Why: The derived table holds one row per city, NULL included: NULL 2, Chicago 2, New York 2, Houston 1, Los Angeles 1. The outer MAX reads that small table: 2. The alias per_city is required (error 1248 without it).
Q12
This was meant to list the books priced above THEIR OWN author's average. Why does it return three books instead of one?
SELECT title, author, price
FROM books
WHERE price > (
    SELECT AVG(price)
    FROM books
    WHERE author = author
)
ORDER BY price;
A) The subquery runs once per author, but AVG ignores the out-of-stock books
B) MySQL treats the second author as a string literal
C) HAVING is missing from the subquery
D) Both author names refer to the inner table, so author = author is always true and the subquery returns the store average
Why: Inside the subquery the nearest table wins: both sides of author = author are the inner books row. The subquery is not correlated at all and returns 10.99, so you get "above the store average" by accident. Fix: alias both copies (FROM books AS b ... FROM books AS b2 WHERE b2.author = b.author).
A is wrong: AVG uses every book; in_stock plays no part here.
B is wrong: An unquoted name is a column, not a string.
C is wrong: HAVING filters groups; there are no groups in this subquery.

3.4 · Subqueries & Nested Queries: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT COUNT(*) AS long_books
FROM books
WHERE pages > (SELECT AVG(pages) FROM books);
+------------+
| long_books |
+------------+
|          3 |
+------------+
Why: The inner query returns 382.4; only Lord of the Rings (1178), Pride and Prejudice (432) and Dune (412) are longer.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT first_name
FROM customers
WHERE city NOT IN (SELECT city FROM customers WHERE id = 5);
Empty set
Why: The list is just (NULL): Eve's city. city NOT IN (NULL) is unknown for every row, so nobody comes back, not even the customers with a city.

Workbook Worked Examples (outputs for reference)

The worked examples print exactly what the workbook shows; they are new examples, not the lessons' own. 3.3: Tolkien 1937-09-21 to 1954-07-29; WHERE leaves one pile (in stock, 5 books over $10); HAVING drops the out-of-stock pile (2 books). 3.4: the latest signup per city is Grace (Chicago), Dave (Houston), Carol (Los Angeles), Frank (New York).

Lab Reference Solutions

Lab 3.3-A: labs/module-05/l3_3-aggregate-functions/customer_summary_starter.sql → customer_summary_solution.sql

The solution prints:

+-----------+-----------+--------------+--------+---------------------+---------------------+
| customers | with_city | missing_city | cities | first_signup        | latest_signup       |
+-----------+-----------+--------------+--------+---------------------+---------------------+
|         8 |         6 |            2 |      4 | 2026-01-04 10:15:00 | 2026-02-10 19:12:00 |
+-----------+-----------+--------------+--------+---------------------+---------------------+
+---------+---------+
| month   | signups |
+---------+---------+
| 2026-01 |       6 |
| 2026-02 |       2 |
+---------+---------+
+----------+-----------+---------------------+
| city     | customers | first_joined        |
+----------+-----------+---------------------+
| New York |         2 | 2026-01-04 10:15:00 |
| Chicago  |         2 | 2026-01-06 14:02:00 |
+----------+-----------+---------------------+
+-------------+-----------------+
| city        | january_signups |
+-------------+-----------------+
| New York    |               2 |
| (no city)   |               1 |
| Chicago     |               1 |
| Houston     |               1 |
| Los Angeles |               1 |
+-------------+-----------------+

What each TODO teaches: (1) the three COUNTs and the NULL rule in one row (8 rows, 6 filled-in cities, 2 missing, 4 different cities); (2) grouping by an expression label (DATE_FORMAT), which sorts correctly as text; (3) HAVING on a pile size, plus MIN as "when did the pile start"; (4) WHERE and HAVING-free grouping with COALESCE: the NULL cities form one pile, labeled (no city). Sticking points: COALESCE in SELECT but GROUP BY city (works, but the label and the pile differ in the learner's head: ask them to group by the same expression), and TODO 3's HAVING typed before GROUP BY (1064).

Handout: labs/module-05/l3_3-aggregate-functions/debug_group_by.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints:

ERROR 1140 (42000): In aggregated query without GROUP BY, expression #1 of SELECT list contains nonaggregated column 'bookstore.customers.first_name'; this is incompatible with sql_mode=only_full_group_by
ERROR 1055 (42000): Expression #2 of SELECT list is not in GROUP BY clause and contains nonaggregated column 'bookstore.customers.last_name' which is not functionally dependent on columns in GROUP BY clause; this is incompatible with sql_mode=only_full_group_by
ERROR 1111 (HY000): Invalid use of group function
ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near 'GROUP BY city' at line 4
  1. Bug A, 1140: a whole-table COUNT next to a plain column. Fix: drop first_name (SELECT COUNT(*) AS customers FROM customers;).
  2. Bug B, 1055: last_name is neither grouped nor aggregated. Fix: MIN(last_name) AS a_last_name (or drop it) and add ORDER BY.
  3. Bug C, 1111: an aggregate in WHERE. Fix: HAVING COUNT(*) > 1 after GROUP BY.
  4. Bug D, 1064: HAVING written before GROUP BY. Fix: GROUP BY city, then HAVING.

Fixed (blocks in blocks_S05.py), the four queries print, in order:

| New York    | Johnson     |         2 |
+-------------+-------------+-----------+
+----------------+-------+
| author         | books |
+----------------+-------+
| J.R.R. Tolkien |     2 |
+----------------+-------+
+----------+-----------+
| city     | customers |
+----------+-----------+
| NULL     |         2 |

Lab 3.4-A: labs/module-05/l3_4-subqueries/page_checks_starter.sql → page_checks_solution.sql

If a learner asks: with an EMPTY list, > ALL is true for every row while > MAX(...) keeps nothing (the lesson's edge case).
