Handout: labs/module-05/l3_4-subqueries/debug_subqueries.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints:

ERROR 1242 (21000): Subquery returns more than 1 row
ERROR 1241 (21000): Operand should contain 1 column(s)
ERROR 1248 (42000): Every derived table must have its own alias
ERROR 1235 (42000): This version of MySQL doesn't yet support 'LIMIT & IN/ALL/ANY/SOME subquery'
  1. Bug A, 1242: two February customers, so two dates for =. Fix: IN.
  2. Bug B, 1241: the IN list has two columns. Fix: select only author; SUM(pages) stays in HAVING.
  3. Bug C, 1248: the derived table has no name. Fix: AS city_counts.
  4. Bug D, 1235: LIMIT inside an IN subquery. Fix: wrap the LIMIT query in a derived table and select from it.

Fixed, the four queries print, in order:

+------------+---------------------+
| first_name | created_at          |
+------------+---------------------+
| Grace      | 2026-02-02 08:55:00 |
| Hank       | 2026-02-10 19:12:00 |
+------------+---------------------+
+---------------------+----------------+
| title               | author         |
+---------------------+----------------+
| Dune                | Frank Herbert  |
| Lord of the Rings   | J.R.R. Tolkien |
| The Hobbit          | J.R.R. Tolkien |
| Pride and Prejudice | Jane Austen    |
+---------------------+----------------+
+--------+
| AVG(n) |
+--------+
| 1.5000 |
+--------+
+---------------------+-------+
| title               | pages |
+---------------------+-------+
| Lord of the Rings   |  1178 |
| Pride and Prejudice |   432 |
+---------------------+-------+

On MariaDB Bug C is a 1064 syntax error near '' instead of 1248 (the lesson's version note).

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 3.3 · Aggregate Functions & GROUP BY
  1. B 6: COUNT(column) counts only the values that are not NULL: 8 rows minus the 2 missing cities is 6. COUNT(*) would return 8.
  2. C In a HAVING clause after GROUP BY: HAVING runs after GROUP BY, when each author's pile has been counted, so it can keep or drop whole groups by their aggregate.
  3. A 4: AVG skips the NULL completely, so it divides 5 + 3 + 4 = 12 by 3 values, not by 4 rows.
  4. B title is neither in GROUP BY nor inside an aggregate: With ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY on, every selected column must be grouped or aggregated. An author with two books has two titles, so title is ambiguous.
  5. A Add an ORDER BY clause: Since MySQL 8.0, GROUP BY makes no promise about order (MariaDB still sorts). ORDER BY is the only way to get the same order everywhere.
Lesson 3.4 · Subqueries & Nested Queries
  1. C 3: Only To Kill a Mockingbird, Dune and Lord of the Rings cost more than 10.99. The Hobbit equals the average, and greater-than does not include equal.
  2. B Use IN instead of =, or make the subquery return a single value: The subquery sits next to = or another single-value operator but returned a list. Use IN for a list, or reduce the subquery to one value with MIN, MAX or a unique condition.
  3. A No rows at all: NOT IN means not equal to every item. Not equal to NULL is unknown for every row, so no row can pass. Filter NULLs out of the subquery or use NOT EXISTS.
  4. C It refers to a column of the outer query's current row: A correlated subquery uses a column of the outer query's current row, such as b2.author = b.author, so its answer can differ for every outer row.
  5. B Adding an alias such as AS author_counts after the closing parenthesis: Every derived table must have its own alias. Adding AS author_counts after the closing parenthesis makes the query run.

Exit Ticket

  1. WHERE and HAVING: any single-row condition in WHERE (in_stock = TRUE, price > 10, published < '1950-01-01') and any aggregate condition in HAVING (COUNT(*) > 1, AVG(price) > 10, SUM(pages) > 400). A learner who puts price > 10 in HAVING has the clauses as interchangeable filters: reteach the run order.
  2. NOT IN with NULL: no rows. Fixes: AND city IS NOT NULL inside the subquery, or NOT EXISTS (which also keeps the NULL-city customers, a different but predictable answer).
  3. Correlated: it refers to the outer query's current row, so it is evaluated again for every outer row. With one table used twice, aliases (b for the outer row, b2 for the inner scan) are how you say which copy a column belongs to; without them author = author compares a row with itself.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A (2 and 2). Thinks COUNT(column) counts rows. Show COUNT(*) and COUNT(city) side by side again in the Session 6 warm-up.
  • Q2 A (WHERE). Treats WHERE and HAVING as the same filter. The run-order diagram is the cure; ask "when does WHERE run?"
  • Q4 B (one name per city). Has used MariaDB or old MySQL, or imagines MySQL picks "the first". The 1055 check exists so answers are honest.
  • Q7 C (7). Counted The Hobbit (exactly 10.99): < and <= are different questions. Worth ten seconds.
  • Q9 any number other than 0. Has not met the NOT IN trap yet: run the lesson's two queries side by side.
  • Q12 A or C. Looks for a missing clause instead of reading what author refers to. Ask them to run the subquery alone: it returns one number, the store average.
  • Exit ticket 3 with "it is inside another query". That is any subquery; correlated means it uses the OUTER row. Ask: "can you run it on its own?"
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 6: Relationships and Foreign Keys

Answers and reasoning for every Session 6 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs and the debug handouts, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 6 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up

W1
Three numbers: predict them. (Customers 1 to 5 signed up before 25 January; Eve has no city.)
SELECT COUNT(*), COUNT(city), COUNT(DISTINCT city)
FROM customers
WHERE created_at < '2026-01-25';
5, 4, 4: five rows, four filled-in cities (Eve's is NULL), four different cities.
+----------+-------------+----------------------+
| COUNT(*) | COUNT(city) | COUNT(DISTINCT city) |
+----------+-------------+----------------------+
|        5 |           4 |                    4 |
+----------+-------------+----------------------+
W2
price > 10 and COUNT(*) > 1: which goes in WHERE, and which in HAVING?
price > 10 in WHERE (one row's column); COUNT(*) > 1 in HAVING (a group's aggregate).
W3
Which book is it?
SELECT title
FROM books
WHERE pages = (SELECT MAX(pages) FROM books);
Lord of the Rings: the row behind MAX(pages).
+-------------------+
| title             |
+-------------------+
| Lord of the Rings |
+-------------------+
W4
How many customers? (Customer 7 is Grace in Chicago, 8 is Hank with no city.)
SELECT COUNT(*)
FROM customers
WHERE city NOT IN (SELECT city FROM customers WHERE id > 6);
0: the list is ('Chicago', NULL), and one NULL empties a NOT IN. Add AND city IS NOT NULL inside, or use NOT EXISTS.
+----------+
| COUNT(*) |
+----------+
|        0 |
+----------+
W5
What is wrong, and what is the error number?
SELECT author, title, MAX(price)
FROM books
GROUP BY author;
Error 1055: title is neither grouped nor aggregated (Tolkien's pile has two titles). Drop it or use MIN(title).
ERROR 1055 (42000): Expression #2 of SELECT list is not in GROUP BY clause and contains nonaggregated column 'bookstore.books.title' which is not functionally dependent on columns in GROUP BY clause; this is incompatible with sql_mode=only_full_group_by

4.1 · Table Relationships: self-check

Q1
A customer can have many delivery addresses; each address belongs to exactly one customer. Where does the foreign key go?
A) customers.address_id
B) addresses.customer_id
C) A junction table customer_addresses
D) In both tables, one pointing at the other
Why: Ask "can one customer have many addresses?" Yes: the key goes on the many side, in addresses (like books.author_id and orders.customer_id).
A is wrong: A column holds one value: each customer could then have only one address (the lesson's Mistake 5).
C is wrong: A junction table is for many-to-many; an address does not belong to several customers.
D is wrong: Two keys pointing at each other duplicate one fact and make inserting either row first impossible.
Q2
Which of these needs a junction table?
A) One author writes many books
B) Each employee has at most one manager
C) Students and the courses they take
D) Each order belongs to one customer
Why: A student takes many courses and a course has many students: two yeses make many-to-many, and that needs a third table with two foreign keys (enrollments), which can also hold facts about the link such as a grade.
A is wrong: One-to-many: books.author_id.
B is wrong: A self-referencing one-to-many: employees.manager_id.
D is wrong: One-to-many: orders.customer_id.
Q3
In the finished bookstore, what does this print?
SELECT book_id, COUNT(*) AS categories
FROM book_categories
GROUP BY book_id
HAVING COUNT(*) = 3
ORDER BY book_id;
Three rows: books 2, 7 and 10, each with 3
Why: Each row of the junction table is one link. Grouping the links per book and keeping the piles of three finds 1984 (2), Brave New World (7) and Fahrenheit 451 (10): Fiction, plus Dystopian and one more.
Q4
The flat books table types "J.R.R. Tolkien" in two rows. A staff member edits the spelling in one of them only. What is this called?
A) An update anomaly: one fact stored twice now disagrees with itself
B) An insert anomaly
C) A delete anomaly
D) Error 1062, a duplicate entry
Why: One fact (the author's name) is stored in several rows; changing one copy leaves the table believing in two Tolkiens, with no error. Storing the name once in authors removes the cause.
B is wrong: An insert anomaly is having nowhere to store something new (an author with no books).
C is wrong: A delete anomaly is losing a fact by deleting another (the last book removes the author).
D is wrong: No rule is broken: that is exactly why the anomaly is dangerous.
Q5
A colleague builds customer_profiles with customer_id INT NOT NULL and a foreign key, but leaves out UNIQUE. What has the relationship become?
A) Many-to-many
B) Still one-to-one: the foreign key enforces it
C) Nothing: MySQL refuses the CREATE TABLE
D) One-to-many: a customer can now have several profiles
Why: A one-to-one relationship in MySQL is a one-to-many whose foreign key column is also UNIQUE. Without UNIQUE, a second profile for Alice is accepted silently (the lesson's demo shows the 1062 that UNIQUE gives).
A is wrong: Many-to-many needs a junction table with two foreign keys.
B is wrong: A foreign key checks that the parent exists, not that it is referenced only once.
C is wrong: The statement is valid; the design is what changed.
Q6
The bookstore's table is called books. What happens?
CREATE TABLE kit_reviews (
    id      INT PRIMARY KEY,
    book_id INT NOT NULL,
    FOREIGN KEY (book_id) REFERENCES book(id)
);
ERROR 1824 (HY000): Failed to open the referenced table 'book'
Why: The foreign key names a table that does not exist (book). The same error appears when the name is right but the child is created before its parent: create parents first, children second (and drop in the opposite order).

4.1 · Table Relationships: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
INSERT INTO book_categories (book_id, category_id) VALUES (11, 3);
ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry '11-3' for key 'book_categories.PRIMARY'
Why: Animal Farm (11) is already in Classic (3): the composite primary key allows each pair once, and the message shows the pair as '11-3'.
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT COUNT(*) AS authors_without_books
FROM authors
WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT author_id FROM books);
+-----------------------+
| authors_without_books |
+-----------------------+
|                     1 |
+-----------------------+
Why: Every book's author_id is NOT NULL, so the NOT IN list holds no NULL and the query is safe: only Isaac Asimov (10) has no book.

4.2 · Foreign Keys & Referential Integrity: self-check

Q7
The bookstore has customers 1 to 8. What happens?
INSERT INTO orders (customer_id, order_date)
VALUES (9, '2026-07-01');
A) ERROR 1451 (23000): Cannot delete or update a parent row
B) ERROR 1452 (23000): Cannot add or update a child row
C) ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry
D) Order 13 is created for customer 9
Why: orders is the CHILD of customers; inserting a child whose parent (customer 9) does not exist is refused by fk_orders_customer: "add or update a child row", 1452. Nothing is inserted.
A is wrong: 1451 is about a PARENT that still has children (a delete or a key change).
C is wrong: Nothing is duplicated; the id is new.
D is wrong: That is exactly what a declared foreign key prevents: an orphan order.
Q8
The bookstore has 18 order lines; order 5 has two of them. What does the SELECT print?
START TRANSACTION;
DELETE FROM orders WHERE id = 5;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM order_items;
ROLLBACK;
A) 16
B) 18
C) ERROR 1451 (23000): Cannot delete or update a parent row
D) 0
Why: fk_items_order is ON DELETE CASCADE: deleting the order deletes its two lines too (16 left). ROLLBACK then puts all of it back.
B is wrong: The lines are not left behind as orphans: CASCADE removes them.
C is wrong: The delete is refused only when a RESTRICT rule protects the row; order lines cascade.
D is wrong: Only order 5's lines go, not every line.
Q9
James Okafor (8) manages Nina Kowalski (9). What does the SELECT print?
START TRANSACTION;
DELETE FROM employees WHERE id = 8;
SELECT manager_id FROM employees WHERE id = 9;
ROLLBACK;
NULL (Nina stays, with no manager)
Why: fk_employees_manager is ON DELETE SET NULL: when the manager row is deleted, the reports keep their rows and their manager_id becomes NULL. The column allows NULL, which SET NULL requires (error 1830 otherwise).
Q10
A music app deletes a playlist. Its rows in playlist_songs (the links between the playlist and its songs) should...
A) block the delete: ON DELETE RESTRICT
B) keep the links with playlist_id NULL: ON DELETE SET NULL
C) go with it: ON DELETE CASCADE
D) stay unchanged: leave the rule out (NO ACTION)
Why: A junction row is only the link itself: without the playlist it means nothing. That is the lesson's first question: can the child exist without its parent? No, so CASCADE (like book_categories and order_items).
A is wrong: Users would have to empty a playlist before deleting it: possible, but it protects nothing worth keeping.
B is wrong: The playlist_id is part of the composite primary key, which cannot be NULL, and a link to nothing is meaningless.
D is wrong: NO ACTION behaves like RESTRICT in MySQL: the delete would be refused.
Q11
The accountant wants fk_items_order changed from CASCADE to RESTRICT. How do you do it in MySQL?
A) ALTER TABLE order_items ALTER FOREIGN KEY fk_items_order ON DELETE RESTRICT;
B) ALTER TABLE order_items MODIFY CONSTRAINT fk_items_order ON DELETE RESTRICT;
C) SET FOREIGN_KEY_CHECKS = 0; then delete as usual
D) DROP FOREIGN KEY fk_items_order, then ADD CONSTRAINT fk_items_order ... ON DELETE RESTRICT
Why: There is no ALTER FOREIGN KEY: drop the key by name and add it again with the new rule (the index MySQL created stays and is reused). The lesson's Exercise 4 does exactly this and then restores CASCADE.
A is wrong: MySQL has no such statement (syntax error 1064).
B is wrong: MODIFY changes a column, not a constraint.
C is wrong: That switches checking off for the session; it changes no rule and lets orphans in.
Q12
What does this print?
SELECT DELETE_RULE
FROM information_schema.REFERENTIAL_CONSTRAINTS
WHERE CONSTRAINT_SCHEMA = 'bookstore'
  AND CONSTRAINT_NAME = 'fk_employees_manager';
SET NULL
Why: information_schema.REFERENTIAL_CONSTRAINTS lists every foreign key with its rules. The employees self-reference is ON DELETE SET NULL (and, with no ON UPDATE written, UPDATE_RULE says NO ACTION).

4.2 · Foreign Keys & Referential Integrity: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
START TRANSACTION;
DELETE FROM categories WHERE id = 4;
SELECT COUNT(*) AS links FROM book_categories;
ROLLBACK;
+-------+
| links |
+-------+
|    23 |
+-------+
Why: fk_bc_category cascades: Fantasy's two links (The Hobbit, Lord of the Rings) go with it, 25 - 2 = 23. ROLLBACK restores both.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
DELETE FROM customers WHERE id = 2;
ERROR 1451 (23000): Cannot delete or update a parent row: a foreign key constraint fails (`bookstore`.`orders`, CONSTRAINT `fk_orders_customer` FOREIGN KEY (`customer_id`) REFERENCES `customers` (`id`) ON DELETE RESTRICT ON UPDATE CASCADE)
Why: Bob has orders, and fk_orders_customer is RESTRICT: 1451, "delete or update a parent row". Nothing changes.

+----------------------+----------------+----------+
| publisher_name       | publisher_city | editions |
+----------------------+----------------+----------+
| Blue Door Publishing | Portland       |        2 |
| Harbor Lane Press    | Boston         |        1 |
| Harbor Lane Press    | Cambridge      |        1 |
| North Star Book      | Denver         |        1 |
| North Star Books     | Denver         |        1 |
+----------------------+----------------+----------+
+----+----------------+-----------------+
| id | title          | publisher_name  |
+----+----------------+-----------------+
|  5 | Fahrenheit 451 | North Star Book |
+----+----------------+-----------------+
+----------------------------+
| editions_without_publisher |
+----------------------------+
|                          0 |
+----------------------------+
+---------------------+----------------------+
| title               | publisher            |
+---------------------+----------------------+

| The Hobbit          | North Star Books     |

| name                 | city     | editions |

| Harbor Lane Press    | Boston   |        2 |
| North Star Books     | Denver   |        2 |
| Blue Door Publishing | Portland |        2 |
+----------------------+----------+----------+

What it teaches: the anomaly table shows both kinds of damage (Harbor Lane Press in two cities: a copy that drifted; North Star split by a typo: a copy that never matched). Step 4 is the point: the UPDATE ... JOIN leaves edition 5 unmatched, and without the IS NULL check Step 5 would fail with 1138 (or, with a nullable column, silently lose the publisher). Learners must also DECIDE Harbor Lane Press's city (the parent table holds one): the cost of an update anomaly is a judgment call during the migration.

Handout: labs/module-06/l4_2-foreign-keys/debug_fk_errors.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints:

ERROR 1452 (23000): Cannot add or update a child row: a foreign key constraint fails (`bookstore`.`orders`, CONSTRAINT `fk_orders_customer` FOREIGN KEY (`customer_id`) REFERENCES `customers` (`id`) ON DELETE RESTRICT ON UPDATE CASCADE)
ERROR 1451 (23000): Cannot delete or update a parent row: a foreign key constraint fails (`bookstore`.`order_items`, CONSTRAINT `fk_items_book` FOREIGN KEY (`book_id`) REFERENCES `books` (`id`) ON DELETE RESTRICT ON UPDATE CASCADE)
ERROR 3780 (HY000): Referencing column 'book_id' and referenced column 'id' in foreign key constraint 'fk_shelf_book' are incompatible.
ERROR 1830 (HY000): Column 'customer_id' cannot be NOT NULL: needed in a foreign key constraint 'fk_notes_customer' SET NULL
ERROR 6125 (HY000): Failed to add the foreign key constraint. Missing unique key for constraint 'fk_events_city' in the referenced table 'customers'
+--------+-------+
| orders | books |
+--------+-------+
|     12 |    11 |
+--------+-------+
  1. Bug A, 1452 (child insert): customer 42 does not exist. Use a real customer id, or create the customer first.
  2. Bug B, 1451 (parent delete): 1984 has sold order lines (fk_items_book RESTRICT). Mark it in_stock = FALSE instead.
  3. Bug C, 3780 (badly formed key): INT UNSIGNED against books.id INT. Copy the parent's exact type: INT.
  4. Bug D, 1830 (badly formed key): SET NULL on a NOT NULL column. Make customer_id INT (nullable), or choose RESTRICT.
  5. Bug E, 6125 (badly formed key): customers.city is not unique, so "the parent" would be ambiguous. Point at a primary key (here a cities table would be the real design).

The final SELECT shows 12 orders and 11 books: every refused statement changed nothing. On MariaDB, Bugs C, D and E all come back as ERROR 1005 ... errno: 150 "Foreign key constraint is incorrectly formed".

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 4.1 · Table Relationships
  1. B books.author_id: The foreign key goes on the many side. Each book holds one author_id, and any number of books can hold the same one.
  2. C A NULL would mean a name did not match, and dropping the text would lose that author: A NULL after the UPDATE means a name found no match in authors. Dropping the text column would then lose that book's author for good, so you check first.
  3. A The foreign key column is also UNIQUE: The foreign key column is also UNIQUE (or is the primary key), so no parent row can be referenced by more than one child row.
  4. C Error 1062: duplicate entry for the primary key: PRIMARY KEY (book_id, category_id) allows each pair once, so MySQL reports error 1062, a duplicate entry 2-1 for the primary key.
  5. B It records what the customer paid then, which must not change when the price does: books.price is today's price; price_at_time records what was paid on the order date. They are two different facts, so old orders keep their real amounts when prices change.
Lesson 4.2 · Foreign Keys & Referential Integrity
  1. C INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, book_id, price_at_time) VALUES (50, 3, 11.99);: Error 1452 is about a child row: order_items.order_id = 50 points at an order that does not exist. Deleting a referenced parent is error 1451.
  2. A They are deleted too: fk_items_order says ON DELETE CASCADE, so the order's rows in order_items are deleted with it.
  3. B SET NULL cannot work on a NOT NULL column: SET NULL would write NULL into book_id, which is declared NOT NULL. MySQL reports error 1830 when the key is created.
  4. A It refuses, like RESTRICT: The default is NO ACTION, which InnoDB checks immediately, exactly like RESTRICT: the delete is refused with error 1451.
  5. C Nothing: they may contain orphans until you check with a query: Rows inserted while the checks were off were never checked, and turning them back on does not re-check. Run an orphan query per key to be sure.

Exit Ticket

  1. Where the key goes: one-to-many: in the many side's table (books.author_id); one-to-one: a foreign key that is also UNIQUE, usually in the optional table (customer_profiles.customer_id); many-to-many: a junction table with two foreign keys and a composite primary key (book_categories).
  2. 1452 vs 1451: 1452 = you were inserting or updating a CHILD and its parent does not exist. 1451 = you were deleting or changing the key of a PARENT that children still point at, under RESTRICT or NO ACTION.
  3. order_items.book_id → books: RESTRICT (the bookstore's choice): sold books stay on file for the sales history; mark them out of stock. Accept SET NULL only with a reason that the history does not need the book (and note the column is NOT NULL today); CASCADE is a red flag (it deletes sales).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A (customers.address_id). Puts the key on the one side: the most common design error. The question "can one A have many Bs?" fixes it; ask it every time.
  • Q4 D (error 1062). Expects the database to catch a spelling difference. It cannot: that is exactly why anomalies are dangerous.
  • Q5 B (still one-to-one). Thinks the foreign key itself limits references. A key checks existence; UNIQUE limits count.
  • Q7 A (1451). Mixes up parent and child. Reteach with the first words of each message.
  • Q8 B or C. B: expects the lines to stay as orphans; C: expects every delete with children to be refused. Look up the rule before predicting.
  • Q10 A or B. Choosing a rule without asking whether the child can exist alone. The two-question diagram is the remedy.
  • Exit ticket 3 with CASCADE. "It makes deleting easy." Ask what the sales report says next year about a book that was deleted.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 7: Joins and the Part 1 Assessment

Answers and reasoning for every Session 7 item: the warm-up, the 4.3 and Part 1 review self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs and the debug handout, the Part 1 assessment's reference run, the lesson page's own quiz and the exit ticket.

Session 7 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up

W1
One order has many lines. Where does the foreign key go?
order_items.order_id: the key goes on the many side.
W2
Predict the count. (Order 3 has one line; fk_items_order is ON DELETE CASCADE.)
START TRANSACTION;
DELETE FROM orders WHERE id = 3;
SELECT COUNT(*) AS items FROM order_items;
ROLLBACK;
17: order 3's one line was deleted with it (CASCADE); ROLLBACK put both back.
+-------+
| items |
+-------+
|    17 |
+-------+
W3
What happens, and why?
DELETE FROM authors WHERE id = 6;
Error 1451: Tolkien has books, and fk_books_author is ON DELETE RESTRICT ("delete or update a parent row").
ERROR 1451 (23000): Cannot delete or update a parent row: a foreign key constraint fails (`bookstore`.`books`, CONSTRAINT `fk_books_author` FOREIGN KEY (`author_id`) REFERENCES `authors` (`id`) ON DELETE RESTRICT ON UPDATE CASCADE)
W4
A key is declared with no ON DELETE. What does MySQL do when a parent with children is deleted?
It refuses: with no rule written the key is NO ACTION, which MySQL enforces exactly like RESTRICT.
W5
How many links?
SELECT COUNT(*) AS links
FROM book_categories
WHERE book_id IN (SELECT id FROM books WHERE author_id = 6);
4: The Hobbit and Lord of the Rings are each Fiction and Fantasy.
+-------+
| links |
+-------+
|     4 |
+-------+

4.3 · Joins: self-check

Q1
Eight customers placed 12 orders; Carol and Hank placed none. How many rows does this return?
SELECT c.first_name, o.id
FROM customers AS c
LEFT JOIN orders AS o ON o.customer_id = c.id;
A) 8
B) 12
C) 14
D) 96
Why: A LEFT JOIN returns every matching pair (12 orders, each with its customer) plus one row for each customer with no partner (Carol, Hank), padded with NULL: 14.
A is wrong: One row per customer would need GROUP BY; Alice alone has three orders.
B is wrong: 12 is the inner join: it drops Carol and Hank.
D is wrong: 96 (8 × 12) is a cross join: a join without its ON condition.
Q2
Which query lists the books that have never been sold?
A) FROM books b LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.book_id = b.id WHERE oi.id IS NULL
B) FROM books b JOIN order_items oi ON oi.book_id = b.id WHERE oi.id IS NULL
C) FROM books b LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.book_id = b.id WHERE b.id IS NULL
D) FROM order_items oi LEFT JOIN books b ON oi.book_id = b.id WHERE b.id IS NULL
Why: The anti-join: keep every book (LEFT JOIN from books), then keep only the rows where the right side found nothing (its primary key is NULL). The answer is The Catcher in the Rye and The Great Gatsby.
B is wrong: An inner join has no unmatched rows, so the IS NULL test keeps nothing.
C is wrong: b.id is never NULL: every row starts from a real book.
D is wrong: This keeps order lines whose book is missing; the foreign key makes that impossible, so it returns nothing.
Q3
Alice (customer 1) placed three orders. What does this print?
SELECT COUNT(*) AS rows_returned
FROM orders AS o
JOIN order_items AS oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE o.customer_id = 1;
4 (one row per order line, not per order)
Why: Joining order_items makes one row per order LINE: order 1 has two lines, orders 2 and 8 one each. Ask "what does one row of this join stand for?" before counting (COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) gives 3).
Q4
What happens when this runs?
SELECT id, order_date, first_name
FROM orders
JOIN customers ON orders.customer_id = customers.id;
A) It returns 12 rows, using the order's id
B) ERROR 1052 (23000): Column 'id' in field list is ambiguous
C) ERROR 1054 (42S22): Unknown column 'id' in 'field list'
D) It returns 96 rows: a cross join
Why: Both orders and customers have an id, and MySQL will not guess which one you mean. Qualify it: orders.id (or o.id with an alias). first_name and order_date exist in only one table each, so they are fine.
A is wrong: MySQL never guesses between two tables' columns.
C is wrong: 1054 is a column that exists nowhere (or a table name after an alias); here it exists twice.
D is wrong: The ON clause is there: this is an inner join.
Q5
Carol (3) has no orders; Dave (4) has two. What does this print?
SELECT c.first_name, COUNT(o.id) AS orders
FROM customers AS c
LEFT JOIN orders AS o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE c.id IN (3, 4)
GROUP BY c.id, c.first_name
ORDER BY c.id;
Carol | 0 and Dave | 2
Why: The LEFT JOIN gives Carol one row padded with NULL. COUNT(o.id) counts only the non-NULL order ids, so Carol gets 0. COUNT(*) would have counted her padded row and said 1 (the lesson's Mistake 4).
Q6
This was meant to list every author with their in-stock books. Isaac Asimov (no books) and the authors of the two out-of-stock books are missing. Why?
SELECT a.name, b.title
FROM authors AS a
LEFT JOIN books AS b ON b.author_id = a.id
WHERE b.in_stock = TRUE
ORDER BY a.id, b.title;
A) LEFT JOIN cannot be combined with WHERE
B) in_stock is a BOOLEAN, so TRUE must be written as 1
C) The ORDER BY removes the NULL rows
D) The WHERE runs after the join: for the padded rows b.in_stock is NULL, so the LEFT JOIN behaves like an inner join
Why: A condition on the right table belongs in ON when every left row must stay: LEFT JOIN books b ON b.author_id = a.id AND b.in_stock = TRUE. Then Asimov, Fitzgerald and Salinger appear with NULL titles (the lesson's Mistake 3).
A is wrong: WHERE is fine after a LEFT JOIN; it just filters the joined rows, NULL-padded ones included.
B is wrong: TRUE and 1 are the same in MySQL.
C is wrong: ORDER BY only sorts.

4.3 · Joins: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT COUNT(*) AS rows_returned
FROM customers AS c
LEFT JOIN orders AS o ON o.customer_id = c.id;
+---------------+
| rows_returned |
+---------------+
|            14 |
+---------------+
Why: Twelve order rows (each with its customer) plus Carol and Hank with NULLs: 14.
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT c.first_name, COUNT(*) AS n
FROM customers AS c
LEFT JOIN orders AS o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE c.id IN (3, 6)
GROUP BY c.id, c.first_name
ORDER BY c.id;
+------------+---+
| first_name | n |
+------------+---+
| Carol      | 1 |
| Frank      | 2 |
+------------+---+
Why: Carol has no orders, but the LEFT JOIN gives her one padded row, and COUNT(*) counts rows: 1 (COUNT(o.id) would say 0). Frank has two orders: 2.

Part 1 Review: self-check

Q7
Review (Lesson 2.2): which column type fits a book's price?
A) DECIMAL(8, 2)
B) FLOAT
C) VARCHAR(10)
D) INT
Why: DECIMAL stores exact decimal values: right for money. The course stores every price as DECIMAL(8, 2).
B is wrong: FLOAT is approximate: sums of prices can be off by fractions of a cent.
C is wrong: Text accepts "about 26" and cannot be summed or compared as a number (Session 1's order sheet).
D is wrong: INT has no cents (unless you store cents, a different design).
Q8
Review (Lessons 2.2 and 2.3): what happens?
INSERT INTO customers (first_name, last_name, email)
VALUES ('Ivy', 'Lane', 'alice@example.com');
ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry 'alice@example.com' for key 'customers.email'
Why: customers.email is UNIQUE and alice@example.com belongs to Alice: the INSERT is refused and nothing is added. UNIQUE is a constraint on a column, like the PRIMARY KEY on id.
Q9
Review (Lesson 3.1): how many books does this count?
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM books WHERE title LIKE 'The %';
A) 2
B) 3
C) 4
D) 11
Why: 'The %' means "starts with The and a space": The Great Gatsby, The Hobbit, The Catcher in the Rye. LIKE ignores case with the course's collation.
A is wrong: Count again: three titles start with "The ".
C is wrong: To Kill a Mockingbird starts with "To", not "The".
D is wrong: 11 is every book; the pattern is a real filter.
Q10
Review (Lessons 3.2 and 3.3): in which order must these clauses be written?
A) SELECT, FROM, GROUP BY, WHERE, HAVING, ORDER BY, LIMIT
B) SELECT, FROM, WHERE, ORDER BY, GROUP BY, HAVING, LIMIT
C) SELECT, FROM, WHERE, HAVING, GROUP BY, ORDER BY, LIMIT
D) SELECT, FROM, WHERE, GROUP BY, HAVING, ORDER BY, LIMIT
Why: The written order (Some Frogs Will Gladly Hop Over Logs). The run order differs: FROM, WHERE, GROUP BY, HAVING, SELECT, ORDER BY, LIMIT; with a join, the JOINs belong to FROM.
A is wrong: WHERE comes before GROUP BY: it picks rows before the piles are made.
B is wrong: ORDER BY comes after grouping (it sorts the result).
C is wrong: HAVING after GROUP BY: written the other way it is error 1064.
Q11
Review (Lesson 3.4): what does this print?
SELECT title
FROM books
WHERE price = (SELECT MIN(price) FROM books);
Pride and Prejudice (one row)
Why: The inner query finds the lowest price (7.99); the outer query returns the row(s) with it. If two books tied, both would come back, unlike ORDER BY price LIMIT 1.
Q12
Review (Lessons 4.1 and 4.2): what is the bookstore's order_items table?
A) A one-to-one extension of orders
B) A copy of books with a quantity column
C) A junction table linking orders and books (many-to-many), carrying facts about each link: quantity and price_at_time
D) A self-referencing table
Why: An order holds many books and a book appears in many orders: two foreign keys in a third table. Its extra columns describe the link (this book in this order). Deleting an order cascades to its lines; deleting a sold book is RESTRICTed.
A is wrong: An order has MANY lines.
B is wrong: It stores book_id, not a copy of the book; price_at_time is history, not duplication.
D is wrong: That is employees (manager_id points at employees).

(2) DISTINCT, because Bob and Grace both bought 1984. (3) The WHERE-versus-ON trap on a new question: as shipped the query shows six customers (Carol and Hank vanish); with the date in ON, eight.

Handout: labs/module-07/l4_3-joins/debug_joins.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints (Bug D's table comes first because it is the only statement that runs):

ERROR 1052 (23000): Column 'id' in field list is ambiguous
ERROR 1054 (42S22): Unknown column 'books.title' in 'field list'
ERROR 1066 (42000): Not unique table/alias: 'employees'
+------------+--------+
| first_name | orders |
+------------+--------+
| Alice      |      4 |
| Bob        |      3 |
+------------+--------+
  1. Bug A, 1052: id exists in orders and customers. Fix: orders.id (or aliases).
  2. Bug B, 1054: books has the alias b, so books.title refers to nothing. Fix: b.title.
  3. Bug C, 1066: employees joined to itself without aliases. Fix: employees AS e JOIN employees AS m ON e.manager_id = m.id.
  4. Bug D (silent): Alice gets 4 and Bob 3: the join to order_items makes one row per order LINE. Fix: COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) (3 and 2), or drop the order_items join.

Fixed (first rows only for A and C):

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 4.3 · Joins
  1. A 11: An inner join returns one row per matching pair. Every book has exactly one author, so there are 11 pairs, and Asimov, with no partner, is left out.
  2. C FROM customers c LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id WHERE o.id IS NULL: The LEFT JOIN keeps every customer and fills the order columns with NULL when there is none; WHERE o.id IS NULL keeps exactly those customers.
  3. B The join gave him one row of NULLs, and COUNT(*) counts rows: The LEFT JOIN gives Asimov one row padded with NULLs, and COUNT(*) counts rows. COUNT(b.id) counts only non-NULL book ids and returns 0.
  4. B Tom, Lena and Ben, each with Priya as manager: It is a self-join: m plays the manager. The rows where the manager is Priya are her three reports, Tom, Lena and Ben (Ben is inactive, but nothing filters on that).
  5. A In the ON clause, after the key condition: In the ON clause the condition only decides which orders match, so customers without a May order still appear with NULLs. In WHERE it would remove them, turning the LEFT JOIN into an inner join.

Exit Ticket

  1. JOIN vs LEFT JOIN: JOIN returns one row per customer-order pair (12 rows; Carol and Hank, who never ordered, are not there). LEFT JOIN also keeps every customer without an order, once, with NULL in the order columns (14 rows).
  2. Hardest task: a reflection. Tally by task: many A1 answers point to Lesson 2.2 and 4.2 (constraints, keys); B4 to the NULL rule (3.3); B7 and B8 to the anti-join and LEFT JOIN with GROUP BY (4.3). If Part 2 follows, open Session 8's refresher with the most frequent one.
  3. Use and practice: a reflection; read them for the course evaluation.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 B (12). Thinks LEFT JOIN returns the same rows as JOIN when "every order has a customer". It is the customers without orders that LEFT JOIN adds.
  • Q2 B. Uses IS NULL after an inner join: has the pattern's words but not its logic. Draw the NULL-padded rows.
  • Q3 3. Counts orders, not order lines: the fan-out habit is not there yet. The board question is the fix.
  • Q6 B or C. Looks for a syntax cause of a logic bug. Run it without the WHERE and watch the NULL rows appear.
  • Q10 A or C. The written order is still shaky; HAVING before GROUP BY is a 1064 they will meet again.
  • Q12 A or B. Has not connected order_items with junction tables. Session 6's relationship map is the remedy.

Session 8 12 self-check items Confidential

Start Here · Part 1 Refresher

Answers to W1 to W5 (not graded). W1 and W2 with MySQL's real output on the freshly loaded bookstore:

W1
Which customers have never placed an order? Say the query shape, then the names.
A LEFT JOIN from customers to orders, keeping the customers whose orders side is NULL (or HAVING COUNT(o.id) = 0): Carol and Hank. Lesson 4.3, the anti-join.
+------------+--------+
| first_name | orders |
+------------+--------+
| Carol      |      0 |
| Hank       |      0 |
+------------+--------+
W2
employees has a manager_id. How do you show each employee next to their manager's name? Who is Sofia's manager?
A self-join: the table twice with two aliases, ON m.id = e.manager_id. Sofia (id 7) reports to Marcus.
+----------+---------+
| employee | manager |
+----------+---------+
| Sofia    | Marcus  |
+----------+---------+
W3
SELECT a.name, COUNT(*) FROM authors a LEFT JOIN books b ON b.author_id = a.id GROUP BY a.id, a.name: what does it say for Isaac Asimov, who has no books, and how do you fix it?
1, because COUNT(*) counts the NULL-padded row the LEFT JOIN made. Count a column of the right table, COUNT(b.id), which skips NULL: 0. (Lesson 4.3, Common Mistake 4.)
W4
Books per author and copies sold in one query: Orwell shows 5 books, but he wrote 2. What happened?
Fan-out: joining order_items repeats each book once per order line. COUNT(DISTINCT b.id) fixes it. Ask what one row of the join stands for. (Lesson 4.3, Common Mistake 5.)
W5
A subquery in FROM (a derived table) fails with error 1248. What is missing?
An alias: every derived table needs its own name, ( ... ) AS t (Lesson 3.4). Today's CTEs give such steps a name up front, and read top to bottom.

5.1 · CTEs & Recursive Queries: self-check

Q1
A statement starts WITH monthly AS (...). How long does monthly exist?
A) Until you disconnect
B) Only while that one statement runs
C) Until you DROP it
D) Until the next COMMIT
Why: A CTE is a named result that belongs to the one statement it is written in. After the semicolon it is gone: a second SELECT * FROM monthly; answers 1146 (the lesson's Common Mistake 4).
A is wrong: That is a user variable (@name) or a temporary table.
C is wrong: Only stored objects (tables, views) are dropped; a CTE is never stored.
D is wrong: Transactions have nothing to do with a CTE's lifetime.
Q2
What does this print?
WITH RECURSIVE s AS (
    SELECT 2 AS n
    UNION ALL
    SELECT n * 2 FROM s WHERE n < 20
)
SELECT GROUP_CONCAT(n ORDER BY n) AS series FROM s;
2,4,8,16,32 (one row, column series)
Why: Each round doubles the row the previous round made: 2, 4, 8, 16. The round that reads 16 still passes n < 20 and makes 32; the round that reads 32 fails the test, makes no row, and the recursion stops. The stop condition tests the row a round reads, so the last row can be past the limit.
Q3
A CTE named org joins to itself, but the statement starts WITH org AS ( without RECURSIVE. What happens?
A) ERROR 1146 (42S02): Table 'bookstore.org' doesn't exist
B) ERROR 3636 (HY000): Recursive query aborted after 1001 iterations
C) It runs, but only the anchor's rows come back
D) ERROR 1406 (22001): Data too long for column
Why: Without RECURSIVE a CTE cannot see its own name, so the JOIN inside it looks for a real table called org (the live demo's last step, and the debug handout's query A).
B is wrong: 3636 needs a working recursion that never stops.
C is wrong: MySQL does not quietly drop the recursive member; it refuses the statement.
D is wrong: 1406 is a column too narrow for what the recursive member builds.
Q4
A recursive CTE named chain starts from one employee. Which condition in the recursive member walks UP to the owner?
A) JOIN chain c ON e.manager_id = c.id
B) JOIN chain c ON e.id = c.id
C) JOIN chain c ON e.id = c.manager_id
D) JOIN chain c ON e.manager_id = c.manager_id
Why: "Who is this person's manager?": the next row is the employee whose id is the manager_id of the row found last. That is how the demo climbs from Sofia to Marcus, Daniel and Maria.
A is wrong: That walks DOWN: "whose manager is this person?" (the org chart).
B is wrong: It finds the same person again every round: the recursion would never move.
D is wrong: It finds colleagues with the same manager, not the manager.
Q5
The bookstore has 10 employees. This query prints 30. What is wrong?
WITH RECURSIVE org AS (
    SELECT id, 1 AS level FROM employees
    UNION ALL
    SELECT e.id, o.level + 1
    FROM employees e JOIN org o ON e.manager_id = o.id
)
SELECT COUNT(*) AS rows_back FROM org;
A) RECURSIVE is missing
B) UNION ALL should be UNION
C) The recursion limit is too low
D) The anchor has no WHERE, so every employee starts a walk of their own
Why: With no WHERE manager_id IS NULL in the anchor, all ten employees are starting points, and each deeper person is found again under every manager above them. No error: the lesson's silent Common Mistake 5. Check with COUNT(*) against COUNT(DISTINCT id).
A is wrong: RECURSIVE is there, and without it the query would fail with 1146.
B is wrong: UNION would hide some duplicates and the wrong anchor with them: fix the anchor instead.
C is wrong: The limit is 1000 rounds; this walk needs four.
Q6
In one sentence: why does a monthly report LEFT JOIN a generated calendar of months to the orders, instead of grouping the orders by month directly?
GROUP BY over orders can only show months that have orders; the recursive calendar has every month, and the LEFT JOIN keeps the empty ones (July shows 0)
Why: Real data has holes. The series comes first, then the data is hung on it; COUNT(o.id) counts 0 for a month with no matching order.

5.1 · CTEs & Recursive Queries: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
WITH RECURSIVE countdown AS (
    SELECT 10 AS n
    UNION ALL
    SELECT n - 3 FROM countdown WHERE n > 3
)
SELECT n FROM countdown;
+------+
| n    |
+------+
|   10 |
|    7 |
|    4 |
|    1 |
+------+
Why: Anchor 10; each round subtracts 3 while the row it reads is above 3: 7, 4, 1. The round that reads 1 fails n > 3 and stops.
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
WITH by_city AS (
    SELECT city, COUNT(*) AS customers
    FROM customers
    GROUP BY city
)
SELECT COUNT(*) AS city_groups, SUM(customers) AS customers
FROM by_city;
+-------------+-----------+
| city_groups | customers |
+-------------+-----------+
|           5 |         8 |
+-------------+-----------+
Why: GROUP BY city makes one group per city, and the two customers with no city (Eve, Hank) form one more group: NULL. Five groups, eight customers. (The column is not called groups: GROUPS is a reserved word in MySQL 8 and later, error 1064.)

5.2 · Window Functions: self-check

Q7
employees has 10 rows in 4 departments. How many rows does this return? SELECT first_name, AVG(salary) OVER (PARTITION BY department) FROM employees;
A) 4
B) 1
C) 10
D) None: ERROR 1140 (a column without GROUP BY)
Why: A window function never collapses rows: every employee comes back, each with their own department's average. GROUP BY department would return 4.
A is wrong: That is GROUP BY department.
B is wrong: That is AVG(salary) without OVER.
D is wrong: OVER makes the mix of a plain column and an aggregate legal.
Q8
Copies sold, best first, are 5, 4, 4 and 3. What do RANK() and DENSE_RANK() (both OVER (ORDER BY copies DESC)) give the book with 3?
RANK 4, DENSE_RANK 3
Why: RANK skips after a tie (1, 2, 2, 4: three books are ahead); DENSE_RANK does not (1, 2, 2, 3). ROW_NUMBER would give 4 too, but it would also give the tied books 2 and 3.
Q9
SELECT title FROM books WHERE RANK() OVER (ORDER BY price DESC) <= 3; fails with error 3593. What is the standard fix?
A) Move the condition to HAVING
B) Compute the rank in a CTE, then filter on it in the outer query
C) Replace WHERE with QUALIFY
D) Give the rank a column alias and filter on the alias in WHERE
Why: Window functions run after WHERE, GROUP BY and HAVING, so none of those can see them. One step computes the window, the next filters it: a CTE (or a derived table) is that next step.
A is wrong: HAVING runs before the windows too (3593 again).
C is wrong: QUALIFY is error 6037 on MySQL 9.7 (hypergraph optimizer only) and 1064 on MariaDB.
D is wrong: WHERE cannot see SELECT aliases: error 1054, Unknown column.
Q10
In SUM(price) OVER (ORDER BY price), The Catcher in the Rye and The Great Gatsby (both 8.99) show the same running total, 34.46. Why?
A) SUM skips duplicate values
B) Prices are sorted as text
C) The window has no PARTITION BY
D) The default frame is RANGE: rows with the same ORDER BY value are added in one step
Why: With ORDER BY and no frame, the frame is RANGE BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW, which lumps ties together. Write ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW and make the order unique (ORDER BY price, title): 25.47 then 34.46.
A is wrong: Both prices are added (34.46 includes both); the problem is when.
B is wrong: price is DECIMAL; the order is numeric.
C is wrong: PARTITION BY would restart the total per group; it does not change how ties are handled.
Q11
Bob (customer 2) has two orders. What does this print?
SELECT id,
       order_date,
       LEAD(order_date) OVER (ORDER BY order_date) AS next_order
FROM orders
WHERE customer_id = 2
ORDER BY order_date;
Two rows: order 3, 2026-03-10, next_order 2026-03-28; order 7, 2026-03-28, next_order NULL
Why: LEAD reads the next row of the window. Order 7 is Bob's last, so there is no next row and LEAD returns NULL (a third argument, LEAD(order_date, 1, ...), would replace it).
Q12
You need exactly one top earner per department, even if two salaries ever tie. Which function numbers the rows?
A) ROW_NUMBER() with a tie-breaker, e.g. ORDER BY salary DESC, id
B) RANK()
C) DENSE_RANK()
D) NTILE(1)
Why: ROW_NUMBER never ties, so pos = 1 is exactly one row per partition; the tie-breaker makes the choice repeatable. RANK and DENSE_RANK would return both tied people.
B is wrong: Two tied top salaries both get rank 1.
C is wrong: Same: both get 1.
D is wrong: NTILE(1) puts every row in bucket 1.

5.2 · Window Functions: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT first_name,
       COUNT(*) OVER ()                        AS everyone,
       COUNT(*) OVER (PARTITION BY department) AS in_dept
FROM employees
WHERE department = 'support'
ORDER BY first_name;
+------------+----------+---------+
| first_name | everyone | in_dept |
+------------+----------+---------+
| James      |        2 |       2 |
| Nina       |        2 |       2 |
+------------+----------+---------+
Why: WHERE keeps only the two support employees BEFORE the windows are computed, so even OVER () sees two rows. Windows never see rows WHERE removed.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT id,
       LAG(id, 1, 0) OVER (ORDER BY id) AS previous_id
FROM orders
WHERE customer_id = 1
ORDER BY id;
+----+-------------+
| id | previous_id |
+----+-------------+
|  1 |           0 |
|  2 |           1 |
|  8 |           2 |
+----+-------------+
Why: LAG's third argument replaces the NULL on the first row: 0. Alice's orders are 1, 2 and 8, so the previous ids are 0, 1, 2.

Lab 5.1-A: labs/module-08/l5_1-ctes/staff_tree_starter.sql → staff_tree_solution.sql

The solution prints:

+-----------+--------+

+-----------+--------+
|         1 |      5 |
|         2 |      5 |
|         3 |      3 |
|         4 |      3 |
|         5 |      3 |
|         6 |      2 |
|         7 |      0 |
|         8 |      0 |
|         9 |      0 |
|        10 |      0 |
|        11 |      0 |
|        12 |      0 |
+-----------+--------+
+-------+------------+----------+
| level | first_name | division |
+-------+------------+----------+
|     1 | Maria      | (owner)  |
|     2 | Daniel     | Daniel   |
|     3 | Marcus     | Daniel   |
|     3 | Priya      | Daniel   |
|     4 | Ben        | Daniel   |
|     4 | Lena       | Daniel   |
|     4 | Sofia      | Daniel   |
|     4 | Tom        | Daniel   |

+----------+--------+-----------+

| James    |      2 |  88500.00 |
+----------+--------+-----------+

Reading it: the month series has 12 rows because the round that reads 12 fails month_num < 12; months 7 to 12 have no orders, and the LEFT JOINs keep them with COALESCE turning NULL into 0. In the tree, the anchor's CAST(NULL AS CHAR(50)) makes the division column wide enough (a bare NULL would make a useless column type); level 2 starts a division with COALESCE, everyone below copies it. The aggregate runs in the main query after the recursion: Daniel's division is everyone under him plus himself (7 people), James's is 2; Maria (the owner) has no division and is filtered out.

Handout: labs/module-08/l5_1-ctes/debug_ctes.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints:

ERROR 1146 (42S02): Table 'bookstore.countdown' doesn't exist
ERROR 3636 (HY000): Recursive query aborted after 1001 iterations. Try increasing @@cte_max_recursion_depth to a larger value.
ERROR 1406 (22001): Data too long for column 'path' at row 1
  1. A, 1146: the CTE names itself without RECURSIVE. Fix: WITH RECURSIVE countdown AS (...): 3, 2, 1.
  2. B, 3636: nothing stops the series. Fix: SELECT d + INTERVAL 1 DAY FROM days WHERE d < '2026-12-31': 365.
  3. C, 1406: the anchor's literal 'Maria' fixed a five-character column, and Maria > Daniel does not fit. Fix: CAST('Maria' AS CHAR(200)) or, better, CAST(first_name AS CHAR(200)).

The checker runs these in a scratch database, so its 1146 message names that database instead of bookstore. On MariaDB, query B does not fail: it returns 1001 with warning 1931.

Handout: labs/module-08/l5_2-window-functions/debug_windows.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints:

ERROR 3593 (HY000): You cannot use the window function 'row_number' in this context.'
ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near 'rank
FROM books
ORDER BY price DESC
LIMIT 3' at line 1
ERROR 6037 (HY000): 'QUALIFY clause' can be used only if the hypergraph optimizer is enabled.
  1. A, 3593: a window function in WHERE. Fix: WITH priced AS (SELECT title, price, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY price DESC) AS pos FROM books) SELECT title, price FROM priced WHERE pos <= 3 ORDER BY pos; (Lord of the Rings, Dune, To Kill a Mockingbird).
  2. B, 1064: rank is a reserved word. Fix: AS rank_num (or backticks).
  3. C, 6037: QUALIFY runs only under the hypergraph optimizer. Fix: the CTE pattern from A.

On MariaDB: A is error 4015, B runs (MariaDB still accepts AS rank), C is 1064.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 5.1 · CTEs & Recursive Queries
  1. B Error 1146: the table does not exist: A CTE exists only inside the statement it is written in. After the semicolon it is gone, so MySQL looks for a real table named monthly and reports error 1146.
  2. A The anchor, before UNION ALL: The anchor, the query before UNION ALL, produces the starting rows once. The recursive member then runs round after round until a round produces no rows.
  3. C 4: The anchor gives 1, then the rounds give 2, 3 and 4. The round that reads 4 fails n less than 4, produces nothing, and the recursion stops: 1, 2, 3, 4.
  4. B Widen the column in the anchor, for example CAST('Maria' AS CHAR(200)): The anchor fixes each column's type and width, and the recursive member can never widen it. CAST('Maria' AS CHAR(200)) in the anchor gives the path room to grow.
  5. C In a view, stored once in the database: A view is stored in the database, so every script and every person can read it by name. CTEs are best for steps that belong to one query.
Lesson 5.2 · Window Functions
  1. C 11: A window function never collapses rows. OVER () makes the whole result the window, so each of the 11 books gets the same average next to its title.
  2. A 4: RANK gives the two books with 3 copies the same rank, 2, then skips: three books are ahead of the one with 2 copies, so it gets 4. DENSE_RANK would give 3.
  3. B Compute the row number in a CTE and filter it in the outer query: Window functions are computed after WHERE, GROUP BY and HAVING. Compute ROW_NUMBER in a CTE, then filter its result with WHERE in the outer query.
  4. C NULL: January is the first row of the window, so there is no previous row and LAG returns NULL. LAG(revenue, 1, 0) would return 0 instead.
  5. A 1: The frame asks for two rows before January, but there are none, so the frame holds only January itself. February averages two months, and from March on every row averages three.

Exit Ticket

  1. Recursive skeleton: WITH RECURSIVE s AS (SELECT 1 AS n UNION ALL SELECT n + 1 FROM s WHERE n < 10) SELECT n FROM s; Anchor = SELECT 1 AS n (runs once); recursive member = SELECT n + 1 FROM s ... (runs per round); stop condition = WHERE n < 10. Accept <= 9; reject a stop condition in the outer SELECT (it would not stop the recursion).
  2. GROUP BY vs PARTITION BY: one row per department versus every employee row with the department's figure added. Accept "collapses" versus "keeps the rows".
  3. WHERE and windows: windows are computed after WHERE (and GROUP BY, HAVING), so WHERE cannot see them (3593); compute the window in a CTE (or derived table) and filter in the outer query.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A ("until you disconnect"). Mixes a CTE with a user variable or a temporary table. Show the 1146 from the demo's step 5 again.
  • Q2 2,4,8,16. Reads the stop condition as a filter on the output. Draw the rounds: the round that reads 16 still makes 32.
  • Q4 A. Walks down instead of up: the most common hierarchy slip. Say the two questions aloud: "whose manager is this?" (down) versus "who is this person's manager?" (up).
  • Q7 A (4 rows). Still thinks of grouping. Run it: ten rows.
  • Q8 "3 and 3" or "4 and 4". Mixed up which function skips. The board table: 1, 2, 2, 4 / 1, 2, 2, 3.
  • Q9 A (HAVING). HAVING runs before windows too; the CTE is the only portable fix.
  • Exit ticket 1 with the WHERE in the outer SELECT. The recursion has no stop condition at all (3636). Worth two minutes at the start of Session 9.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 9: Indexes and Views

Answers and reasoning for every Session 9 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs, both debug handouts, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 9 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up

Answers to W1 to W5, with MySQL's real output where the question shows code:

W1
What does this print?
WITH RECURSIVE s AS (
    SELECT 1 AS n UNION ALL SELECT n + 2 FROM s WHERE n < 7
)
SELECT GROUP_CONCAT(n ORDER BY n) AS odd FROM s;
1,3,5,7: the round that reads 7 fails n < 7 and stops.
+---------+
| odd     |
+---------+
| 1,3,5,7 |
+---------+
W2
Which price_level do The Catcher in the Rye and The Great Gatsby (both 8.99) get, and Animal Farm after them?
SELECT title, price,
       DENSE_RANK() OVER (ORDER BY price DESC) AS price_level
FROM books
WHERE price < 9.50
ORDER BY price DESC, title;
Both 2, Animal Farm 3: DENSE_RANK ties without a gap (RANK would give Animal Farm 4).
+------------------------+-------+-------------+
| title                  | price | price_level |
+------------------------+-------+-------------+
| Brave New World        |  9.49 |           1 |
| The Catcher in the Rye |  8.99 |           2 |
| The Great Gatsby       |  8.99 |           2 |
| Animal Farm            |  8.49 |           3 |
| Pride and Prejudice    |  7.99 |           4 |
+------------------------+-------+-------------+
W3
Why can't WHERE filter on ROW_NUMBER(), and what do you write instead?
Windows are computed after WHERE (3593): number the rows in a CTE, filter in the outer query.
W4
In a recursive CTE over employees, which join walks UP the hierarchy?
e.id = cte.manager_id ("who is this person's manager?"); e.manager_id = cte.id walks down.
W5
AVG(salary) with GROUP BY department versus AVG(salary) OVER (PARTITION BY department): how many rows from the 10 employees?
4 versus 10: GROUP BY collapses, OVER keeps every row.

6.1 · Indexes & Query Optimization: self-check

Q1
Which index does every InnoDB table have, even though you never created one by name?
A) One index per column
B) The primary key: the rows themselves are stored in its order (the clustered index)
C) An index on every VARCHAR column
D) None: MySQL scans every table until you add indexes
Why: InnoDB stores the rows in a B-tree sorted by the primary key, so WHERE id = ... is always fast. UNIQUE constraints and foreign keys add more indexes automatically (email, fk_orders_customer ...).
A is wrong: Only the columns you (or a constraint) choose are indexed.
C is wrong: Types do not decide it; constraints and CREATE INDEX do.
D is wrong: The primary key is always there.
Q2
sales_log has an index on (customer_id, sold_on) and no other index. Which query can NOT seek with it?
A) WHERE customer_id = 3
B) WHERE customer_id = 3 AND sold_on >= '2025-03-01'
C) WHERE sold_on >= '2025-03-01' AND customer_id = 3
D) WHERE sold_on BETWEEN '2025-03-01' AND '2025-03-31'
Why: A composite index serves its leftmost prefix. The dates are sorted only within each customer, so a date range alone has no place to jump to (at best a skip scan). The order of the conditions in WHERE does not matter, which is why C works as well as B.
A is wrong: customer_id is the first column: a seek.
B is wrong: Both columns, in index order: the best case.
C is wrong: Same as B: MySQL does not care about the order of the AND.
Q3
sold_on has an index. Rewrite WHERE YEAR(sold_on) = 2024 so MySQL can use it.
WHERE sold_on >= '2024-01-01' AND sold_on < '2025-01-01'
Why: The index is sorted by the column, not by YEAR(column). A range on the bare column (up to, but not including, the next January 1) gives the same rows and stays correct if the column ever becomes DATETIME.
Q4
receipt_no is CHAR(7) with a unique index, yet WHERE receipt_no = 4242 shows Table scan. Why?
A) The text column is converted to a number on every row, so the index order no longer helps
B) Unique indexes cannot be used for equality
C) The table needs ANALYZE TABLE first
D) 4242 is too small: receipt numbers start at 1000000
Why: A string column compared with a number is converted row by row ('0004242' becomes 4242), and converted values are not in the index's sort order. WHERE receipt_no = '0004242' is a single-row lookup again. SHOW WARNINGS after the EXPLAIN even says so (warning 1739).
B is wrong: Equality on a unique index is the best case: Rows fetched before execution.
C is wrong: Fresh statistics do not change a type conversion.
D is wrong: The value matches a real row (the count is 1); only the plan is slow.
Q5
An index idx_sales_customer_date (customer_id, sold_on) exists. You run CREATE INDEX idx_sales_customer ON sales_log (customer_id);. What happens?
A) ERROR 1061: Duplicate key name
B) MySQL refuses: the column is already indexed
C) It succeeds without an error or a warning, and the new index is pure overhead
D) It replaces the composite index
Why: The composite index already serves customer_id alone (its leftmost prefix), so the new one only slows every write. MySQL warns only when the columns are exactly the same (1831). Check SHOW INDEX before adding one.
A is wrong: 1061 is about the NAME, and this name is new.
B is wrong: MySQL lets you index a column many times.
D is wrong: Indexes are never replaced silently; both now exist.
Q6
DROP INDEX fk_orders_customer ON orders; fails. Which error number, and why does MySQL refuse?
1553: the foreign key from orders to customers needs an index on customer_id, and this is the only one
Why: InnoDB created fk_orders_customer for the foreign key. To replace it, create another index that starts with customer_id first; InnoDB then drops its own.

6.1 · Indexes & Query Optimization: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT INDEX_NAME, COLUMN_NAME
FROM information_schema.STATISTICS
WHERE TABLE_SCHEMA = 'bookstore' AND TABLE_NAME = 'categories'
ORDER BY INDEX_NAME;
+------------+-------------+
| INDEX_NAME | COLUMN_NAME |
+------------+-------------+
| name       | name        |
| PRIMARY    | id          |
+------------+-------------+
Why: Two indexes: the primary key and name, the unique index MySQL created for the UNIQUE constraint on categories.name (named after the column).
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM employees WHERE email = 'nina.kowalski@bookstore.example'\G
*************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Rows fetched before execution  (cost=0..0 rows=1)
Why: email is UNIQUE, so a constant comparison finds at most one row while MySQL is still planning: Rows fetched before execution, the same line as a primary-key lookup.

6.2 · Views: self-check

Q7
What does MySQL store when you create a view?
A) Only the query: the rows are computed every time the view is read
B) A copy of the rows, refreshed every night
C) A copy of the rows, refreshed on every INSERT
D) An index on the base tables
Why: A view is a named, saved SELECT (with its definer and security settings). Querying it runs the query against the base tables, so it is always current and exactly as fast as that query.
B is wrong: That is a summary table you refresh yourself; MySQL has no materialized views.
C is wrong: Views hold no rows at all.
D is wrong: Views have no indexes; the base tables' indexes do the work.
Q8
What happens when the second statement runs?
CREATE VIEW v_ny AS
SELECT id, first_name, city
FROM customers
WHERE city = 'New York'
WITH CHECK OPTION;
UPDATE v_ny SET city = 'Boston' WHERE id = 1;
ERROR 1369 (HY000): CHECK OPTION failed 'bookstore.v_ny'
Why: Alice would leave the view she was changed through. WITH CHECK OPTION rejects any write through the view that produces a row the view itself would not show; nothing is changed.
Q9
Which of these views accepts an UPDATE that passes through to its base table?
A) SELECT author_id, COUNT(*) AS books FROM books GROUP BY author_id
B) SELECT DISTINCT city FROM customers
C) SELECT id, title, price FROM books WHERE price < 10
D) SELECT title FROM books UNION SELECT name FROM authors
Why: Each row of C is exactly one row of books, so MySQL knows which row to change. Grouping, DISTINCT and UNION make one view row stand for several base rows (error 1288).
A is wrong: A count has no single base row behind it.
B is wrong: DISTINCT merges rows: not updatable (IS_UPDATABLE says NO).
D is wrong: UNION combines two tables' rows.
Q10
v_all was created as SELECT * FROM products. Then a column color is added to products. What does SELECT * FROM v_all return?
A) ERROR 1356
B) All columns, including color
C) An empty result until the view is refreshed
D) The old columns only, without color
Why: The * was expanded into a fixed column list when the view was created, and that list is what MySQL saved. List columns explicitly and replace the view when the table gains one it should show.
A is wrong: 1356 is for a renamed or dropped column the view still names.
B is wrong: The column list is frozen at creation.
C is wrong: Views are never "refreshed"; the rows are current, only the column list is fixed.
Q11
Why does the course put ORDER BY in the query that reads a view, and not inside the view?
MySQL ignores a view's ORDER BY when the outer query has its own, and the SQL standard promises no order from a view: sort where you read
Why: The lesson's "Performance and Limitations" section. The project's checklist says it too: no view contains an ORDER BY; every query that reads one does.
Q12
An account has SELECT on v_staff_directory only. The view uses the default SQL SECURITY DEFINER. What can the account do?
A) Read every column of employees, salaries included
B) Read the directory through the view, but not the employees table itself
C) Nothing: it has no privilege on employees
D) Update salaries through the view
Why: The view reads employees with its definer's rights, so it works for the account; SELECT on employees directly fails with 1142. The view is the security boundary: never select more columns than the audience should see.
A is wrong: Only the view's columns (no salary) are visible.
C is wrong: DEFINER security is exactly what makes the view work for it.
D is wrong: The grant is SELECT only, and the view has no salary column.

6.2 · Views: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE VIEW v_pricey AS SELECT title FROM books WHERE price > 12;
START TRANSACTION;
UPDATE books SET price = 9.00 WHERE id = 1;
SELECT title FROM v_pricey ORDER BY title;
ROLLBACK;
DROP VIEW v_pricey;
+-------------------+
| title             |
+-------------------+
| Lord of the Rings |
+-------------------+
Why: Only Lord of the Rings: Dune's price dropped to 9.00 inside the transaction, and the view runs its query against the current rows. After ROLLBACK the view shows Dune again; nothing needs refreshing.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE VIEW v_cities AS SELECT DISTINCT city FROM customers;
SELECT TABLE_NAME, IS_UPDATABLE
FROM information_schema.VIEWS
WHERE TABLE_SCHEMA = 'bookstore' AND TABLE_NAME = 'v_cities';
DROP VIEW v_cities;
+------------+--------------+
| TABLE_NAME | IS_UPDATABLE |
+------------+--------------+
| v_cities   | NO           |
+------------+--------------+
Why: NO: DISTINCT merges rows, so one view row can stand for several customers and MySQL cannot know which one to change.
