The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 6.1 · Indexes & Query Optimization
  1. B It returns the right rows after reading the whole table: Without an index MySQL reads every row and tests each one: a full table scan. The result is correct, it just takes longer as the table grows.
  2. A WHERE customer_id = 7 AND sold_on >= '2026-01-01': customer_id is the leftmost column, so MySQL can jump to customer 7, and inside it the dates are sorted, so the date range is a tight slice.
  3. C Every row is read and then filtered: no index is used: Table scan means every row is read and the Filter keeps the matches. On a big table that is the step to fix, with an index or a rewritten condition.
  4. C The index is sorted by the column, not by the function's result: The index is sorted by sold_on, not by YEAR(sold_on), so MySQL must compute the function for every row. A range on the bare column (from 2026-01-01 up to 2027-01-01) can use the index.
  5. A Make it INVISIBLE and watch the slow queries: An invisible index is ignored by the optimizer but still maintained, so you can watch for slow queries and make it VISIBLE again instantly if something needed it.
Lesson 6.2 · Views
  1. C The new numbers, including the new order: A view stores only its query. Every SELECT runs that query against the current base tables, so the new order is counted immediately.
  2. B A GROUP BY with COUNT(*): With GROUP BY, one view row stands for many base rows, so MySQL cannot tell which row an update should change. Such views report IS_UPDATABLE = NO and fail with error 1288.
  3. A Rejects writes through the view that would create rows the view cannot show: It rejects inserts and updates through the view that would produce a row the view's WHERE clause excludes, with error 1369.
  4. B The old columns only, without color: The star was expanded into a fixed column list when the view was created, so the new column is silently missing until the view is replaced.
  5. C Read the directory through the view, but not the table itself: The view reads employees with its definer's privileges, so the account sees exactly the view's columns and rows, and nothing else of the table.

Exit Ticket

  1. Index: (customer_id, book_id, sold_on): both equality columns first (either order), the sort column last. (customer_id, sold_on) alone still has to check the book on every row; (sold_on, ...) first cannot seek.
  2. Index killers: a function or arithmetic around the column (YEAR(sold_on), price * 1.1); a leading %; a text column compared with a number; OR across columns. Accept "a condition that matches most of the table" as a reason the optimizer ignores an index.
  3. Views: reasons: reuse a long query, a safe updatable slice (with a check option), a security boundary. Not: stored rows, indexes, speed. Accept "refresh itself" only if they explain it is always current because it stores no rows.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q2 C. Believes the order of the conditions in WHERE matters. It does not; the order of the columns in the index does.
  • Q4 B or C. Has not met type conversion. Run the lesson's step with SHOW WARNINGS: 1739 says it in words.
  • Q5 A. Mixes up an index's name with its columns.
  • Q7 B. Expects a cache. MySQL has no materialized views; P3 shows the view is always current.
  • Q9 A or B. Thinks "simple-looking" means updatable. The test: one view row = one base row.
  • Q10 B. The most common view surprise; the lesson's Common Mistake 5.
  • Exit ticket 1 with (sold_on, customer_id, book_id). The sort column first: worth two minutes of the Session 10 warm-up.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 10: Normalization and Stored Programs

Answers and reasoning for every Session 10 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs, both debug handouts, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 10 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up

Answers to W1 to W5, with MySQL's real output where the question shows code:

W1
Which plan line do you expect, and why is there an index at all?
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM orders WHERE customer_id = 4\G
Index lookup on orders using fk_orders_customer: InnoDB created that index for the foreign key.
*************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Index lookup on orders using fk_orders_customer (customer_id = 4)  (cost=0.7 rows=2)
W2
Is this view updatable?
CREATE VIEW v_cheap AS SELECT id, title, price FROM books WHERE price < 9;
SELECT TABLE_NAME, IS_UPDATABLE FROM information_schema.VIEWS
WHERE TABLE_SCHEMA = 'bookstore' AND TABLE_NAME = 'v_cheap';
DROP VIEW v_cheap;
YES: each view row is one books row (no grouping, DISTINCT or UNION).
+------------+--------------+
| TABLE_NAME | IS_UPDATABLE |
+------------+--------------+
| v_cheap    | YES          |
+------------+--------------+
W3
An index on (customer_id, sold_on): can a query on a sold_on range alone seek with it?
No: the dates are sorted only within each customer (leftmost prefix).
W4
What does MySQL store when you create a view?
Only the query; rows are computed on every read.
W5
What does WITH CHECK OPTION refuse?
A write through the view that would produce a row the view cannot show (1369).

6.3 · Database Design & Normalization: self-check

Q1
Alice's email is stored on three order rows. Someone corrects it on one row only, and now the table holds two emails for her. Which anomaly is this?
A) An update anomaly
B) An insert anomaly
C) A delete anomaly
D) A first normal form violation
Why: A fact stored several times was changed in one place: the copies drift apart and no query can tell which is right. The cause is the same as for all three anomalies: facts about a customer stored in an order table.
B is wrong: An insert anomaly is when you cannot add a fact (a customer with no order) without another one.
C is wrong: A delete anomaly is losing a fact (a customer) by deleting another (their only order).
D is wrong: Every cell here holds one value; the problem is repetition, which 2NF and 3NF deal with.
Q2
A contacts table has the columns name, phone1, phone2 and phone3. Which normal form does it break?
A) Second normal form
B) Third normal form
C) First normal form: a repeating group of columns
D) None: three columns are fine
Why: Numbered columns are a list spread across columns: a fourth phone needs a new column, and "who has this number?" must check three columns. 1NF: one row per phone in a child table (contact_id, phone).
A is wrong: 2NF is about partial dependencies on a composite key.
B is wrong: 3NF is about non-key columns that depend on other non-key columns.
D is wrong: It works until someone has four numbers, and every search gets three times longer.
Q3
enrollments(student_id, course, course_room, grade) has the key (student_id, course). Each course meets in one room. Which rule does course → course_room break?
A) 1NF
B) 2NF: course_room depends on only part of the key
C) 3NF: a transitive dependency
D) None: course is part of the key
Why: course_room needs only course, not the whole key, so every student's row repeats the room. Fix: courses(course, room) and enrollments(student_id, course, grade).
A is wrong: Every cell holds one value.
C is wrong: Transitive means through a NON-key column; course is part of the key.
D is wrong: Being part of the key is exactly what makes it a partial dependency.
Q4
employees(id, name, dept_id, dept_name) has the key id. Which dependency breaks 3NF, and what is the fix?
dept_id → dept_name (a transitive dependency: id → dept_id → dept_name); move dept_name to departments(id, name) and keep dept_id as a foreign key
Why: dept_name describes the department, not the employee. As long as it lives in employees, renaming a department means updating every employee in it.
Q5
You split a flat table into four, then run two EXCEPT queries in opposite directions (old rows EXCEPT the rebuilt join, and the other way). Both print Empty set. What does that prove?
A) Every new table has a primary key
B) The new tables contain no duplicates
C) The design is in 3NF
D) The four tables, joined back, hold exactly the original rows: the split lost nothing and added nothing
Why: EXCEPT returns rows of the first query missing from the second. One direction catches lost rows, the other catches invented ones (a wrong join). Both empty = lossless.
A is wrong: Keys are a separate check (SHOW CREATE TABLE).
B is wrong: EXCEPT compares the two sides; it says nothing about duplicates inside one table.
C is wrong: A lossless split can still keep a transitive dependency.
Q6
SELECT member_email FROM club_signups GROUP BY member_email HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT member_name) > 1; prints Empty set. What can you conclude?
A) member_email → member_name is proved
B) The data does not contradict member_email → member_name; whether it is a rule is a business decision
C) member_name → member_email holds
D) The table is in 3NF
Why: Data can disprove a dependency (one counterexample is enough) but never prove one: the next sign-up might break it. The rule comes from the business ("an email belongs to one member").
A is wrong: Six rows cannot prove a rule about all future rows.
C is wrong: The query tests the other direction.
D is wrong: One dependency says nothing about the whole table's normal form.

6.3 · Database Design & Normalization: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE TABLE sheet (member VARCHAR(30), club VARCHAR(30));
INSERT INTO sheet VALUES ('Ana', 'Chess'), ('Ben', 'Chess'), ('Ana', 'Poetry'),
                         ('Cy', 'Poetry'), ('Ben', 'Chess');
CREATE TABLE members (id INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY, name VARCHAR(30) UNIQUE);
INSERT INTO members (name) SELECT DISTINCT member FROM sheet ORDER BY member;
SELECT COUNT(*) AS sheet_rows, (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM members) AS members FROM sheet;
+------------+---------+
| sheet_rows | members |
+------------+---------+
|          5 |       3 |
+------------+---------+
Why: Five sheet rows, three distinct members (Ana, Ben, Cy): SELECT DISTINCT collapses the copies, and the UNIQUE on name would refuse a second run (1062).
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT title FROM books WHERE price < 9
EXCEPT
SELECT title FROM books WHERE in_stock = FALSE
ORDER BY title;
+---------------------+
| title               |
+---------------------+
| Animal Farm         |
| Pride and Prejudice |
+---------------------+
Why: Books under $9 are The Great Gatsby, The Catcher in the Rye, Animal Farm and Pride and Prejudice; EXCEPT removes the two that are out of stock. The ORDER BY sorts the whole EXCEPT result.

7.1 · Stored Procedures & Functions: self-check

Q7
In the mysql client, without changing the delimiter, you type a procedure whose body is BEGIN SELECT ...; END;. What happens?
A) Two ERROR 1064 lines: the client sent the text in two pieces at the semicolons
B) ERROR 1418
C) The procedure is created
D) ERROR 1304: PROCEDURE already exists
Why: The client splits at every ; and sends the unfinished CREATE ... BEGIN SELECT ... (1064 near ''), then the leftover END (1064 near 'END'). DELIMITER // ... // ... DELIMITER ; sends the whole body at once (the debug handout's section A).
B is wrong: 1418 is for functions without characteristics.
C is wrong: Only a one-statement body without BEGIN and END works without a delimiter change.
D is wrong: Nothing was created, so nothing exists yet.
Q8
What does this print? (Books under $9: The Great Gatsby, The Catcher in the Rye, Animal Farm, Pride and Prejudice.)
CREATE PROCEDURE count_cheaper(IN p_price DECIMAL(8, 2), OUT p_n INT)
    SELECT COUNT(*) INTO p_n FROM books WHERE price < p_price;
CALL count_cheaper(9.00, @n);
SELECT @n AS cheaper;
4 (in a column named cheaper)
Why: The one-statement body needs no BEGIN and no delimiter change. SELECT ... INTO stores the count in the OUT parameter, which lands in @n; the CALL itself prints nothing.
Q9
A stored function returns ROUND(p_amount * 1.08, 2) and reads no table. Which characteristics are true of it?
A) READS SQL DATA
B) MODIFIES SQL DATA
C) NOT DETERMINISTIC (the default)
D) DETERMINISTIC and NO SQL
Why: Same input, same output, and no SQL statement in the body: the lesson's with_tax. With binary logging on, one of DETERMINISTIC, NO SQL or READS SQL DATA is required (1418), and the promise must be true.
A is wrong: It reads no table.
B is wrong: It changes nothing.
C is wrong: False modesty is legal but untrue, and on its own it is refused with 1418.
Q10
A procedure runs SELECT title INTO p_title FROM books WHERE author_id = p_author_id;. What happens when it is called for J.R.R. Tolkien, who has two books?
A) p_title gets the first title in id order
B) p_title is NULL
C) ERROR 1172 (42000): Result consisted of more than one row
D) p_title holds both titles, separated by a comma
Why: One variable cannot hold two rows. The procedure was created without complaint; the error appears only on a call that hits it, which is why routines are tested with awkward inputs. Fix: an aggregate (MIN(title)) or ORDER BY ... LIMIT 1.
A is wrong: MySQL does not pick one for you.
B is wrong: NULL comes from an aggregate over no rows, not from two rows.
D is wrong: That would be GROUP_CONCAT.
Q11
What does the CALL print?
DELIMITER //
CREATE PROCEDURE check_qty(IN p_qty INT)
BEGIN
    IF p_qty < 1 THEN
        SIGNAL SQLSTATE '45000' SET MESSAGE_TEXT = 'Quantity must be at least 1';
    END IF;
    SELECT p_qty AS accepted;
END //
DELIMITER ;
CALL check_qty(0);
ERROR 1644 (45000): Quantity must be at least 1
Why: SIGNAL SQLSTATE '45000' raises your own error, reported as 1644 with your message, and stops the procedure: the SELECT after it never runs.
Q12
Which of these can a stored function NOT do?
A) Return one value
B) Read tables (declared READS SQL DATA)
C) Run COMMIT or START TRANSACTION
D) Be used inside WHERE
Why: Functions cannot control transactions and cannot return result sets; procedures can (Lesson 7.2 puts transactions inside procedures). Used in WHERE, a function runs once per row, so keep it cheap.
A is wrong: That is what a function is for.
B is wrong: copies_sold and author_revenue do exactly that.
D is wrong: Anywhere an expression fits: the functions lab filters with copies_sold(id) = 0.

7.1 · Stored Procedures & Functions: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
DELIMITER //
CREATE PROCEDURE double_it(INOUT p_n INT)
BEGIN
    SET p_n = p_n * 2;
END //
DELIMITER ;
SET @n = 10;
CALL double_it(@n);
CALL double_it(@n);
SELECT @n AS n;
+------+
| n    |
+------+
|   40 |
+------+
Why: INOUT reads the caller's value and replaces it: 10 → 20 → 40.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
DELIMITER //
CREATE PROCEDURE first_title(IN p_author_id INT, OUT p_title VARCHAR(200))
BEGIN
    SELECT MIN(title) INTO p_title FROM books WHERE author_id = p_author_id;
END //
DELIMITER ;
SET @t = 'nothing yet';
CALL first_title(10, @t);
SELECT @t AS asimov_first_title;
+--------------------+
| asimov_first_title |
+--------------------+
| NULL               |
+--------------------+
Why: NULL, not 'nothing yet': an OUT parameter starts as NULL inside the procedure (it does not carry the caller's value in), and MIN over no rows is NULL. (With INOUT, 'nothing yet' would come in, but MIN over no rows would still overwrite it with NULL.)

| shipping_fee | YES              | NO SQL          |

shipping_fee is DETERMINISTIC and NO SQL: the same item count always gives the same fee. copies_sold reads order_items, whose rows change, so READS SQL DATA (DETERMINISTIC would be a false promise the server cannot check). The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 6.3 · Database Design & Normalization
  1. A First normal form: 1NF requires one value per cell. A comma list puts two values in one cell, which is why SQL cannot sum or join on them.
  2. C A non-key column that depends on another non-key column: A non-key column determined by another non-key column, like order_id determining cust_email and cust_email determining cust_city. 3NF removes it by moving the customer columns to their own table.
  3. B 2NF: it depends on part of the key: order_date depends on order_id, which is only part of the composite key: a partial dependency, so the table is not in 2NF. That is why the date repeats on every line of an order.
  4. A No: it records what was paid on that order, a different fact from today's price: price_at_time is a fact about the sale, determined by (order_id, book_id). books.price is the current price. They only coincide until the first price change, and then old orders must keep what was actually paid.
  5. B When a measured query is too slow and you have a plan to keep the total in sync: Denormalize only for a measured problem that indexes and better queries did not solve, and only with a plan (a transaction, a trigger or a refresh) that keeps the stored value correct.
Lesson 7.1 · Stored Procedures & Functions
  1. B The mysql command-line client: DELIMITER is a client command. The mysql client uses it to decide where one statement ends before sending it; the server only ever receives the finished CREATE PROCEDURE statement.
  2. C Every book in the table: Inside the routine the name id means the parameter, so the condition is 1 = 1 on every row and all the books come back, with no error or warning.
  3. A READS SQL DATA: The function reads tables and changes nothing, so READS SQL DATA is the honest description. It is also enough to avoid error 1418.
  4. C A stored function: Only a stored function returns a single value that can be used inside an expression, once per row of the query.
  5. A Error 1644 with the message from SIGNAL, and nothing is inserted: The IF check runs first and SIGNAL raises error 1644 with the procedure's own message. The procedure stops there, before either INSERT, so no author and no book are added.

Exit Ticket

  1. Partial dependency: order_id → order_date (moves to orders) or book_title → book_price (moves to books). Reject (order_id, book_title) → quantity: that is the whole key, which is fine.
  2. Check before change: so a refused call leaves the data untouched (statements that already ran are not undone by SIGNAL); SIGNAL SQLSTATE '45000' SET MESSAGE_TEXT = '...', reported as error 1644.
  3. Function / procedure: a function (one value, inside a SELECT); a procedure (it inserts rows and returns the new id through an OUT parameter, like the project's add_book).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q2 A or B. Thinks numbered columns are a dependency problem. They are a list: 1NF.
  • Q3 C. Mixes up "part of the key" (2NF) with "another non-key column" (3NF).
  • Q6 A. Treats data as proof; worth repeating with the worked example's price_at_time.
  • Q7 C. Believes the server reads DELIMITER, or has only seen one-statement bodies.
  • Q9 A. Picks READS SQL DATA by reflex: the function reads no table.
  • Q10 A. Expects MySQL to pick a row: it refuses (1172).
  • Exit ticket 3 "procedure" for both. Ask them to write the SELECT with a CALL inside it: impossible.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 11: Transactions and JSON

Answers and reasoning for every Session 11 item: the warm-up, Checkpoint 2 (rubric, model answer, weak answers), both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 11 12 self-check items Checkpoint 2 Confidential

Warm-up

  1. W1 (6.3) 1NF: one value per cell. Fix: one row per book in a child table (member_id, book_id).
  2. W2 (6.3) "The whole key" is 2NF (no column depends on only part of a composite key); "nothing but the key" is 3NF (no column depends on another non-key column).
  3. W3 (7.1) The mysql client. It changes where the client cuts statements; the server never sees the word DELIMITER.
  4. W4 (7.1) A stored function: it returns one value and can sit inside an expression. A procedure runs with CALL.
  5. W5 (7.1) 11.99 for Dune and 7.99 for 1984: 80% of the price, rounded to cents.

W5 prints:

+-------+-------+---------+
| title | price | on_sale |
+-------+-------+---------+
| Dune  | 14.99 |   11.99 |
| 1984  |  9.99 |    7.99 |
+-------+-------+---------+

Checkpoint 2: Rubric, Model Answer and Weak Answers

The task

Thirty minutes, alone, open notes. Parts 1 to 4 in labs/module-11/checkpoint-2/checkpoint2_starter.sql, Part 5 on paper in the workbook (the class_signups table).

PartLessonsTask
Part 15.1, 5.2The best seller(s) of each category by copies sold (SUM of order_items.quantity), ties sharing first place: a CTE for the copies and a window function to rank. Columns category, title, copies, sorted by category.
Part 25.1Everyone who reports to Daniel Reyes (employee 2), directly or through someone else, with a level column (1 = reports to Daniel directly), sorted by level and id: a recursive CTE.
Part 36.2The view v_customer_revenue (customer_id, customer, orders, revenue) for customers who have ordered, then a query on the view: customers with revenue above 30, highest first.
Part 46.1The plan of SELECT id, first_name FROM customers WHERE city = 'Chicago' before and after an index that helps it: the first line of each plan.
Part 56.3On paper: the table class_signups below. Name its key, the dependencies that break 2NF and 3NF, and the tables (with keys) you would split it into.

Rubric. Five rows, four levels, 20 points. "Proficient" in every row (15 points) is a successful checkpoint. Circle one cell per row and write one sentence of advice.

Criterion1 · Beginning2 · Developing3 · Proficient4 · Exemplary
CTE and window function (Part 1)No ranking: a GROUP BY with MAX(copies) that cannot say which title won, or no result.A ranking, but over the whole shop (no PARTITION BY), or the window function filtered in the same query's WHERE (ERROR 3593).A CTE for the copies, RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY category ORDER BY copies DESC) in a second step, filtered on 1 outside: five rows (Classic, Dystopian and Fiction 1984 with 4; Fantasy The Hobbit 3; Science Fiction Dune 3).Also says why the filter needs a second step (window functions run after WHERE) and why RANK, not ROW_NUMBER, keeps ties.
Recursive CTE (Part 2)Only the direct reports (WHERE manager_id = 2), or a self-join with a fixed depth.A recursive CTE that starts from Daniel himself (adds him as level 1) or has no working level column.Anchor manager_id = 2 at level 1, recursive member joined on e.manager_id = t.id with level + 1: six rows, Priya and Marcus at 1; Tom, Lena, Sofia and Ben at 2.Also explains why the recursion stops (no one reports to the level-2 people) and notices Ben is inactive, keeping or filtering him on purpose.
View (Part 3)No view: the query typed directly, or a view that does not run.A view that runs but counts order LINES as orders (COUNT(*) gives Alice 4) or leaves out the name.COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) and SUM(quantity * price_at_time) in the view; the query on the view returns Alice Johnson 3 / 58.95, Dave Brown 45.96, Frank Miller 41.96, Grace Wilson 33.97, Bob Smith 33.47.Also says a view stores the query, not the rows (it is always current), and that a LEFT JOIN would list customers who never ordered.
Index and EXPLAIN (Part 4)No plan read, or an index on a column the search does not use.An index on city, but the plans are not read (no before/after) or misread.Before: Table scan on customers under a Filter; after CREATE INDEX ... (city): Index lookup on customers using idx_customers_city.Also reads the row estimates (8 scanned, then 2) and says what the index costs (space, slower writes) and why 8 rows hide the gain.
Normalization (Part 5)A list of tables with no key or reasons.Finds the 2NF problems (student_name; class_title, room, teacher) or the 3NF one (teacher -> teacher_phone), not both.Key (student_email, class_code); 2NF: student_name depends on student_email, class_title/room/teacher on class_code; 3NF: teacher_phone depends on teacher. Tables: students, teachers, classes (teacher FK), signups (both FKs as the key).Also names the anomalies (Lee Wong's phone in three rows; a class nobody signed up for cannot exist; deleting Cy's signup loses Glass Fusing) and questions whether room belongs to the class.
Model answer, Parts 1 to 4: labs/module-11/checkpoint-2/checkpoint2_solution.sql

The file prints (MySQL 9.7, fresh bookstore):

+-----------------+------------+--------+
| category        | title      | copies |
+-----------------+------------+--------+
| Classic         | 1984       |      4 |
| Dystopian       | 1984       |      4 |
| Fantasy         | The Hobbit |      3 |
| Fiction         | 1984       |      4 |
| Science Fiction | Dune       |      3 |
+-----------------+------------+--------+
+-------+------+---------------+------------+
| level | id   | employee      | manager_id |
+-------+------+---------------+------------+
|     1 |    3 | Priya Nair    |          2 |
|     1 |    6 | Marcus Hill   |          2 |
|     2 |    4 | Tom Becker    |          3 |
|     2 |    5 | Lena Park     |          3 |
|     2 |    7 | Sofia Alvarez |          6 |
|     2 |   10 | Ben Carter    |          3 |
+-------+------+---------------+------------+
+---------------+--------+---------+
| customer      | orders | revenue |
+---------------+--------+---------+
| Alice Johnson |      3 |   58.95 |
| Dave Brown    |      2 |   45.96 |
| Frank Miller  |      2 |   41.96 |
| Grace Wilson  |      2 |   33.97 |
| Bob Smith     |      2 |   33.47 |
+---------------+--------+---------+
*************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Filter: (customers.city = 'Chicago')  (cost=1.05 rows=1)
    -> Table scan on customers  (cost=1.05 rows=8)
*************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Index lookup on customers using idx_customers_city (city = 'Chicago')  (cost=0.7 rows=2)

Reading it: Part 1 shows 1984 winning three categories (4 copies) and no tie this time; RANK would keep a tie, ROW_NUMBER would silently drop one. Part 2's recursion stops at level 2 because nobody reports to Tom, Lena, Sofia or Ben. Part 3: Alice's 3 orders hold 4 order lines, the reason COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) matters. Part 4: the cost numbers differ by machine; the access method (table scan, then index lookup) is the answer.

Model answer, Part 5 (normalization)
  • Key: (student_email, class_code): one student in one class.
  • 2NF (partial dependencies): student_email → student_name; class_code → class_title, room, teacher. They depend on only part of the key, so Ana's name is stored twice and Pottery Basics' details twice.
  • 3NF (transitive dependency): teacher → teacher_phone. The phone describes the teacher, not the sign-up or the class; Lee Wong's number is stored three times.
  • Tables: students(email PK, name); teachers(id PK, name, phone); classes(code PK, title, room, teacher_id FK → teachers); signups(student_email FK → students, class_code FK → classes, PRIMARY KEY (student_email, class_code)).
  • Anomalies it removes: update (change Lee Wong's phone in three rows), insert (a new class with no sign-ups has nowhere to go), delete (cancelling Cy's sign-up erases Glass Fusing).
Two weak answers, run for real

Part 3 with COUNT(*) instead of COUNT(DISTINCT o.id): it counts order lines, so Alice gets 4 "orders" (Developing in the View row):

SELECT CONCAT(c.first_name, ' ', c.last_name) AS customer,
       COUNT(*) AS orders,
       SUM(oi.quantity * oi.price_at_time) AS revenue
FROM customers AS c
JOIN orders AS o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items AS oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE c.id = 1
GROUP BY c.id, c.first_name, c.last_name;
+---------------+--------+---------+
| customer      | orders | revenue |
+---------------+--------+---------+
| Alice Johnson |      4 |   58.95 |
+---------------+--------+---------+

Part 1 with the window function in WHERE: MySQL refuses, because window functions are computed after WHERE (Developing in the first row; the fix is the second step):

SELECT c.name AS category, b.title, SUM(oi.quantity) AS copies,
       RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY c.id ORDER BY SUM(oi.quantity) DESC) AS place
FROM categories AS c
JOIN book_categories AS bc ON bc.category_id = c.id
JOIN books AS b            ON b.id = bc.book_id
JOIN order_items AS oi     ON oi.book_id = b.id
WHERE RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY c.id ORDER BY SUM(oi.quantity) DESC) = 1
GROUP BY c.id, c.name, b.id, b.title;
ERROR 3593 (HY000): You cannot use the window function 'rank' in this context.'

7.2 · Transactions: self-check

Q1
You type START TRANSACTION; and an UPDATE that succeeds, then close the terminal window without COMMIT. What happens to the change?
A) It is kept: the UPDATE succeeded
B) The server rolls it back when the connection ends
C) It waits, and is committed the next time you log in
D) It is kept, but only your account can see it
Why: Nothing is committed unless you say COMMIT (or autocommit does it for you). A dropped connection ends the open transaction with a rollback (the lesson's Common Questions).
A is wrong: Succeeding is not the same as being committed; inside a transaction the change is still provisional.
C is wrong: Transactions do not survive the connection; a new login starts with nothing open.
D is wrong: Uncommitted changes are visible to your own session only while it is open; once it closes they are gone.
Q2
What does the last statement print?
CREATE TABLE stock (id INT PRIMARY KEY, qty INT NOT NULL);
INSERT INTO stock VALUES (1, 5), (2, 5);
START TRANSACTION;
UPDATE stock SET qty = qty - 1 WHERE id = 1;
SAVEPOINT s1;
UPDATE stock SET qty = qty - 1 WHERE id = 2;
ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT s1;
UPDATE stock SET qty = qty + 3 WHERE id = 2;
COMMIT;
SELECT id, qty FROM stock ORDER BY id;
Two rows: id 1 qty 4, id 2 qty 8
Why: The first UPDATE (id 1 to 4) happened before the savepoint and stays. ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT s1 undoes only the -1 on id 2. The +3 on id 2 comes after the rollback, so id 2 goes from 5 to 8, and COMMIT keeps both.
Q3
Which statement, typed inside an open transaction, commits that transaction on the spot?
A) SAVEPOINT before_change;
B) SELECT price FROM books WHERE id = 1 FOR UPDATE;
C) ALTER TABLE books ADD COLUMN rating INT;
D) ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT before_change;
Why: Schema changes (CREATE, ALTER, DROP, RENAME, TRUNCATE) cause an implicit commit before they run, so a later ROLLBACK has nothing left to undo: the trap the demo showed with CREATE TABLE.
A is wrong: A savepoint is a bookmark inside the transaction; the transaction stays open.
B is wrong: FOR UPDATE takes row locks inside the transaction; it does not end it.
D is wrong: Rolling back to a savepoint undoes part of the work and keeps the transaction open.
Q4
Window A runs START TRANSACTION; and UPDATE accounts SET balance = 0 WHERE id = 1; and does not commit. Window B then runs SELECT balance FROM accounts WHERE id = 1 FOR UPDATE;. What does B get?
A) 0, A's new value
B) The last committed balance, at once
C) ERROR 1213 at once: a deadlock
D) Nothing yet: B waits for A's row lock, and fails with ERROR 1205 if A holds it past the timeout
Why: A locking read (FOR UPDATE) needs the same row lock A's UPDATE holds, so it waits like a writer. A plain SELECT would not wait: it reads the committed snapshot.
A is wrong: No session ever sees another's uncommitted value under REPEATABLE READ.
B is wrong: That is what a PLAIN SELECT gets; FOR UPDATE must lock the row first.
C is wrong: A deadlock needs two transactions each waiting for the other; here only B waits.
Q5
In the transfer procedure's handler, why does RESIGNAL come right after ROLLBACK?
A) So the caller still receives the original error after the half-done changes are undone
B) RESIGNAL commits whatever the handler did not roll back
C) Without it, the handler would run again and again
D) Every handler must end with RESIGNAL or the procedure does not compile
Why: ROLLBACK cleans up; RESIGNAL passes the same error (1644, 3819, ...) on, so the app knows the call failed and why. A handler that only rolls back hides the failure.
B is wrong: RESIGNAL raises an error; it never commits anything.
C is wrong: An EXIT handler runs once and leaves the procedure.
D is wrong: RESIGNAL is optional syntax; leaving it out compiles fine, which is exactly why the silent version is a trap.
Q6
An app's checkout fails with ERROR 1213 (40001): Deadlock found when trying to get lock; try restarting transaction. What should the app do?
A) Nothing: the statements before the deadlock were saved
B) Run only the statement that failed again
C) Raise innodb_lock_wait_timeout so it never happens again
D) Run the whole transaction again from the start
Why: InnoDB picked this transaction as the deadlock victim and rolled ALL of it back. The message is the advice: restart the transaction. Short transactions that lock rows in the same order make it rare.
A is wrong: The victim's whole transaction was rolled back; nothing of it was saved.
B is wrong: The earlier statements were undone too; repeating one statement alone leaves the job half done.
C is wrong: A deadlock is detected at once, not after a timeout; the timeout setting does not affect it.

7.2 · Transactions: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE TABLE counter (n INT);
INSERT INTO counter VALUES (1);
SET autocommit = 0;
UPDATE counter SET n = n + 10;
ROLLBACK;
SET autocommit = 1;
SELECT n FROM counter;
+------+
| n    |
+------+
|    1 |
+------+
Why: With autocommit off, the UPDATE waited for a decision; ROLLBACK discarded it, so n is still 1. Switching autocommit off for a whole session is rare: one forgotten COMMIT and the work is lost at disconnect.
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
CREATE TABLE seats (id INT PRIMARY KEY, taken INT NOT NULL);
INSERT INTO seats VALUES (1, 0), (2, 0);
START TRANSACTION;
UPDATE seats SET taken = 1 WHERE id = 1;
SAVEPOINT one_seat;
UPDATE seats SET taken = 1 WHERE id = 2;
ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT one_seat;
COMMIT;
SELECT SUM(taken) AS seats_taken FROM seats;
+-------------+
| seats_taken |
+-------------+
|           1 |
+-------------+
Why: Seat 1 was taken before the savepoint and kept; seat 2 was taken after it and rolled back. COMMIT keeps what is left: one seat.

7.3 · JSON Columns: self-check

Q7
On the web page, CONCAT(title, ': ', attributes->'$.series') prints Dune: "Dune Chronicles". Which change removes the quotes?
A) Wrap the whole CONCAT in TRIM()
B) Use JSON_EXTRACT instead of ->
C) Use ->> instead of ->
D) Store the series without quotes in the document
Why: -> returns a JSON value, and a JSON string keeps its double quotes. ->> (JSON_UNQUOTE of JSON_EXTRACT) returns plain text: use it whenever a value leaves JSON for display, CONCAT or comparison.
A is wrong: TRIM removes spaces, not quote characters.
B is wrong: JSON_EXTRACT is exactly what -> means; same quotes.
D is wrong: Every JSON string is stored with double quotes; that is the format, not a mistake.
Q8
What does the SELECT print?
CREATE TABLE shelf (id INT PRIMARY KEY, info JSON);
INSERT INTO shelf VALUES (1, '{"tags": ["new", "signed", "gift"], "copies": 2}');
SELECT info->>'$.tags[last]' AS last_tag, JSON_LENGTH(info->'$.tags') AS tag_count
FROM shelf;
gift and 3
Why: [last] is the last element of the array, and ->> returns it as plain text; JSON_LENGTH counts the three elements.
Q9
A document already has a format key. What does JSON_INSERT(doc, '$.format', 'ebook') return?
A) The document unchanged
B) The document with format replaced by ebook
C) The document with a second format key
D) An error: the key already exists
Why: JSON_INSERT only adds missing keys; JSON_SET adds or replaces; JSON_REPLACE only replaces existing keys. All three return a changed copy, never an error, for a key that is or is not there.
B is wrong: That is JSON_SET (or JSON_REPLACE).
C is wrong: A JSON object never holds the same key twice.
D is wrong: The JSON_* change functions do not raise errors for existing or missing keys.
Q10
WHERE attributes->>'$.format' = 'hardcover' is slow on a table of a million books. What is MySQL's fix?
A) CREATE INDEX idx_attr ON books (attributes);
B) A generated column for $.format, with an index on that column
C) Change the column type from JSON to TEXT
D) Write JSON_EXTRACT instead of ->>
Why: A JSON column cannot be indexed as a whole (error 3152). A VIRTUAL generated column pulls one path out, and its index is used even by the original ->> expression, as the demo's EXPLAIN showed.
A is wrong: That is error 3152: JSON columns are indexed only through generated columns.
C is wrong: TEXT loses validation and is no easier to index by path.
D is wrong: Same lookup, same full scan.
Q11
What does this print?
SELECT JSON_OBJECT('title', 'Dune', 'id', 1, 'price', 14.99) AS doc;
{"id": 1, "price": 14.99, "title": "Dune"}
Why: MySQL stores JSON in a binary form with the keys of every object sorted: shorter keys first, then alphabetically. The data is the same; only the order changed, so never rely on key order.
Q12
The store wants to record four things. Which ONE belongs in a JSON column rather than a regular column or table?
A) The customer an order belongs to
B) A book's price
C) The line items of an order
D) An optional gift-wrap note some orders have, with a color and a message
Why: Optional, varied, display-only details are what JSON is for. The other three are core data: you join on the customer, sum the price, and line items are a list of related things that need their own table with foreign keys.
A is wrong: You join on it and it needs a foreign key: a column.
B is wrong: Every book has one, and every total uses it: a DECIMAL column.
C is wrong: A list of related rows is a child table (order_items), the lesson's 'when not to use JSON' table.

7.3 · JSON Columns: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT JSON_VALID('{"a": 1}') AS ok,
       JSON_VALID("{'a': 1}")  AS single_quotes,
       JSON_TYPE('[1, 2]')     AS kind;
+------+---------------+-------+
| ok   | single_quotes | kind  |
+------+---------------+-------+
|    1 |             0 | ARRAY |
+------+---------------+-------+
Why: JSON_VALID accepts double quotes only (single quotes give 0); JSON_TYPE names the kind of value: ARRAY.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
SET @doc = '{"format": "paperback"}';
SELECT JSON_INSERT(@doc, '$.format', 'ebook')  AS ins,
       JSON_REPLACE(@doc, '$.pages', 300)      AS rep;
+-------------------------+-------------------------+
| ins                     | rep                     |
+-------------------------+-------------------------+
| {"format": "paperback"} | {"format": "paperback"} |
+-------------------------+-------------------------+
Why: JSON_INSERT never replaces an existing key (format stays paperback); JSON_REPLACE never adds a missing key (no pages). Both return the document unchanged here; JSON_SET would have done both.

+---------------+--------------+

|           6 |     15 |
|           7 |     25 |
+-------------+--------+

| customer_id | points |
+-------------+--------+
|           2 |    170 |
+-------------+--------+

| log_rows |
+----------+
|        0 |
+----------+

| autocommit | isolation       |
+------------+-----------------+
|          1 | REPEATABLE-READ |
+------------+-----------------+

What each TODO teaches: (1) two UPDATEs that only make sense together, committed as one; (2) a rehearsal: the DELETE of 5 orders cascades to 8 order lines inside the transaction, and ROLLBACK brings all of them back; (3) ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT keeps the bonus and drops the slip that zeroed everyone (as shipped, the zeros are committed); (4) the implicit commit: as shipped, CREATE TABLE ends the transaction, the INSERT then autocommits and the ROLLBACK undoes nothing (Bob 10, log_rows 1); moved above START TRANSACTION, the ROLLBACK undoes both (Bob 170, log_rows 0).

Then, at the prompt (real output, same session as the solution):

CALL return_item(99);
CALL return_item(13);
SELECT (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders) AS orders,
       (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM order_items) AS items,
       (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM order_items WHERE id = 13) AS line_13_kept;
ERROR 1644 (45000): No such order line
ERROR 1644 (45000): Customer has no loyalty account
+--------+-------+--------------+
| orders | items | line_13_kept |
+--------+-------+--------------+
|     11 |    16 |            1 |
+--------+-------+--------------+

Reading it: line 17 was one of order 12's two lines, so order 12 stays; line 3 was order 2's only line, so the order goes too. Points are FLOOR(quantity × price): 8 for Dave, 7 for Alice. Eve has no loyalty row, so the UPDATE changes nothing, the SIGNAL fires after both DELETEs, and the handler rolls them back: line 13 is still there. Without TODOs 1 to 3 (the starter as shipped), the same call deletes Eve's line and THEN fails: the half-done return the lesson warns about.

Handout: labs/module-11/l7_2-transactions/debug_lost_points.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints (the table, then the two errors at the lines where they happened):

ERROR 3819 (HY000): Check constraint 'chk_points' is violated.
ERROR 1305 (42000): SAVEPOINT before_bonuses does not exist
+-------------+--------+
| customer_id | points |
+-------------+--------+
|           1 |      0 |
|           2 |    340 |
|           4 |     20 |
|           6 |     80 |
|           7 |      0 |
+-------------+--------+
  1. A, 3819: Grace has 0 points, so her -50 breaks the CHECK. The failed UPDATE undid only itself; COMMIT then saved Bob's +50: 50 points from nothing. Fix: ROLLBACK after the error (in code, a handler).
  2. B, 1305: the savepoint is before_bonus, the ROLLBACK names before_bonuses. The transaction stays open with everyone doubled, and COMMIT keeps it (Bob 170 × 2 = 340). Fix: the right name.
  3. C, silent: CREATE TABLE committed Alice's zero before it ran, so the ROLLBACK had nothing to undo. Fix: create the table before START TRANSACTION.

Fixed (real run; the 3819 still appears, because the statement still fails, but nothing is saved):

ERROR 3819 (HY000): Check constraint 'chk_points' is violated.
+-------------+--------+
| customer_id | points |
+-------------+--------+
|           1 |    300 |
|           2 |    120 |
|           4 |     10 |
|           6 |     40 |
|           7 |      0 |
+-------------+--------+

| id | first_name | prefs                                                                     |
+----+------------+---------------------------------------------------------------------------+

+------------+-----------+
| newsletter | customers |
+------------+-----------+
| NULL       |         3 |
| false      |         1 |
| true       |         4 |
+------------+-----------+

Reading it: a JSON true read with ->> is the text 'true' (comparing with the number 1 or TRUE finds nothing). Dave's prefs exist without a genres key, so JSON_CONTAINS_PATH says 0; Carol, Eve and Hank have no document at all, so it says NULL: the difference between "missing key" and "missing document". Grace's document lost the language key and gained a genre, and its keys come back sorted. The generated column turns three states into a GROUP BY: true, false and NULL (no prefs).

Lab 7.3-B: labs/module-11/l7_3-json-columns/order_documents_starter.sql → order_documents_solution.sql

The solution prints (an empty first line marks the silent SET):

+-------------------------------------------------------------------+
| order_doc                                                         |
+-------------------------------------------------------------------+
| {"date": "2026-01-15", "lines": 2, "total": 34.97, "order_id": 1} |
| {"date": "2026-02-20", "lines": 1, "total": 7.99, "order_id": 2}  |
| {"date": "2026-04-05", "lines": 1, "total": 15.99, "order_id": 8} |
+-------------------------------------------------------------------+
+-----------------------------------------+
| best_sellers                            |
+-----------------------------------------+
| {"1984": 4, "Dune": 3, "The Hobbit": 3} |
+-----------------------------------------+
+---------+------+------------------------+----------+
| book_id | qty  | title                  | in_stock |
+---------+------+------------------------+----------+
|       4 |    5 | The Great Gatsby       |        0 |
|       8 |    3 | The Catcher in the Rye |        0 |
|      42 |    1 | NULL                   |     NULL |
+---------+------+------------------------+----------+
+-----------------------+--------------------+
| supplier              | still_out_of_stock |
+-----------------------+--------------------+
| Riverside Book Supply |                  0 |

Reading it: JSON_OBJECT accepts aggregates in a grouped query; the total stays a number. JSON_OBJECTAGG sorts its keys (shortest first), so the order is stable even though the rows are not ordered. JSON_TABLE's LEFT JOIN exposes the supplier's mistake (book 42 does not exist) before anything is updated; the UPDATE joins only real books. Learners who used an inner JOIN in step 3 never see book 42: ask what the supplier should be told.

Handout: labs/module-11/l7_3-json-columns/debug_bad_json.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped, MySQL prints:

ERROR 3140 (22032): Invalid JSON text: "Missing a name for object member." at position 1 in value for column 'wishlists.items'.
ERROR 3143 (42000): Invalid JSON path expression. The error is around character position 1.
ERROR 3152 (42000): JSON column 'items' supports indexing only via generated columns on a specified JSON path.
+--------------------+
| label              |
+--------------------+
| Alice ("birthday") |
+--------------------+
Empty set
+-------------+-------+--------------------------------------------------------------+
| customer_id | owner | items                                                        |
+-------------+-------+--------------------------------------------------------------+
|           1 | Alice | {"public": true, "book_ids": [1, 9], "occasion": "birthday"} |
|           7 | Grace | {"public": false, "book_ids": [1, 6]}                        |
+-------------+-------+--------------------------------------------------------------+
  1. A, 3140: single quotes inside the document. Fix: '{"book_ids": [2, 11], "public": true}' (or JSON_OBJECT).
  2. B, 3143: the path has no $. Fix: items->>'$.book_ids'.
  3. C, 3152: a JSON column cannot be indexed whole. Fix: a generated column for one path (occasion) with an index on it.
  4. D, silent: -> keeps the JSON quotes: Alice ("birthday"). Fix: ->>.
  5. E, silent: $.bookIds does not exist (the key is book_ids), so MEMBER OF finds nobody and nothing warns. Fix: the real key name.

Fixed (real run):

+-------+---------+
| Alice | [1, 9]  |
| Bob   | [2, 11] |
| Grace | [1, 6]  |

+------------------+
| label            |
+------------------+
| Alice (birthday) |

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 7.2 · Transactions
  1. A Atomicity: Atomicity: all of a transaction's changes are kept, or none of them. The order row and its item rows succeed or fail together.
  2. C The last committed value, immediately: InnoDB gives plain readers the last committed version of the row, without waiting. B sees A's change only after A commits.
  3. B The first UPDATE only: In MySQL a failed statement rolls back only itself and the transaction stays open, so COMMIT saves the first UPDATE. Roll back yourself, or use a handler.
  4. B CREATE TABLE demo_audit (note VARCHAR(100)): DDL such as CREATE TABLE causes an implicit commit before it runs, so a later ROLLBACK cannot undo the earlier changes.
  5. C Another transaction held the row's lock longer than the timeout: Another transaction held a lock on the row, and your statement gave up after innodb_lock_wait_timeout seconds (50 by default). Only that statement failed.
Lesson 7.3 · JSON Columns
  1. C attributes->>'$.format': The double arrow extracts and unquotes in one step: it is short for JSON_UNQUOTE(JSON_EXTRACT(attributes, '$.format')).
  2. A JSON keys are case-sensitive, so $.FORMAT is NULL for every book: JSON keys are case-sensitive. No document has a FORMAT key, so the expression is NULL for every row, and NULL never equals anything. MySQL does not warn about a missing key.
  3. B Add a generated column for $.format and index that column: A generated column extracts the value from each document, and an ordinary index on that column can be used, even by queries that write the original JSON expression.
  4. C It fails with error 3140, because JSON keys must be double-quoted strings: The key format has no double quotes, so the text is not valid JSON and the JSON column rejects it with error 3140. The row keeps its old value.
  5. B The price, which every book has and every order total uses: Every book has a price, it needs an exact DECIMAL type and NOT NULL, and orders and reports calculate with it all the time. That is regular-column data.

Exit Ticket

  1. Committed after 3819: every change made before the failed statement in that transaction; the failed statement undid only itself. ROLLBACK after an error, or a handler.
  2. The handler: DECLARE EXIT HANDLER FOR SQLEXCEPTION BEGIN ROLLBACK; RESIGNAL; END; (declared after the variables, before the other statements).
  3. JSON or column: JSON for optional, varied, display-only details (series, edition, awards, illustrated); a column for what every row has or what you join, sum or constrain (price, author_id, customer_id). Accept any pair with a reason from the lesson's table.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A ("kept, because the UPDATE succeeded"). Success is not commitment. Rerun the two-window step: B never saw the change until COMMIT.
  • Q3 A or D (a savepoint statement ends the transaction). Confuses bookmarks with endings; only COMMIT, ROLLBACK and implicit commits end it.
  • Q4 B ("the committed value at once"). Right for a plain SELECT, wrong for FOR UPDATE: the whole point of a locking read is to wait.
  • Q6 B ("repeat the failed statement"). The victim's whole transaction is gone; repeating one statement leaves half a transfer.
  • Q7 B (JSON_EXTRACT). Thinks the function is different from the arrow; it is the same thing. Only ->> (or JSON_UNQUOTE) removes quotes.
  • Q12 C (line items in JSON). The most tempting design mistake of the lesson: a list of related things with prices and books needs its own table and foreign keys.
  • Checkpoint, Part 3 orders = 4 for Alice. Counting rows after a join counts the many side; COUNT(DISTINCT) or aggregate before joining.

Session 12: Users, Permissions and Backups

Answers and reasoning for every Session 12 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs (with the logins as demo accounts), the 8.1 shell runs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 12 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up

  1. W1 (7.2) No. ALTER TABLE caused an implicit commit before it ran, so the UPDATE was already committed and the ROLLBACK had nothing to undo.
  2. W2 (7.2) 1205: a lock wait timed out; only the waiting statement failed and the transaction is still open. 1213: a deadlock; InnoDB rolled the whole transaction back, so retry all of it.
  3. W3 (7.3) ->> : it returns plain text; -> would return the JSON string "hardcover" with its quotes, and the comparison would fail.
  4. W4 (7.3) It turns a JSON array (in a column or a literal) into rows with typed columns that you can join, group and filter.
  5. W5 (7.2 + 7.3) 2 and ["gift"]: the JSON_SET happened inside the transaction and the ROLLBACK discarded it.

W5 prints:

+------+----------+
| qty  | tags     |
+------+----------+
| 2    | ["gift"] |
+------+----------+

7.4 · User Management & Permissions: self-check

Q1
A server has 'app'@'localhost' and 'app'@'%'. How many accounts is that?
A) One account that may connect from two places
B) Two separate accounts, each with its own password and privileges
C) One account: % already includes localhost, so the second row is ignored
D) None: the second CREATE USER failed because the name was taken
Why: An account is the pair 'user'@'host'. The same name with another host is another account, which is why SHOW GRANTS FOR 'shop_reports'@'%' fails with 1141 when only @'localhost' exists.
A is wrong: Privileges and passwords belong to each pair separately.
C is wrong: Both rows are real accounts; MySQL picks the most specific host that matches a login.
D is wrong: The pair is the identity; the same user name with a different host is allowed.
Q2
How many lines does the SHOW GRANTS print, and what are they?
CREATE USER 'kit_quiz'@'localhost' IDENTIFIED BY 'CHANGE-ME-quiz-1';
GRANT SELECT ON bookstore.* TO 'kit_quiz'@'localhost';
GRANT INSERT ON bookstore.orders TO 'kit_quiz'@'localhost';
SHOW GRANTS FOR 'kit_quiz'@'localhost';
Three: GRANT USAGE ON *.*, GRANT SELECT ON `bookstore`.*, GRANT INSERT ON `bookstore`.`orders`
Why: Every account has the USAGE line ("may connect"). Then one line per level: the database-level SELECT and the table-level INSERT stay separate lines because they are at different levels.
Q3
An app account gets ERROR 1143 (42000): SELECT command denied ... for column 'city' in table 'customers'. What does it mean?
A) The password is wrong
B) The account may not read the customers table at all
C) The account is locked
D) The account has column privileges on customers, and city is not one of them
Why: 1143 is the column-level cousin of 1142: the account may read some columns of customers (granted with SELECT (col, ...)) but not this one. SELECT * fails with 1142 for the same reason.
A is wrong: A wrong password is 1045, at login.
B is wrong: That is 1142 (table level); 1143 names a column.
C is wrong: A locked account is 3118, at login.
Q4
Tom and Lena have the role bookstore_sales as a default role. An administrator runs GRANT INSERT ON bookstore.customers TO 'bookstore_sales';. When can they insert customers?
A) Right away, both of them, and every future account given the role
B) Only after FLUSH PRIVILEGES
C) Only after each is granted INSERT on customers directly
D) Only accounts created after the GRANT
Why: A role is a bundle: changing it changes every account that has it, at once. That is the whole point of roles (and GRANT never needs FLUSH PRIVILEGES).
B is wrong: GRANT, REVOKE and CREATE USER update the privileges in memory immediately.
C is wrong: That would defeat the role.
D is wrong: Existing members get it too.
Q5
shop_reports has GRANT SELECT ON bookstore.*. What does REVOKE SELECT ON bookstore.books FROM 'shop_reports'@'localhost'; do?
A) Hides books and keeps every other table readable
B) Removes SELECT on the whole database
C) Fails with ERROR 1147: there is no table-level grant on books to take away
D) Works only after FLUSH PRIVILEGES
Why: Revoke at the level you granted. A database-level grant cannot have one table cut out of it; to hide one table, grant table by table (or through views).
A is wrong: MySQL has no "all tables except" grant.
B is wrong: The table-level REVOKE does not touch the database-level grant.
D is wrong: The statement fails; FLUSH PRIVILEGES changes nothing about that.
Q6
Write the statement that disables 'kit_mailer'@'localhost' without losing its grants.
ALTER USER 'kit_mailer'@'localhost' ACCOUNT LOCK;
Why: A locked account keeps its privileges; a login fails with 3118 until ALTER USER ... ACCOUNT UNLOCK. DROP USER would delete the account and its grants.

7.4 · User Management & Permissions: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT COUNT(*) AS kit_accounts
FROM mysql.user
WHERE user LIKE 'kit\_%';
+--------------+
| kit_accounts |
+--------------+
|            0 |
+--------------+
Why: A fresh 9.7 server has no kit_ accounts (the labs drop theirs at the end). If this is not 0 on a learner's laptop, a lab stopped halfway: run its DROP USER line.

8.1 · Importing & Exporting Data: self-check

Q7
Where do you type mysqldump?
A) At the mysql> prompt, ending with ;
B) In phpMyAdmin's SQL tab
C) At the terminal's shell prompt ($ or PS>), not inside the mysql client
D) Inside a SOURCE file
Why: mysqldump is a separate program that connects like any client. Typed at mysql> the server receives it as SQL and answers ERROR 1064 (debug_import.sql, bug A).
A is wrong: That is the 1064 the demo showed.
B is wrong: phpMyAdmin sends SQL to the server too; it has its own Export tab instead.
D is wrong: A SOURCE file is SQL, read by the client and sent to the server.
Q8
Which mysqldump option puts the stored procedures and functions into the backup?
A) --routines
B) --events
C) --single-transaction
D) --databases
Why: Routines are left out by default; --routines adds them (--events adds scheduled events; triggers are in by default).
B is wrong: --events adds scheduled events, not procedures.
C is wrong: --single-transaction makes the dump consistent without locking tables.
D is wrong: --databases adds CREATE DATABASE and USE lines.
Q9
What does this print?
SELECT STR_TO_DATE('07/04/1976', '%m/%d/%Y') AS published,
       NULLIF('', '') AS pages;
1976-07-04 and NULL
Why: STR_TO_DATE reads the text with the US pattern month/day/year and returns a real DATE. NULLIF returns NULL when its two arguments are equal: the trick that turns empty CSV fields into NULL.
Q10
You restore a dump made WITHOUT --databases using mysql -u root -p < bookstore_backup.sql and get ERROR 1046 (3D000) at line 22: No database selected. What is the fix?
A) Take the dump again with --set-gtid-purged=OFF
B) Run SET FOREIGN_KEY_CHECKS = 0 first
C) Use SOURCE instead of <
D) Name the target database: mysql -u root -p bookstore_test < bookstore_backup.sql (or dump with --databases)
Why: Without --databases the file has no CREATE DATABASE or USE line, so you choose the target when you restore (real output from the demo's shell run).
A is wrong: That fixes ERROR 3546 (GTIDs), a different failure.
B is wrong: Foreign keys are not the problem; no database is selected.
C is wrong: SOURCE without a current database fails the same way.
Q11
Why load a stranger's CSV into a staging table before touching books?
A) LOAD DATA cannot load into a table that has foreign keys
B) To look at, convert and check the rows (dates, empty values, missing authors) before any real table changes
C) Staging tables load faster than real tables
D) LOAD DATA LOCAL only works with staging tables
Why: A staging table with the file's columns lets you SELECT what arrived, find what is missing with a LEFT JOIN, and then copy only the good rows in one transaction.
A is wrong: LOAD DATA can load into any table you may INSERT into.
C is wrong: Speed is not the reason.
D is wrong: LOCAL works with any table.
Q12
You run SELECT title FROM books INTO OUTFILE '/var/lib/mysql-files/titles.txt'; a second time. What happens?
A) The file is overwritten with the new rows
B) The rows are added to the end of the file
C) ERROR 1086: the file already exists
D) MySQL writes titles(1).txt
Why: INTO OUTFILE never overwrites, which protects earlier exports and system files. Write to a new name (add the date) or delete the old file at the terminal.
A is wrong: Overwriting is exactly what MySQL refuses to do.
B is wrong: There is no append mode.
D is wrong: MySQL never renames for you.

8.1 · Importing & Exporting Data: predict, then run

P2
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT STR_TO_DATE('31.12.1999', '%d.%m.%Y') AS eu_date,
       STR_TO_DATE('31.12.1999', '%m/%d/%Y') AS us_pattern;
+------------+------------+
| eu_date    | us_pattern |
+------------+------------+
| 1999-12-31 | NULL       |
+------------+------------+
Why: The European pattern reads the text; the US pattern does not match it, so STR_TO_DATE returns NULL (with warning 1411). In an INSERT, strict mode turns that into an error (debug_import.sql, bug C).
P3
Predict the output, then run it.
SELECT NULLIF('', '') AS empty_to_null,
       NULLIF('412', '') AS pages_text,
       COALESCE(NULL, '') AS for_people;
+---------------+------------+------------+
| empty_to_null | pages_text | for_people |
+---------------+------------+------------+
| NULL          | 412        |            |
+---------------+------------+------------+
Why: NULLIF('', '') is NULL (the empty CSV field becomes a real NULL); NULLIF('412', '') keeps the text; COALESCE(NULL, '') turns NULL into an empty string, for files people open.
Self-check code, run for real

| GRANT SELECT ON `bookstore`.* TO `kit_quiz`@`localhost`        |
| GRANT INSERT ON `bookstore`.`orders` TO `kit_quiz`@`localhost` |
+----------------------------------------------------------------+

Q12, the same INTO OUTFILE twice (the second line is the real answer):

ERROR 1086 (HY000): File '/var/lib/mysql-files/titles.txt' already exists

TODO 6, logged in as kit_warehouse in a second session before the lock (real output):

UPDATE books SET in_stock = FALSE WHERE id = 1;
UPDATE books SET price = 0 WHERE id = 1;
Query OK, 1 row affected
Rows matched: 1  Changed: 1  Warnings: 0
ERROR 1143 (42000): UPDATE command denied to user 'kit_warehouse'@'localhost' for column 'price' in table 'books'

Reading it: MySQL merges the table-level SELECT and the column-level UPDATE on books into one SHOW GRANTS line, but information_schema keeps them apart (TABLE_PRIVILEGES vs COLUMN_PRIVILEGES). The REVOKE names the table it was granted on. A locked account keeps its grants (SHOW GRANTS still lists them) and its login fails with 3118. The UPDATE of in_stock also needs SELECT on books for its WHERE: that is why the warehouse has both.

Handout: labs/module-12/l7_4-user-management/debug_grants.sql (deliberately broken)

As shipped:

ERROR 1410 (42000): You are not allowed to create a user with GRANT
ERROR 1141 (42000): There is no such grant defined for user 'kit_mailer' on host '%'
ERROR 1396 (HY000): Operation CREATE USER failed for 'kit_mailer'@'localhost' (User account already exists)
ERROR 1147 (42000): There is no such grant defined for user 'kit_mailer' on host 'localhost' on table 'orders'
+-----------------------------------------------------------+
| Grants for kit_mailer@localhost                           |
+-----------------------------------------------------------+
| GRANT USAGE ON *.* TO `kit_mailer`@`localhost`            |
| GRANT SELECT ON `bookstore`.* TO `kit_mailer`@`localhost` |
+-----------------------------------------------------------+
  1. A, 1410: kit_mailr does not exist, and modern MySQL never creates an account in GRANT. Fix: the right name.
  2. B, 1141: 'kit_mailer'@'%' is a different account from @'localhost'. Fix: the exact pair.
  3. C, 1396: the account already exists. Fix: CREATE USER IF NOT EXISTS (or DROP USER IF EXISTS first, as the top of the file does).
  4. D, 1147: SELECT was granted on bookstore.*, not on orders. A database-level grant cannot lose one table; the real fix is to revoke the database-level SELECT and grant only what the tool needs (the customers columns).

Fixed (real run):

+---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| Grants for kit_mailer@localhost                                                             |
+---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| GRANT USAGE ON *.* TO `kit_mailer`@`localhost`                                              |
| GRANT SELECT (`email`, `first_name`) ON `bookstore`.`customers` TO `kit_mailer`@`localhost` |
+---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
